Localization and Cantor spectrum for quasiperiodic discrete Schrödinger operators with asymmetric, smooth, cosine-like sampling functions
Yakir Forman, Tom VandenBoom

Abstract
We prove Cantor spectrum and almost-sure Anderson localization for quasi-periodic discrete Schrödinger operators H = ε ​ Δ + V H=\varepsilon\Delta+V with potential V V sampled with Diophantine frequency α \alpha from an asymmetric, smooth, cosine-like function v ∈ C 2 ​ ( 𝕋 , [ − 1 , 1 ] ) v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1]) for sufficiently small interaction ε ≤ ε 0 ​ ( v , α ) \varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}(v,\alpha) . We prove this result via an inductive analysis on scales, whereby we show that locally the Rellich functions of Dirichlet restrictions of H H inherit the cosine-like structure of v v and are uniformly well-separated.



1
Introduction and main results

Fix a bounded real sequence
V=\{V_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{Z}}
and
\varepsilon>0
. We consider the discrete Schrödinger operator
H=H(V,\varepsilon)
whose pointwise action on sequences in
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
is given by

\displaystyle(H\psi)(n)

\displaystyle:=\left((\varepsilon\Delta+V)\psi\right)(n)

(1.1)

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon(\psi(n+1)+\psi(n-1))+V_{n}\psi(n).

Restricted to
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
,
H
is a bounded self-adjoint linear operator.
H
is called Anderson localized if it exhibits an
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
basis of exponentially decaying eigenvectors. The Anderson model, where
V
is sampled random i.i.d. from a nontrivial probability distribution, is almost surely Anderson localized; in this sense, localization is a standard measure of “randomness” of the potential. That said, certain non-random potentials, e.g.
V_{n}=\cos(\theta+n\alpha)
, are likewise almost-surely Anderson localized for small
\varepsilon
and almost every
\alpha
. Proofs of this fact have historically relied on either the analyticity of cosine and subsequent uniform positivity of the Lyapunov exponent or its inherent symmetry. We offer a new perturbative proof of almost-sure localization and Cantor spectrum for potentials sampled from any
C^{2}
-smooth Morse function with two monotonicity intervals along a Diophantine rotation on the circle.

We consider “cosine-like” sampling functions from
\mathbb{T}=\mathbb{R}/\mathbb{Z}
into
[-1,1]
having two non-degenerate critical points; that is, we consider functions
f\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1])
having two monotonicity intervals and satisfying a Morse condition

\displaystyle d_{0}\leq|\partial_{\theta}f|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f|\leq D_{0}.

We say
\alpha\in[0,1]\setminus\mathbb{Q}
is
(C,\tau)
-Diophantine when

\displaystyle\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C}{|n|^{\tau}},\quad n\in\mathbb{Z}.

(1.2)

In this case we write
\alpha\in DC_{C,\tau}
. It is well-known that Lebesgue almost every
\alpha
is Diophantine for some
(C,\tau)
. We consider potentials
V=V(\theta_{*})
generated by sampling a cosine-like
v
along an irrational rotation by
\alpha
starting at
\theta_{*}
, i.e.

\displaystyle V_{n}(\theta_{*})=v(\theta_{*}+n\alpha),

and denote by
H(\theta_{*})=H(V(\theta_{*}),\varepsilon)
.

Theorem

(Main Theorem)
.

Let
v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1])
be a function with two monotonicity intervals satisfying a Morse condition

d_{0}\leq|\partial_{\theta}v|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}v|\leq D_{0}

and let
\alpha\in DC_{C,\tau}
. Then there exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(v,\alpha)
such that, for
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}
,

1.

The spectrum
\Sigma=\Sigma(v,\alpha)
of
H(\theta_{*})
is a Cantor set, and

2.

For Haar almost every
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
,
H(\theta_{*})
is Anderson localized, i.e. has pure-point spectrum with exponentially decaying eigenfunctions.

Our approach to the proof of the Main Theorem is an inductive analysis of scales. Multiscale analysis techniques are a well-established approach to localization in arbitrary spatial dimensions, cf.
[
8
,
10
,
11
,
12
,
13
,
14
,
15
]
, and are quite general in their scope; for a recent survey of multiscale analysis techniques, see
[
21
]
. In one spatial dimension, as in our model, one has access to additional tools (like the Lyapunov exponent and transfer matrices) which can simplify the analysis. Indeed, uniform positivity of the Lyapunov exponent often lends itself to almost-sure
nonperturbative
localization results
[
1
,
3
]
. Such results were initially established by Jitomirskaya for the Almost Mathieu operator
[
18
]
, and this strategy has been well-applied in the analytic regime due to the Hermann “subharmonicity trick,” see e.g.
[
2
,
3
,
4
,
5
,
25
]
, as well as in the random i.i.d. regime due to Fürstenberg’s Theorem, see e.g.
[
6
,
7
,
9
,
17
,
19
]
.

For smooth sampling functions, proving uniform positivity of the Lyapunov exponent becomes a substantial challenge. In this case, only perturbative strategies have thus far been successful. Fröhlich-Spencer-Wittwer established a perturbative approach to proving almost-sure Anderson localization for symmetric cosine-like smooth potentials
v
with sufficiently small interaction for almost every irrational
\alpha

[
14
]
, with additional work in this direction pioneered by Sinai
[
24
]
. Positive Lyapunov exponents and Cantor spectrum were verified via perturbative methods in the general asymmetric cosine-like case by Wang-Zhang
[
26
,
27
]
.

1.1
What this paper accomplishes

Our result will naturally be compared to the aforementioned works of Fröhlich-Spencer-Wittwer
[
14
]
, Sinai
[
24
]
, and Wang-Zhang
[
26
,
27
]
. Our main accomplishments relative to these prior works are, respectively:

1.

Removing the crucial symmetry assumption on the sampling function
v
from
[
14
]
in part by an inductive analysis of differences of inverse functions; cf. Section 4.

2.

Establishing the existence of open spectral gaps via a novel Cauchy interlacing argument (cf. Theorem
3.2
) and a multiscale induction scheme which preserves the size of these gaps (cf. Proposition
5.3
).

3.

Eliminating double-resonances, i.e., those phases
\theta
for which the frequency
\alpha
is problematically recurrent relative to the sampling function
v
infinitely often (cf. Lemma
6.7
).

Each of these steps requires new ideas not present in the above works; we describe the difficulties which must be overcome, and our approaches to overcoming them, below.

1.1.1
Removing symmetry

In
[
14
]
, the authors prove an analogue to the Main Theorem under the additional assumption of even symmetry of the sampling function
v
defining the potential:
v(\theta)=v(-\theta)
. This assumption is crucial to their analysis because the symmetry is inherited by the Rellich functions (i.e., parametrized eigenvalues)
\mathbf{E}^{\Lambda}
of any Dirichlet restriction
H^{\Lambda}
of
H
to an interval
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
; specifically, if
\Lambda=[c-L,c+L]
, then

\displaystyle\mathbf{E}^{\Lambda}(\theta)=\mathbf{E}^{\Lambda}(-\theta-2c\alpha),\quad\theta\in\mathbb{T}.

In particular, it follows that there is antisymmetry in the derivative:

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}^{\Lambda}(\theta)=-(\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}^{\Lambda})(-\theta-2c\alpha).

These symmetries come with two crucial benefits. First, Rellich curves of
H^{\Lambda}
have predetermined critical points when

\displaystyle\|2\theta_{c}+2c\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}=0.

These critical points are common to any Rellich curves of
H^{\Lambda}
. This fact, alongside opposite-signed second derivatives (cf. (
1.9
)), allows one to conclude a uniform local eigenvalue separation from the classical pointwise eigenvalue separation for eigenvalues with eigenvectors localized on a common support (cf. Lemma
1.5
). The uniform local separation of Rellich curves guarantees that different Rellich curves cannot resonate with one another, which in turn allows one to, e.g., apply calculus and the Diophantine condition to single Rellich curves at every step of the induction.

The second important consequence of the symmetry of the Rellich functions is that the difference of local inverse functions – which plays a crucial role in identifying double resonances (cf. Figure
1
) – is effectively independent of the scale and the energy, cf.
[
14
, Lemma 5.7]
. This means that, in the even setting, one can define the bad sets of double resonant phases uniformly in the energy. In our construction, the difference of local inverses of Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{s,+}^{-1}-\mathbf{E}_{s,-}^{-1}
depends on the parent Rellich function, and thus so too do our bad sets of phases. By carefully controlling the number of double resonances and child Rellich functions coming from each parent (cf. equation (
6.1
) below), we can discretize this energy dependence and again construct a uniform bad set of phases at each scale, whose limit superior we eventually eliminate to prove Anderson localization.

1.1.2
Opening gaps

Sinai’s paper
[
24
]
is likewise foundational in establishing an approach to proving perturbative localization results in the quasiperiodic setting; indeed, general aspects of our argument, like the idea illustrated in Figure
3
below, echo some of the ideas developed therein. However, Sinai’s paper suffers from fundamental flaws as written, each of which our approach overcomes.

Sinai’s approach to studying quasiperiodic operators is
a priori
a natural one: for fixed frequency
\alpha
having best rational approximants
\alpha_{s}=p_{s}/q_{s}
with
q_{s}
having controlled growth rate
q_{s+1}\lesssim(s+1)^{2}q_{s}
, approximate the whole-line operator
H
via the
q_{s}
-periodic operators
H^{(s)}_{\varepsilon}(\theta)
. In Sinai’s construction, the transition between steps
s
and
s+1
involves approximating
H_{\varepsilon}^{(s+1)}(\theta)
with shifts
H^{(s)}_{\varepsilon}(\theta+n\alpha_{s+1})
. By the definition of the periodic approximants
\alpha_{s}
, one has

\displaystyle|\alpha_{s}-\alpha_{s+1}|=\frac{1}{q_{s}q_{s+1}}\gtrsim\frac{1}{(s+1)^{2}q_{s}^{2}}

and so the general error incurred in an inductive step
s\mapsto s+1
is, at best,
polynomial in
q_{s}^{-1}
. In contrast, in this inductive step there are double-resonant eigenfunctions whose centers of localization are separated at distances
q_{s}
. The separation between the resultant Rellich functions is
exponentially small in
q_{s}
, comparable to
\varepsilon^{q_{s}}
. Thus, after making an inductive step using periodic approximations, the uniform local separation between Rellich curves – even coming from previous scales! – is completely destroyed, taking along with it the ability to consider only self-resonances of a given Rellich curve.

Critiques aside, Sinai’s intuition regarding the importance of the gaps between children of resonant Rellich curves is informative. Describing these gaps in our regime is perhaps the most novel contribution in this work; we do so by proving the Rellich functions at scale
s+1
are simultaneously interlaced by a pair of auxiliary curves which are
C^{1}
close to the parent curves from scale
s
, which must be monotone with opposite-signed derivatives; cf. Lemma
3.8
.

1.1.3
Eliminating double resonances

Compared to the works of Fröhlich-Spencer-Wittwer and Sinai, relatively recent progress on understanding localization for smooth quasiperiodic discrete Schrödinger operators was made by Wang and Zhang
[
26
]
. Therein, the authors show perturbatively that the Lyapunov exponent of the associated transfer matrices is arbitrarily close to
|\log\varepsilon|
, and they likewise prove a Large Deviation Theorem. Their proof again proceeds by studying an auxiliary transfer matrix which is in some sense an asymptotic approximation to the Schrödinger transfer matrix as
\varepsilon
decreases to zero. In a follow-up paper
[
27
]
, the authors use the characterization of spectral energies as corresponding to non-uniform hyperbolicity of the associated transfer matrix to prove Cantor spectrum in the model.

In the one-dimensional setting, uniformly positive Lyapunov exponents and a Large Deviation Theorem are key ingredients to proving Anderson localization; however, alone they are insufficient to identify a full-measure set of phases for which localization holds. This final ingredient for localization, often called “elimination of double resonances,” requires a careful understanding of the sets of double resonant phases at each inductive step. These bad sets of phases depend crucially on the energies for which they are resonant.

In our construction, given a Morse, two-monotonicity interval Rellich curve
\mathbf{E}_{s}
at scale
s
, we can identify the bad sets of phases for
\mathbf{E}_{s}
and control their sizes using the Morse condition. One can control all of the bad sets simultaneously, then, by controlling the number of Rellich children
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
one constructs at each step. Here again the Diophantine condition and the Morse, two-monotonicity interval structure of each
\mathbf{E}_{s}
are crucial in order to separate the double-resonant energy regions, in turn allowing us to define at most
1/\rho^{3}_{s-1}
children of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
, each having bad sets of phases of size at most
\rho_{s}^{1/5}
(cf. the proof of Lemma
6.7
). Choosing our scales appropriately, we will have summability of the cumulative size of all bad sets at all scales, and the Borel-Cantelli lemma will apply. We sketch the first step of our inductive procedure below.

1.2
Idea of the proof

For an interval
\Lambda=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}
, we denote by
H^{\Lambda}
the restriction of
H
to
\Lambda
with Dirichlet boundary conditions,
\psi(a-1)=\psi(b+1)=0
. Letting
|\Lambda|:=b-a+1
, one can identify
H^{\Lambda}
with the
|\Lambda|\times|\Lambda|
matrix

\displaystyle H^{\Lambda}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}V_{a}&\varepsilon&&&&\\
\varepsilon&V_{a+1}&\varepsilon&&&\\
&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&&\\
&&\varepsilon&V_{b-1}&\varepsilon\\
&&&\varepsilon&V_{b}\end{bmatrix}.

Notice that the base dynamics of our model imply

\displaystyle H^{\Lambda}(\theta+n\alpha)=H^{\Lambda+n}(\theta)

for any
n\in\mathbb{Z}
,
\theta\in\mathbb{T}
; furthermore, by the regularity of our sampling function
v
, any one-parameter family of Dirichlet restrictions
H^{\Lambda}(\theta)
exhibits
|\Lambda|
Rellich functions (i.e., parametrized eigenvalues
\mathbf{E}(\theta)
) which are necessarily simple and
C^{2}
.

Generalized eigenvalues of the whole-line operator
H
(i.e., energies
E
with solutions
\psi
to
H\psi=E\psi
growing at most polynomially) are limit points of the eigenvalues of the finite-volume operators
H^{\Lambda}
as
\Lambda
grows to
\mathbb{Z}
; furthermore, they are spectrally dense in the spectrum of
H

[
22
,
23
]
. By the Poisson formula

\displaystyle\psi(n)=\varepsilon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(n,a)\psi(a-1)+\varepsilon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(n,b)\psi(b+1),\quad n\in\Lambda,\,E\notin\spec(H^{\Lambda}(\theta))

exponential off-diagonal decay of Green’s functions
R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}=(H^{\Lambda}(\theta)-E)^{-1}
can be favorably leveraged against the at-most polynomial growth of generalized eigenfunctions
\psi
on
\Lambda
. If we can inductively construct intervals of increasing length on which we have Green’s function decay at (or near) generalized eigenvalues, we can thus prove exponential decay of generalized eigenfunctions, hence Anderson localization.

The initial step in our induction involves constructing intervals
\Lambda_{1}=\Lambda_{1}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
from the degenerate interval
\Lambda_{0}=\{0\}
in a way which is stable in
\theta_{*}
and
E_{*}
. At this zeroth scale, the eigenvalue of
H^{\Lambda_{0}}(\theta_{*})
is precisely the value of the sampling function
v(\theta_{*})
. The difficult aspect of the induction is identifying how the structural assumptions on
v
are reflected by the Rellich functions of
H^{\Lambda_{s}}
at future scales
s\geq 1
.

In
non-resonant
situations where
|V_{m}-E|\geq\rho\gg\varepsilon
is large for all
m\in\Lambda
, the Green’s function exhibits off-diagonal decay at a rate of
\varepsilon/\rho
on
\Lambda
by a classical Neumann series argument (cf. Lemma
5.1
). One thus focuses on those cases when
|V_{m}-E|
is small:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{0}(\theta_{*},E_{*})=\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:|V_{m}(\theta_{*})-E_{*}|\lesssim\rho\}.

If an element
m\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
is separated by some relatively large distance
L
from any other element of
\mathcal{S}_{0}
, we call it
simple resonant
(for
v
,
\rho
, and
L
). For simple resonant
m
, one can build an interval
\Lambda^{(1)}_{1}=[m-L/2,m+L/2]
so that any eigenvector of the associated Dirichlet restriction
H^{\Lambda_{1}^{(1)}}(\theta)
with eigenvalue near
E_{*}
will be localized near
m
, again by the Poisson formula (
1.11
). By classical perturbation theory (cf. Lemma
1.2
and Proposition
2.1
), it follows that
H^{\Lambda^{(1)}_{1}}
has a single Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{1}(\theta)
near
E_{*}
for
\theta
near
\theta_{*}
, and thus
\mathbf{E}_{1}
will be well-approximated in
C^{2}
by the sampling function
\mathbf{E}_{0}:=v
(cf. Proposition
2.2
).

The obstruction to localization, then, are
double resonances
, where (at least) two resonant sites
m_{j}
are within
L
of one another. These double resonances are unavoidable; an illustration of such a resonance can be seen in Figure
1
.

n\alpha
v
E_{n}(v)
J_{0,n}
I_{0,n,-}
I_{0,n,+}

Figure 1
:
Double resonance of the sampling function
v
: if
\theta_{0,n,-}\in I_{0,n,-}
is the value such that
v(\theta_{0,n,-})=v(\theta_{0,n,-}+n\alpha)=E_{n}(v)
, then
0
and
n
are in
\mathcal{S}_{0}(\theta_{0,n,-},E_{n})
.

In our setting, the Diophantine condition and the structure of
v
guarantee that at
most
two resonant sites
m_{1}
and
m_{2}
can be nearby for appropriate choices of
\rho
and
L
; furthermore, each of these nearby pairs
(m_{1},m_{2})
must be well-separated from one another. Indeed, let
v:\mathbb{T}\to[-1,1]
be our Morse sampling function with two monotonicity intervals
\mathbf{I}_{\pm}
and denote by
v_{\pm}:=v|_{\mathbf{I}_{\pm}}
the monotone restrictions of
v
. By the Morse condition, one sees (cf. Lemma
4.2
)

\displaystyle|v_{\pm}(\theta)-v_{\pm}(\theta^{\prime})|\gtrsim\|\theta-\theta^{\prime}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{T}},\quad\theta,\theta^{\prime}\in\mathbf{I}_{\pm}.

(1.3)

By the Morse condition and (
1.3
), double resonant sites on the orbit of
\theta_{*}
cannot occur near critical points of
v
; we thus find a lower bound
\nu=\nu(\rho,L)\gg\rho
on
|v^{\prime}|
for double resonant phases
\theta\in I_{0,n,\pm}:=v_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{0,n})
. What’s more, given the two-monotonicity structure of
v
, there can be at most
4L
such double-resonant intervals
I_{0,n,\pm}
,
|n|\leq L
(cf. Figure
1
). Thus, the region of double-resonant phases is of size at most
4L\rho/\nu
. It also follows from the Diophantine condition (
1.2
) and the two-monotonicity interval structure of
v
that the sequence
V_{m}=v(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)
can only recur to a neighborhood of
E
for at most two nearby values of
m
:

\displaystyle\min_{m_{1},m_{2},m_{3}\in\mathcal{S}_{0}}\{|m_{1}-m_{2}|,|m_{2}-m_{3}|\}\gtrsim\rho^{-1/2\tau}

Thus, if we choose
L
of order, e.g.,

\displaystyle L^{6}\sim\rho^{-1/2\tau},

then resonant sites
m\in\mathcal{S}_{0}
which are not simple resonant appear in distinct pairs
(m_{1},m_{2})
with
|m_{1}-m_{2}|\leq L
such that each pair is separated from other pairs by at least
L^{6}
.

Given the separation of these pairs, one can build an interval
\Lambda^{(2)}_{1}=[m_{1}-L^{2}/2,m_{1}+L^{2}/2]
such that, by classical perturbation theory (cf. Lemma
1.2
and Proposition
3.1
),
H^{\Lambda^{(2)}_{1}}
has two Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{1,\vee}>\mathbf{E}_{1,\wedge}
near
E_{*}
for
\theta
near
\theta_{*}
. These new Rellich functions, however, must necessarily deviate significantly from the parent function
v
near their the crossing point
E_{n}(v)
. One of the primary technical thrusts of this paper is demonstrating that these new functions locally 1) retain the crucial Morse and two-monotonicity structural properties of the function
v
, and 2) separate with a stable, quantifiable gap between them:

Theorem

(cf. Theorem
3.2
)
.

In our setting, double resonances of a Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{s}
of
H^{\Lambda_{s}}
resolve as a pair of uniformly locally separated Morse Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,\vee}>\mathbf{E}_{s+1,\wedge}
of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}^{(2)}}
with at most one critical point, cf. Figure
2
.

The gap demonstrated in the above Theorem serves two purposes: First, it ensures that any Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{1}
can only resonate with itself at future scales, which ultimately enables our induction. Second, the size of the gap is sufficiently large to remain open through each step of the inductive procedure; the ubiquity of these gaps yields Cantor spectrum.

\sim\varepsilon^{|n|}
\mathbf{E}_{0}(\theta+n\alpha)
\mathbf{E}_{1,n,\lor}(\theta)
\mathbf{E}_{0}(\theta)
\mathbf{E}_{1,n,\land}(\theta)

:
The resolution of a double resonance of
\mathbf{E}_{0}=v
into a pair of uniformly locally well-separated Rellich curves of a Dirichlet restriction
H^{\Lambda_{1}^{(2)}}
of
H
. The interval
\Lambda_{1}^{(2)}
depends on the crossing value
E_{n}(v)
.

\mathbf{J}_{1,n,\lor}
\mathbf{I}_{1,n,\lor}
\mathbf{E}_{1,n,\lor}

Figure 3
:
A cartoon output of the first inductive step: a collection
\mathcal{E}_{1}
of local Rellich functions of various Dirichlet restrictions of
H
, whose domains (and their relevant translates, e.g.
\mathbf{I}_{1,n,\vee}+n\alpha
) cover the circle
\mathbb{T}
. The curves in black come from double resonances, and the curves in gray are simple resonant. Note that different curves need not agree on the overlap of their domains.

From the initial scale function
\mathbf{E}_{0}=v
, we thus construct a collection
\mathcal{E}_{1}
of well-separated Rellich functions of certain Dirichlet restrictions of
H
whose domains cover the circle
\mathbb{T}
with the same structural properties as
\mathbf{E}_{0}
, cf. Figure
3
.
The inductive argument proceeds on each such constructed function: by properly defining our parameters at scale
s
relative to our initial choice of
\varepsilon=:\delta_{0}
, we can ensure that the size
\delta_{s}
of the resonant eigenvectors near the edges of
\Lambda_{s}
is much smaller than the eigenvalue separation
\rho_{s}
, allowing the procedure above to iterate; we choose these parameters in such a way so as to ensure the bad sets of phases have summable measures (cf. Lemma
6.7
). The Borel-Cantelli lemma guarantees that the collection
B
of
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
which are double-resonant infinitely often has zero measure; our full-measure set of localized phases is the complement
\Theta=\mathbb{T}\setminus B
.

The ultimate output of the induction is a tree of cosine-like Rellich curves, cf. Figure
4
. Each fixed energy
E_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
admits a path of “
E_{*}
-relevant” Rellich functions through this tree; this path either terminates at finite depth if
E_{*}
is non-spectral or proceeds without end. Given
\theta_{*}\in\Theta
, we consider the infinite path determined by some generalized eigenvalue
E_{*}=E(\theta_{*})
of
H(\theta_{*})
; by our construction of
\Theta
, this path eventually consists
only of simple-resonant children
. The resulting stability of the approximate eigenfunctions
\psi_{s}
will yield exponential localization of the corresponding generalized eigenfunction, showing that the generalized eigenvalue is an honest eigenvalue with decaying eigenfunction. The spectral density of generalized eigenvalues guaranteed by Schnol’s lemma completes the localization argument.
Moreover, our construction ensures that each node of the tree
has some double-resonant descendant
. Since each double resonance opens a spectral gap which will remain open for all future scales, this observation guarantees that spectral gaps open arbitrarily close to any fixed energy
E_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
; thus the spectrum is a Cantor set.

{forest}

Figure 4
:
After completing the induction, we have a tree
\mathcal{E}
of Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{s}:\mathbf{I}_{s}\to\mathbf{J}_{s}
. Any energy
E_{*}\in\mathbb{R}
admits a path through this tree. The superscript
(j)
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, of each child indicates the resonance type relative to the parent.

1.2.1
A brief roadmap of the paper

The article proceeds as follows: in the remainder of this section, we will establish some basic notation and foundational lemmas for use throughout the paper; the proofs of these lemmas can all be found in Appendix B. Sections 2, 3, and 4 provide the crucial infrastructure for our induction. In Sections 2 and 3, we develop the spectral tools to analyze descendants of a given Rellich curve in the simple- and double-resonant settings, respectively, at every scale. The by-now classical but necessary details for proving Green’s function decay on nonresonant intervals are included in Appendix A. Section 4 consists of technical machinery allowing us to classify energy regions
J_{s,i}
in the codomain of a cosine-like function
\mathbf{E}_{s}
as simple- or double-resonant. Section 5 applies the content of Sections 2, 3, and 4 in a multiscale induction scheme: given a Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{s}
, we use the restrictions of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
to the resonance regions
J_{s,i}
to construct Rellich children
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
according to the recipes in Sections 2 and 3. Section 6 characterizes the spectrum of
H
in terms of the complete collection of Rellich functions constructed in Section 5, proving Cantor spectrum via the permanent stability of the gaps coming from double resonances; finally, we eliminate double resonances and prove Anderson localization as outlined above.

1.3
Notation and preliminaries

In this subsection, we establish some notation and collect the foundational lemmas which will serve as the starting point for our discussion; the proofs of each of these lemmas can be found in Appendix B.

Throughout the paper we use
I
to denote intervals of phases
\theta
,
J
to denote intervals of energies
E
,
\Lambda
to denote intervals of integers, and we denote by
B_{r}(x)
the open ball of radius
r
centered at
x
. We reserve a handful of parameters for our multiscale inductive procedure; these parameters are:

L
: Lengths of spacial intervals
\Lambda
.

\rho
: Resonance scale for phase and energy intervals
I
and
J
.

\delta
: The size of a localized eigenfunction at the edge of an interval
\Lambda
.

\nu
: Local lower bound on the magnitude of a first derivative of a Rellich function.

(\ell,\gamma)
: Green’s function decay parameters, see below.

Due to the nature of the induction, we will occasionally consider up to three scales simultaneously, a “current” scale, a “next” scale, and a “previous” scale; we use hats
\widehat{\cdot}
or checks
\widecheck{\cdot}
above the corresponding parameters when we intend to suggest application to the next or previous scales, respectively. With this convention, one should keep in mind the following general scale principles, which we clarify more precisely as necessary:

\displaystyle\delta\ll\rho\ll\widecheck{\rho}\ll\nu,

\displaystyle\gamma\sim|\log\varepsilon|,

\displaystyle L\ll\widehat{\ell}\ll\widehat{L}.

We now fix the notation for our objects of study. Let
V\in\ell^{\infty}(\mathbb{Z},\mathbb{R})
be a real-valued potential. Restricted to
\ell^{2}(\mathbb{Z})
, the linear operator
H(V,\varepsilon)
is self-adjoint and bounded (with
\|H\|\leq\|V\|_{\infty}+2\varepsilon
). For an interval
\Lambda=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}
we denote by
H^{\Lambda}
the restriction of
H
to
\Lambda
with Dirichlet boundary conditions,
\psi(a-1)=\psi(b+1)=0
. Letting
|\Lambda|:=b-a+1
, one identifies
H^{\Lambda}
with a
|\Lambda|\times|\Lambda|
matrix by making an appropriate change of basis. With this identification, we will frequently treat vectors
\widecheck{\psi}
defined only on a subset
\widecheck{\Lambda}\subset\Lambda
as though they are vectors in
\mathbb{C}^{|\Lambda|}
; in this situation, we abuse notation and conflate
\widecheck{\psi}
with its extension to
\Lambda
given by
\widecheck{\psi}(m)=0
,
m\in\Lambda\setminus\widecheck{\Lambda}
.

Consider a partition of
\Lambda
given by
\mathcal{P}=\cup_{j=0}^{p}\Lambda_{j}
,
\Lambda_{j}=[a_{j},b_{j}]
, where
b_{j}+1=a_{j+1}
for all
0\leq j\leq p-1
and
a_{0}=a,b_{p}=b
. We define the partitioned operators
H^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}
and
\Gamma^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}
by

\displaystyle H^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle:=\bigoplus_{j}H^{\Lambda_{j}},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}

\displaystyle:=H^{\Lambda}-H^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}.

We fix once and for all the trivial partition
\mathcal{P}_{0}
with
a_{j}=b_{j}=j
, and note that in this special case

\displaystyle H^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}

\displaystyle=V^{\Lambda},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\Delta^{\Lambda}.

We denote by
\spec(A)
the spectrum of an operator
A
. For
E\notin\spec(H^{\Lambda})
, we denote by
R^{\Lambda}(E):=(H^{\Lambda}-E)^{-1}
the resolvent operator and by
R^{\Lambda}_{E}(m,n):=\langle\delta_{m},R^{\Lambda}(E)\delta_{n}\rangle
the associated Green’s function. Given a partition
\mathcal{P}
of
\Lambda
, we define
R^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}
analogously, with
H^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}
replacing
H^{\Lambda}
. We will make frequent use of the resolvent identity

\displaystyle R^{\Lambda}(E)-R^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}(E)

\displaystyle=-R^{\Lambda}(E)\,\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda}\,R^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}(E).

Let
E\notin\spec H^{\Lambda}
; given
\ell\in\mathbb{N},\gamma>0
, we say that an interval
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
satisfies the
Green’s function decay property for
(\ell,\gamma)
if, for all

m,n\in\Lambda
with
|m-n|\geq\ell
,

\log|R^{\Lambda}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma|m-n|.

1.3.1
Perturbation theory for differentiable one-parameter self-adjoint operator families

We now recall some important lemmas for use throughout the paper; the proofs of these lemmas are appended.

We begin with a straightforward consequence of rank-nullity:

Lemma 1.1
.

Let
\chi
and
P
each be orthogonal projections on a finite-dimensional vector space. If

\displaystyle\|(I-\chi)P\|<1

then
\rank(\chi)\geq\rank(P)
.

We apply this lemma in the context of spectral projections for real symmetric matrices. For a symmetric matrix
A
having normalized eigenpairs
(\lambda_{j},\psi_{j})
,
\|\psi_{j}\|=1
, and a Borel set
J\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote by
\chi_{J}(A)
the spectral projection

\displaystyle\chi_{J}(A):=\sum_{\lambda_{j}\in J}\psi_{j}\psi_{j}^{\top}

onto the direct sum of those eigenspaces of
A
with
\lambda_{j}\in J
. We have the following fundamental perturbative result relating eigenvectors of a symmetric matrix
A
having quantitatively separated spectrum to approximations thereof:

Lemma 1.2
.

Let
A
be a real symmetric matrix, and let
E_{*}\in{\mathbb{R}}
and
\delta>0
. Suppose there exists a nonzero orthogonal projection
P
such that

\displaystyle\|(A-E_{*})P\|\leq\delta.

Then
\chi_{\overline{B}_{\delta}(E_{*})}(A)\neq 0
, and for any
\rho>\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A))P\right\|\leq\delta/\rho.

(1.4)

In particular, for any unit vector
\phi
in the image of
P
, the spectral renormalization

\displaystyle\psi:=\frac{\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)\phi}{\|\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)\phi\|}

is well-approximated by
\phi
:

\displaystyle\left\|\phi-\psi\right\|\leq\sqrt{2}\delta/\rho.

(1.5)

For a Borel set
J\subset\mathbb{R}
, we denote the
partial resolvent

\displaystyle R_{\perp}(E;A,J):=\sum_{\lambda_{j}\notin J}\frac{\chi_{\{\lambda_{j}\}}(A)}{\lambda_{j}-E}

and sometimes abbreviate the special case

\displaystyle R_{\perp}(\lambda_{j};A):=\sum_{\lambda_{k}\neq\lambda_{j}}\frac{\chi_{\{\lambda_{k}\}}(A)}{\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{j}}.

Note that, by definition,

\displaystyle R_{\perp}(E;A,J)(A-E)=I-\chi_{J}(A)

(1.6)

is the spectral projection off of the eigenspaces of
A
associated to
\lambda_{j}\in J
. Furthermore, one has that

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(\lambda_{j};A)\|

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\dist(\lambda_{j},\spec A\setminus\{\lambda_{j}\})}.

The partial resolvents
R_{\perp}
are of great importance in computing derivatives of eigenvectors for smooth one-parameter families of symmetric matrices:

Lemma 1.3

(Feynman formulae)
.

Let
A(\theta)
denote a one-parameter family of symmetric real matrices, and suppose that
A
is
C^{2}
at
\theta_{*}
, and that
(E_{*},\psi_{*})
is a simple eigenpair of
A(\theta_{*})
. Then there is a family
(\mathbf{E},\psi)(\theta)
of locally simple eigenpairs in a neighborhood of
\theta_{*}
, normalized so that
\|\psi(\theta)\|=1
, which is twice differentiable at
\theta_{*}
. Furthermore, one has

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}(\theta_{*})

\displaystyle=\langle\psi,A^{\prime}(\theta_{*})\psi\rangle,

(1.7)

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\psi(\theta_{*})

\displaystyle=-R_{\perp}(A,E_{*})A^{\prime}(\theta_{*})\psi_{*}

(1.8)

and

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}^{2}\mathbf{E}(\theta_{*})

\displaystyle=\langle\psi,A^{\prime\prime}(\theta_{*})\psi\rangle+2\langle\partial_{\theta}\psi,A^{\prime}(\theta_{*})\psi\rangle.

(1.9)

Finally, one has that

\displaystyle\langle\partial_{\theta}\psi,\psi\rangle=0.

(1.10)

1.3.2
Spectral analysis of discrete Schrödinger operators

We recall some classical lemmas regarding eigenfunctions of discrete Schrödinger operators. The first, which is sometimes referred to as the “Poisson formula,” relates the value of an eigenfunction at a particular site
n
to Green’s functions of Dirichlet restrictions of
H
:

Lemma 1.4

(Poisson Formula)
.

Let
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{Z}}
satisfy the formal Schrödinger difference equation
H\psi=E\psi
, and let
\Lambda=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}
be an interval. Then

\displaystyle\psi(n)=\varepsilon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(n,a)\psi(a-1)+\varepsilon R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(n,b)\psi(b+1),\quad n\in\Lambda

(1.11)

provided
E\notin\spec(H^{\Lambda})
.

The same relation holds if we replace
H
with
H^{\hat{\Lambda}}
for some interval
\hat{\Lambda}=[\hat{a},\hat{b}]\supsetneq\Lambda
and let
\psi\in\mathbb{C}^{|\hat{\Lambda}|}
satisfy
H^{\hat{\Lambda}}\psi=E\psi
. In this case, we use the convention that
\psi(\hat{a}-1)=\psi(\hat{b}+1)=0
.

The final result that we recall is a classical eigenvalue separation lemma (cf. e.g.
[
14
,
20
]
), which quantifiably separates the eigenvalues of simultaneously localized eigenvectors of a discrete Schrödinger operator:

Lemma 1.5
.

Let
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
,
\widehat{\psi}_{2}
be distinct eigenfunctions for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with corresponding eigenvalues
\widehat{E}_{1}
,
\widehat{E}_{2}
. Suppose there is an interval
{\Lambda}\subset\widehat{\Lambda}
supporting half of the mass of both
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
and
\widehat{\psi}_{2}
, i.e., such that
\|\widehat{\psi}_{j}\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2}\|\widehat{\psi}_{j}\|_{\widehat{\Lambda}}^{2}
,
j=1,2
. Then

\displaystyle|\widehat{E}_{1}-\widehat{E}_{2}|\geq\frac{\varepsilon}{6|{\Lambda}|^{2}}\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{-|{\Lambda}|}>\left(\frac{\varepsilon}{3}\right)^{3|{\Lambda}|}

where
m_{{\Lambda}}:=\max_{k\in{\Lambda},j=1,2}|v(k)-\widehat{E}_{j}|^{2}
.

In particular, if
2\rho<(\varepsilon/3)^{3|\Lambda|}
,
|\widehat{E}_{1}-E_{*}|<\rho/2
, and
E_{*}
is well-separated from any other eigenvalues of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
(i.e.,
\|R_{\perp}(E_{*};H^{\widehat{\Lambda}},\{\widehat{E}_{1},\widehat{E}_{2}\})\|\leq\frac{2}{3}\rho^{-1}
), we have the partial resolvent bound

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}},\{\widehat{E}_{1}\})\|\leq\rho^{-1},\quad E\in B_{\rho/2}(E_{*}).

(1.12)

1.4
Acknowledgments

The authors would like to express their deep and sincere gratitude to Michael Goldstein and Wilhelm Schlag for their guidance and numerous helpful conversations throughout the duration of this project.
The work of Y.F. was supported in part by a fellowship award through the National Defense Science and Engineering Graduate (NDSEG) Fellowship Program, sponsored by the Air Force Research Laboratory (AFRL), the Office of Naval Research (ONR) and the Army Research Office (ARO).

2
Simple resonance

In this section, we study the situation of simple resonance, where an interval
\widehat{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}
contains precisely one resonant “core” subinterval
{\Lambda}\subset\widehat{\Lambda}
such that
H^{\Lambda}(\theta_{*})
has a unique eigenvalue near a fixed
E_{*}
. Under smallness and stability assumptions on the boundary values of the corresponding eigenvector, alongside off-diagonal Green’s function decay on the maximal connected components of
\widehat{\Lambda}\setminus{\Lambda}
(the “shoulders” of
\widehat{\Lambda}
), we prove that
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
has a unique resonant eigenpair child which is well-approximated by the parent eigenpair of
H^{{\Lambda}}
.

Fix
v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1])
with
\|\partial_{\theta}v\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\theta}^{2}v\|_{\infty}\leq D_{0}
,
(\theta_{*},E_{*})\in\mathbb{T}\times[-2,2]
, and
0<\varepsilon<1/7
. Let
\widehat{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}
be an interval with subinterval “core”
{\Lambda}
of length
{L}=|{\Lambda}|
. We denote the left and right shoulders of
\widehat{\Lambda}
by
{\Lambda}_{l/r}
, respectively, and denote the corresponding partition of
\widehat{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{l}\cup{\Lambda}\cup{\Lambda}_{r}
by
\mathcal{P}
. Fix constants
\rho,\delta,\gamma,\ell>0
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle\delta<\rho^{3}/2

\displaystyle<\rho/16,

(2.1)

\displaystyle\log 7<\gamma

\displaystyle\leq|\log\varepsilon|

(2.2)

Assumption 1
.

Suppose the following hold for
|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho/8D_{0}
:

1.

(Simple resonance) The operator
H^{{\Lambda}}(\theta)
has a Rellich pair
({\mathbf{E}},{\psi})(\theta)
such that

{\mathbf{E}}(\theta_{*})=E_{*}
.

2.

(Eigenvector decay) The unit eigenvector
{\psi}(\theta)
of
H^{{\Lambda}}(\theta)
has
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values bounded by
\delta/\varepsilon
; i.e. one has

\displaystyle\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}{\psi}\|\leq 2\delta.

(2.3)

Furthermore, for any Rellich pair
(\widehat{E},\widehat{\psi})(\theta)
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta)
with
|\widehat{E}-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
, the unit eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
also has
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values no larger than
\delta/\varepsilon
; i.e. one has

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\widehat{\psi}\|\leq 4\delta.

(2.4)

3.

(Green’s function decay on the shoulders of
\Lambda
)
For
m,n\in{\Lambda}_{l/r}
with
|m-n|\geq\ell
, one has

\displaystyle\log|R^{{\Lambda}_{l/r}}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma|m-n|

(2.5)

for
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
.

4.

(Eigenvalue separation)
{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)
is the unique eigenvalue of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
in
B_{7\rho/4}(E_{*})

\displaystyle\chi_{\{{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)\}}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta))

\displaystyle=\chi_{B_{7\rho/4}(E_{*})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)).

(2.6)

In particular, we have the partial resolvent bound

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta),\{{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)\})\|\leq 4\rho^{-1},\quad E\in B_{3\rho/2}(E_{*}).

(2.7)

5.

(Eigenvector stability) The unit eigenvector
{\psi}(\theta)
of
H^{{\Lambda}}(\theta)
has stably small
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values:

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\partial_{\theta}{\psi})\|\leq 25D_{0}\delta\rho^{-1}.

(2.8)

The remainder of this section proceeds under Assumption
1
.

2.1
The resonant eigenpair of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}

Under Assumption
1
, we first show the existence of a unique eigenpair of
H^{\Lambda}
which is localized near near
{\Lambda}
. Specifically, denoting the localization region
\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}
and modified shoulders
\widehat{\Lambda}_{l/r}
by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle:=[\inf{\Lambda}-\ell,\sup{\Lambda}+\ell]\cap\widehat{\Lambda},

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}_{l/r}

\displaystyle:={\Lambda}_{l/r}\setminus\Lambda_{\text{loc}},

we have the following:

Proposition 2.1

(Simple resonant eigenpair)
.

Under Assumption
1
, we have:

1.

Any unit eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with corresponding eigenvalue
\widehat{E}
with
|\widehat{E}-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
is Anderson localized on
\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}
:

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}}

\displaystyle\geq 2/3,

\displaystyle\log|\widehat{\psi}(j)|

\displaystyle\leq-\gamma\dist(j,{\Lambda}),\quad j\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{l/r}

2.

There exists a Rellich pair
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}},\widehat{\psi})
for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}:B_{\rho/8D_{0}(\theta_{*})}\to B_{\rho/4}(E_{*})
and eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
satisfying

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-\mathbf{E}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta,

(2.9)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}

\displaystyle={\psi}+\phi

(2.10)

where
\|\phi\|\leq 3\delta/\rho
.

3.

Uniformly for
\theta
with
|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho/8D_{0}
and
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{5}{4}\rho
, there are no other Rellich functions of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
within
\rho/4
of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}
:

\displaystyle\left\|R_{\perp}\left(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta),\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)\}\right)\right\|\leq 4\rho^{-1}.

(2.11)

In particular, this applies to all
|E-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)|\leq\rho
.

Proof.

Suppose
(\widehat{E},\widehat{\psi})
is a Rellich pair for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
such that
|\widehat{E}-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
normalized so that
\|\widehat{\psi}\|=1
. Then, denoting
{\Lambda}_{l}=[{a}_{l},{b}_{l}]
, we have for
j\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{l}

\displaystyle\log|\widehat{\psi}(j)|

\displaystyle=\log|\varepsilon R_{\theta,\widehat{E}}^{{\Lambda}_{l}}({b}_{l},j)\widehat{\psi}({b}_{l}+1)|

\displaystyle\leq\log|\varepsilon|-\gamma|j-{b}_{l}|

\displaystyle\leq-\gamma(|j-{b}_{l}|+1)

\displaystyle=-\gamma\dist(j,{\Lambda})

by the Poisson formula (
1.11
) and (
2.5
). The case
j\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{r}
is entirely analogous.

Using this decay, we have that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{\widehat{\Lambda}_{l/r}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\widehat{\Lambda}_{l/r}}e^{-\gamma\dist(j,{\Lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{1-e^{-\gamma}}<\frac{1}{6}.

Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}}\geq 2/3

for any resonant eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
.

Denoting by
P=\chi_{B_{3\rho/2}(E_{*})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}})
and by
\chi=\chi_{B_{3\rho/2}(E_{*})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}})
, suppose
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}},\widehat{\psi})
is a resonant eigenpair of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-E_{*}|<3\rho/2
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|(I-\chi)P\|

\displaystyle=\|R_{\perp}(\mathbf{E};H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}},{\mathbf{E}})(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})P\|

\displaystyle\leq 4\rho^{-1}\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}P\|\leq 16\delta\rho^{-1}.

Lemma
1.1
and assumption (
2.6
) imply
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
has at most one such resonant eigenvector.

The existence of a Rellich pair
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}},\widehat{\psi})
with
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}:B_{\rho/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\to B_{\rho/4}(E_{*})
and satisfying (
2.9
) follows from Lemma
1.2
after noting that

\displaystyle\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta)-{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)){\chi}\|\leq\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta

(2.12)

and, by (
1.7
),

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{E}}(\theta)-E_{*}|

\displaystyle\leq D_{0}|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho/8.

Thus,
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}
is the unique eigenvalue of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-E_{*}|<3\rho/2
, and (
2.11
) holds.
Finally, since
\widehat{\psi}
is the unique unit vector (up to a sign) in the image of
\chi_{B_{\rho}({\mathbf{E}})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}})
, (
1.5
) implies (
2.10
).
∎

2.2
Local Rellich function structure

In the simple resonant setting, the resonant Rellich pair
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}},\widehat{\psi})
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is well-approximated by the resonant Rellich pair
({\mathbf{E}},{\psi})
of
H^{{\Lambda}}
:

Proposition 2.2
.

Uniformly for
\theta\in B_{\rho/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
, there is a constant
C=C(v)
depending only on
v
so that

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-{\mathbf{E}})\right|\leq C\frac{\delta}{\rho^{k}},\quad 0\leq k\leq 2

(2.13)

and

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi})\|\leq C\frac{\delta}{\rho^{k+1}},\quad 0\leq k\leq 1.

(2.14)

Proof.

By
the previous proposition,
we have

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-{\mathbf{E}}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta,

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq 3\frac{\delta}{\rho}.

Thus, by the Feynman formula (
1.7
) and Cauchy-Schwarz,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-{\mathbf{E}})\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\langle\widehat{\psi},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}\rangle-\langle{\psi},V^{\prime}{\psi}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|V^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}\|

\displaystyle\leq 6D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}.

By (
1.8
), (
2.7
), and (
2.11
), we immediately get

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}\psi\|\leq\frac{4D_{0}}{\rho},\quad\|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}\|\leq\frac{4D_{0}}{\rho}.

By differentiating the relation
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\widehat{\psi}=\widehat{\mathbf{E}}\widehat{\psi}
, we likewise find that

\displaystyle(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})(\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}))

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-V^{\prime})\widehat{\psi}-(\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}-V^{\prime}){\psi}+(\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+({\mathbf{E}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}))\partial_{\theta}{\psi}

\displaystyle=(\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-V^{\prime})(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi})+(\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-{\mathbf{E}})){\psi}+(\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+({\mathbf{E}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}))\partial_{\theta}{\psi}

and so, by the triangle inequality, the assumption (
2.8
), and the above estimates,

\displaystyle\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})(\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}))\|

\displaystyle\leq 6D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}+6D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}+25D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}+8D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}

\displaystyle\leq 45D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}

Denote by
\widehat{\chi}
the spectral projection onto
\widehat{\psi}
. Then, since
R_{\perp}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}})(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})=I-\widehat{\chi}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi})\|

\displaystyle\leq\|R_{\perp}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}})\|\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})(\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}))\|+|\langle\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}),\widehat{\psi}\rangle|

\displaystyle=\|R_{\perp}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}})\|\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}})(\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}))\|+|\langle\widehat{\psi}-{\psi},\partial_{\theta}\psi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 180D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}+12D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 200D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}

where in the second line we have used that
\langle\partial_{\theta}\psi,\psi\rangle=0
(and similarly for
\widehat{\psi}
).

Finally, by (
1.8
), (
1.9
), and Cauchy-Schwarz, we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}^{2}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}-{\mathbf{E}})\right|

\displaystyle\leq\left|\langle\widehat{\psi},V^{\prime\prime}\widehat{\psi}\rangle-\langle{\psi},V^{\prime\prime}{\psi}\rangle\right|+2\left|\langle\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}\rangle-\langle\partial_{\theta}{\psi},V^{\prime}{\psi}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|V^{\prime\prime}\|_{\infty}\|\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}\|+2\|V^{\prime}\|_{\infty}\left(\|\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\psi}-{\psi})\|+\|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\psi}\|\|\widehat{\psi}-{\psi}\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 6D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho}+2D_{0}\left(200D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}+12D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 500D_{0}^{2}\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}.

Taking
C=C(v)=500D_{0}^{2}
proves the proposition.
∎

3
Double resonance

In this section, we study the more complicated situation of double resonance, where an interval
\widehat{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}
contains precisely two resonating subintervals
{\Lambda}_{\pm}\subset\widehat{\Lambda}
such that
H^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}(\theta_{*})
each has a unique eigenvalue
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
near a fixed
E_{**}
; in our setting, these eigenvalues will have relatively large, opposite signed derivatives:
\pm\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}\geq\nu>0
. Under smallness and stability assumptions on the boundary values of the corresponding eigenvector, alongside off-diagonal Green’s function decay on the maximal connected components of
\widehat{\Lambda}\setminus({\Lambda}_{-}\cup{\Lambda}_{+})
, we prove that
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
has precisely two well-separated resonant Rellich children (cf. Figure
2
).

Fix
v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1])
with
\|\partial_{\theta}v\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\theta}^{2}v\|_{\infty}\leq D_{0}
,
(\theta_{**},E_{**})\in\mathbb{T}\times[-2,2]
, and
0<\varepsilon<1/7
. Let
\widehat{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}
be an interval containing two subintervals
{\Lambda}_{-},{\Lambda}_{+}
with
\dist({\Lambda}_{-},{\Lambda}_{+})\geq\max_{\pm}\{|{\Lambda}_{\pm}|\}
and denote the corresponding partition
\widehat{\Lambda}=:{\Lambda}_{l}\cup{\Lambda}_{-}\cup{\Lambda}_{c}\cup{\Lambda}_{+}\cup{\Lambda}_{r}
by
\mathcal{P}
; despite the notation, we do not insist
{\Lambda}_{-}
be left of
{\Lambda}_{+}
. Let
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{-}\cup{\Lambda}_{c}\cup{\Lambda}_{+}
,
{L}=|{\Lambda}|
, and fix constants
\widecheck{\rho},\delta,\gamma,\ell,\nu>0
satisfying the following relations:

\displaystyle\delta<\widecheck{\rho}^{3}/2

\displaystyle<\frac{\widecheck{\rho}^{2}\nu}{2400D_{0}}<1/16,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\log 7\leq\gamma

\displaystyle\leq|\log\varepsilon|,

(3.2)

\displaystyle 8\delta\leq\frac{\widecheck{\rho}^{2}\nu^{3}}{192D_{0}^{3}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\widecheck{\rho}}{48D_{0}^{2}}.

(3.3)

Assumption 2
.

Suppose the following hold for
|\theta-\theta_{**}|<{\widecheck{\rho}}/8D_{0}
:

1.

(Double resonance) Each operator
H^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}(\theta_{**})
has a unique eigenpair
({\mathbf{E}}_{\pm},{\psi}_{\pm})(\theta_{**})
such that

{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta_{**})={\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{**})=E_{**}
.

2.

(Eigenvector decay) The unit eigenvector
{\psi}_{\pm}(\theta)
of
H^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}(\theta)
has
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values no larger than
\delta/\varepsilon
; i.e. one has

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{\psi}_{\pm}\|\leq 2\delta,\quad j=1,2.

(3.4)

Furthermore, for any resonant eigenpair
(\widehat{E},\widehat{\psi})
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
|\widehat{E}-E_{**}|<\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}
, the unit eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
also has
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values no larger than
\delta/\varepsilon
; i.e., one has

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\widehat{\psi}\|\leq 4\delta.

(3.5)

3.

(Green’s function decay off
{\Lambda}_{-}\cup{\Lambda}_{+}
)
For
m,n\in{\Lambda}_{l/c/r}
with
|m-n|\geq\ell
one has

\displaystyle\log|R^{{\Lambda}_{l/c/r}}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma|m-n|

(3.6)

for
|E-E_{**}|<\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}
.

4.

(Eigenvalue separation)
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}(\theta)
are the only eigenvalues of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)
in
B_{7\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
:

\displaystyle\chi_{\{{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta)\}\cup\{{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)\}}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta))=\chi_{B_{7\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta)).

(3.7)

In particular, we have

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(\theta),\{{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta)\}\cup\{{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)\})\|

\displaystyle\leq{4\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}},\quad E\in B_{3\widecheck{\rho}/2}(E_{**}).

(3.8)

5.

(Eigenvector stability) The unit eigenvectors
{\psi}_{\pm}(\theta)
of
H^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}(\theta)
have stably small
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values:

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\partial_{\theta}{\psi}_{\pm})\|\leq 25D_{0}\delta\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}.

(3.9)

6.

(Transversality of Rellich functions) The eigenpairs
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
have large, opposite-signed derivatives:

\displaystyle\pm\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}(\theta)\geq\nu.

(3.10)

The remainder of this section proceeds under Assumption
2
.

3.1
The resonant eigenpairs of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}

Denote by

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\text{loc}}

\displaystyle:=[\inf{\Lambda}_{\pm}-\ell,\sup{\Lambda}_{\pm}+\ell]\cap\widehat{\Lambda},

\displaystyle{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{l/r}

\displaystyle:={\Lambda}_{l/r}\setminus\Lambda_{\text{loc}}.

Under Assumption
2
, the operator
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
has precisely two resonant eigenpairs
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet},\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet})
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
, and
\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet}
are simultaneously localized on
\Lambda_{\text{loc}}
:

Proposition 3.1

(Double resonant eigenpairs)
.

Under Assumption
2
above, we have:

1.

Any unit eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with corresponding eigenvalue
\widehat{E}
with
|\widehat{E}-E_{**}|<\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}
is Anderson localized on
\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}
:

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}}

\displaystyle\geq 2/3,

\displaystyle\log|\widehat{\psi}(j)|

\displaystyle\leq-\gamma\dist(j,{\Lambda}),\quad j\in{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{l/r}.

2.

There exist two Rellich pairs
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet},\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet})
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}:B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\to B_{\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
.
We normalize so that
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}>\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
. The eigenvectors satisfy

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}

\displaystyle=A{\psi}_{+}+B{\psi}_{-}+\phi_{\vee}

(3.11)

\displaystyle\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge}

\displaystyle=B{\psi}_{+}-A{\psi}_{-}+\phi_{\wedge}

(3.12)

where
A(\theta)
and
B(\theta)
satisfy
A^{2}+B^{2}=1
and
\|\phi_{\bullet}\|\leq 24\delta{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}
.

3.

Uniformly for
\theta
with
|\theta-\theta_{**}|<\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}
and
|E-E_{**}|<\frac{5}{4}\widecheck{\rho}
, there are no other Rellich functions within
\widecheck{\rho}/4
of
E
:

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta),\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)\}\cup\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\})\|\leq{4\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}.

(3.13)

Proof.

The decay of any resonant eigenvector follows from the Green’s function decay (
3.6
) and the Poisson formula (
1.11
) identically to the single resonant case, so here we refer the reader to the proof of Proposition
2.1
.

Using this decay, we have that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{l/r}}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in\Lambda_{\pm}}e^{-\gamma\dist(j,{\Lambda})}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{e^{-\gamma}}{1-e^{-\gamma}}<\frac{1}{6}.

Consequently, we have that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}\|_{\Lambda_{\text{loc}}}\geq 2/3

for any resonant unit eigenvector
\widehat{\psi}
.

We fix
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
and will suppress its notation.
Denoting by
P=\chi_{B_{3\widecheck{\rho}/2}(E_{**})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}})
and by
\chi=\chi_{B_{3\widecheck{\rho}/2}(E_{**})}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}})
, suppose
(\widehat{E},\widehat{\psi})
is a resonant eigenpair of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
|\widehat{E}-E_{**}|<3\widecheck{\rho}/2
. Then we have

\displaystyle\|(I-\chi)P\|

\displaystyle=\|R_{\perp}(\widehat{E};H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}},B_{\widecheck{\rho}}(E_{**}))(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-\widehat{E})P\|

\displaystyle\leq 4{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}P\|\leq 16\delta{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}.

Lemma
1.1
and the assumption (
3.7
) imply
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
has at most two resonant eigenpairs
(\widehat{E},\widehat{\psi})
satisfying
|\widehat{E}-E_{**}|<3\widecheck{\rho}/2
.
If such an eigenpair exists, since
\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-\widehat{E})P\|\leq 4\delta
, and
\{{\psi}_{+},{\psi}_{-}\}
forms an orthonormal basis for the image of
\chi_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}}}(\widehat{E})
, Lemma
1.2
implies
\widehat{\psi}
must be of the form (
3.11
).

To see the existence of two eigenpairs
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet},\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet})
with
E_{\bullet}:B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\to B_{\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
, first note that,

\displaystyle\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){\psi}_{\pm}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma^{\Lambda}_{\mathcal{P}}{\psi}_{\pm}\|\leq 2\delta<\widecheck{\rho}/8

and, by (
1.7
),

\displaystyle|{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}-E_{**}|\leq D_{0}|\theta-\theta_{**}|<\widecheck{\rho}/8,

so at least one such eigenpair (e.g.,
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee},\widehat{\psi}_{\vee})
) must exist by Lemma
1.2
. Let
A
and
B
be defined as in (
3.11
), and denote by
{\psi}_{\vee}:=A{\psi}_{+}+B{\psi}_{-}
and by
{\psi}_{\wedge}:=B{\psi}_{+}-A{\psi}_{-}
.

Denote by

P^{c}_{\vee}:=I-\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}^{\top}
and by
\chi^{c}_{\vee}:=I-{\psi}_{\vee}{\psi}_{\vee}^{\top}
. By (
3.11
), we have
\|P_{\vee}^{c}-\chi_{\vee}^{c}\|\leq 48\delta{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}
; thus, letting
\widehat{H}_{\wedge}:=P_{\vee}^{c}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}P_{\vee}^{c}
and
{H}_{\wedge}:=\chi_{\vee}^{c}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\chi_{\vee}^{c}
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\widehat{H}_{\wedge}-E_{**}){\psi}_{\wedge}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|(\widehat{H}_{\wedge}-{H}_{\wedge}){\psi}_{\wedge}\|+\|({H}_{\wedge}-E_{**}){\psi}_{\wedge}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\|\|P_{\vee}^{c}-\chi_{\vee}^{c}\|+\|\chi_{\vee}^{c}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-E_{**}){\psi}_{\wedge}\|

\displaystyle\leq 192\delta{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}+|A|(|{\mathbf{E}}_{-}-E_{**}|+2\delta)+|B|(|{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-E_{**}|+2\delta)

\displaystyle\leq 192\delta{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}+\sqrt{2}(\widecheck{\rho}/8+2\delta)

\displaystyle<\widecheck{\rho}/4

where in the penultimate line we have used that
|A|+|B|\leq\sqrt{2}
. Thus,
\widehat{H}_{\wedge}
(and consequently
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
) must have an eigenpair
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge},\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge})
where
\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge}
satisfies (
3.12
).
∎

3.2
Local Rellich function structure

Keeping in mind the eigenvalue separation Lemma
1.5
, we fix a separation constant

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\varepsilon/3\right)^{3|\Lambda_{\textnormal{loc}}|}.

(3.14)

We also define the crossed parent curves

\displaystyle{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}:=\max\{{\mathbf{E}}_{+},{\mathbf{E}}_{-}\},\quad{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}:=\min\{{\mathbf{E}}_{+},{\mathbf{E}}_{-}\}.

In this subsection, we will prove a precise formulation of the heuristic demonstrated in Figure
2
. In order to do so, we will introduce two new parameters: an intermediate parameter
\eta\ll\widecheck{\rho}
, representing the distance from
\theta_{**}
where
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
begins to deviate from
{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
, and a second resonance parameter
\rho\ll\eta
with respect to which, away from
B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
, the parent curves
{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
are simple-resonant.

Theorem 3.2
.

Under the assumptions above, the two Rellich pairs
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet},\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet})
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
, for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
from Proposition
3.1
satisfy the following:

1.

The Rellich functions
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
are Morse with precisely one critical point in
B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
, with Morse constants

\displaystyle d

\displaystyle=\nu/12,

\displaystyle D

\displaystyle=2D_{0}(1+D_{0}\widehat{\sigma}^{-1})

2.

The Rellich functions
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
are uniformly separated on
B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
:

\displaystyle\inf_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-\sup_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\geq\frac{\nu\widehat{\sigma}}{2D_{0}+\nu}

3.

Fix
\eta<\frac{\nu^{2}\widecheck{\rho}}{100D_{0}^{3}}
and
\rho<\frac{8}{9}\nu\eta
. There is a constant
C=C(v)
depending only on
v
so that, for
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\setminus B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet})\right|\leq C\frac{\delta}{\rho^{k}},\quad 0\leq k\leq 2.

(3.15)

3.2.1
Lower bounds on the second derivative

By Proposition
3.1
, there exist two Rellich pairs
(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet},\widehat{\psi}_{\bullet})
for
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
with
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}:B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\to B_{\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
. We will prove lower bounds on the second derivative near
\theta_{**}
:

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\eta<\frac{\nu^{2}\widecheck{\rho}}{100D_{0}^{3}}
.
There exists a constant
C=C(v)
depending only on
v
such that, for
\theta\in B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)\right|\leq\nu/12

\displaystyle\implies\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)\geq C\frac{\nu^{5/2}\widecheck{\rho}}{\delta},

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\right|\leq\nu/12

\displaystyle\implies-\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\geq C\frac{\nu^{5/2}\widecheck{\rho}}{\delta}.

We prove this Proposition via a series of lemmas. Suppose that
|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta_{**})|\geq|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{**})|
(the argument is similar for the opposite case), and define
1\leq r\leq D_{0}/\nu
such that

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta_{**})|=r|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{**})|.

Lemma 3.4
.

Uniformly for
\theta\in B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
,

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}\left({\mathbf{E}}_{+}+r{\mathbf{E}}_{-}\right)(\theta)\right|\leq 8D_{0}^{3}\frac{\eta}{\widecheck{\rho}\nu}

Proof.

By equations (
1.9
) and (
3.8
), we have the uniform bound

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}\right|\leq D_{0}+2D_{0}^{2}\|R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}({\mathbf{E}}_{\pm})\|\leq D_{0}+2D_{0}^{2}{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}.

The claimed bound follows by integrating; specifically, since
\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}
and
\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}
have opposite signs, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta_{**})+r\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{**})=0,

and so

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}\left({\mathbf{E}}_{+}+r{\mathbf{E}}_{-}\right)(\theta)\right|

\displaystyle=\left|\int_{\theta_{**}}^{\theta}\partial_{\theta}^{2}({\mathbf{E}}_{+}+r{\mathbf{E}}_{-}){\mathrm{d}}t\right|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\theta_{**}}^{\theta}\left|\partial_{\theta}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}\right||{\mathrm{d}}t|+r\int_{\theta_{**}}^{\theta}\left|\partial_{\theta}^{2}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}\right||{\mathrm{d}}t|

\displaystyle\leq(1+r)(D_{0}+2D_{0}^{2}{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}})\eta

\displaystyle\leq 8D_{0}^{3}\frac{\eta}{\widecheck{\rho}\nu}\qed

We now prove Proposition
3.3
for
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
, noting that the case
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
is entirely analogous. To begin, we use Proposition
3.1
to relate
\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
to the Rellich functions from the previous scale:

Lemma 3.5
.

With the notation from Proposition
3.1
and
r
as above,
if
|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|\leq\nu/12
, then

\displaystyle A^{2}\geq\frac{5}{12r},\quad B^{2}\geq\frac{1}{4}.

Proof.

We simply apply the Feynman formula (
1.7
) and make the substitutions (
3.11
) and (
3.12
), using the fact that
{\psi}_{\pm}
are disjointly supported. Specifically,

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\vee},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=A^{2}\langle{\psi}_{+},V^{\prime}{\psi}_{+}\rangle+B^{2}\langle{\psi}_{-},V^{\prime}{\psi}_{-}\rangle+2\langle\phi_{\vee},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle-\langle\phi_{\vee},V^{\prime}\phi_{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=A^{2}\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{+}+B^{2}\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}+2\langle\phi_{\vee},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle-\langle\phi_{\vee},V^{\prime}\phi_{\vee}\rangle

\displaystyle=(B^{2}-rA^{2})\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}+\xi,

where the error term
\xi
has

\displaystyle|\xi|\leq 96D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}}+8D_{0}^{3}\frac{\eta}{\widecheck{\rho}\nu}<\frac{1}{12}\nu

by Cauchy-Schwarz, Proposition
3.1
, equations (
3.1
) and (
3.3
), and the previous lemma.

By assumption, we have
|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{-}|\geq\nu
; thus, if
|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|\leq\nu/12
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\nu}{12}>|B^{2}-rA^{2}|\nu-|\xi|,

and so

\displaystyle|B^{2}-rA^{2}|<\frac{1}{12}+\frac{|\xi|}{\nu}<\frac{1}{6}.

It follows that

\displaystyle(1+r)A^{2}=1-(B^{2}-rA^{2})\geq 5/6;

the inequalities on
A^{2}
and
B^{2}
follow.
∎

We now proceed to prove Proposition
3.3
.

Proof of Proposition
3.3
.

Let
\theta\in B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
. By expanding the Feynman-type formula (
1.9
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}

\displaystyle=\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\vee},V^{\prime\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle-\frac{2\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle^{2}}{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}}-2\langle R^{\Lambda}_{\perp}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee};H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta),\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}\}\cup\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}\})V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle.

(3.16)

The first term is bounded by
D_{0}
. Furthermore,
since
|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-E_{**}|<\widecheck{\rho}/4
,
the third term is bounded by
8D_{0}^{2}{\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}}
by Proposition
3.1
.
We will show that the second term is large.

By Proposition
3.1
and the previous lemma,

\displaystyle\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(AB\left(\langle{\psi}_{+},V^{\prime}{\psi}_{+}\rangle-\langle{\psi}_{-},V^{\prime}{\psi}_{-}\rangle\right)+\langle\phi_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle+\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\phi_{\vee}\rangle-\langle\phi_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\phi_{\vee}\rangle\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\geq(AB)^{2}\left(\partial_{\theta}({\mathbf{E}}_{+}-{\mathbf{E}}_{-})\right)^{2}-288D_{0}^{2}\delta/\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5}{48r}(1+r)^{2}\nu^{2}-288D_{0}^{2}\delta/\widecheck{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{5}{48}\nu^{2}-288D_{0}^{2}\delta/\widecheck{\rho}\geq\frac{1}{16}\nu^{2},

where the last inequality follows from (
3.1
).

It remains to show that the denominator
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
is small. By Proposition
3.1
,

\displaystyle|A||{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|

\displaystyle\leq|{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|\left(|\langle{\psi}_{+},\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle|+|\langle{\psi}_{+},\phi_{\vee}\rangle|\right)

\displaystyle\leq|\langle{\psi}_{+},(H^{{\Lambda}_{+}}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee})\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle|+|{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}||\langle{\psi}_{+},\phi_{\vee}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 40\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}}.

Similarly,
|B||{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}|\leq 40\delta/\widecheck{\rho}
. Since

\displaystyle\min\{|A|,|B|\}\geq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{r}}\geq\sqrt{\frac{\nu}{D_{0}}},

it follows that

\displaystyle|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}|\leq|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}-{\mathbf{E}}_{+}|+|{\mathbf{E}}_{+}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|\leq 80\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}}\sqrt{\frac{D_{0}}{\nu}}.

Combining the above estimates, we get

\displaystyle\frac{2\langle\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge},V^{\prime}\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}\rangle^{2}}{|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{640\sqrt{D_{0}}}\frac{\widecheck{\rho}\nu^{5/2}}{\delta},

and so

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}|

\displaystyle\geq C\frac{\widecheck{\rho}\nu^{5/2}}{\delta}

with
C=C(v)=(800\sqrt{D_{0}})^{-1}
, e.g.. The bound for
\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
is similar. By (
3.3
), the middle term
in (
3.16
) dominates; the sign of

\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
then must match the sign of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
,
which is positive for
\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
.
∎

3.2.2
Uniform local separation of Rellich functions

By Proposition
3.1
,
\widehat{\psi}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\psi}_{\wedge}
are simultaneously localized on the interval
\Lambda_{\text{loc}}
, so Lemma
1.5
guarantees Rellich function separation

\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\geq\widehat{\sigma}

(3.17)

for each
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}
. In fact, the Rellich functions are uniformly separated:

Proposition 3.6
.

The two resonant Rellich functions
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}:B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\to B_{\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
are uniformly separated for all
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
; specifically,

\displaystyle\inf_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-\sup_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\geq\frac{\nu\widehat{\sigma}}{2D_{0}+\nu}

The proof of this proposition involves a novel argument utilizing the Cauchy Interlacing Theorem:

Theorem 3.7

(Cauchy Interlacing Theorem)
.

Let
A
be an
n\times n
Hermitian matrix, let
m\leq n
, and let
P
be an
m\times n
matrix such that
PP^{*}=I_{m\times m}
. Let
B=PAP^{*}
be a compression of
A
, and denote the (ordered) eigenvalues of
A
(respectively
B
) by
\alpha_{1}\leq\alpha_{2}\leq\dots\leq\alpha_{n}
(resp.,
\beta_{1}\leq\beta_{2}\leq\dots\leq\beta_{m}
). Then

\displaystyle\alpha_{k}\leq\beta_{k}\leq\alpha_{k+n-m}.

The interlacing theorem is proven via a standard Min-Max argument, cf. Appendix B. We will use the interlacing theorem to compare
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
to a pair of auxiliary operators, which we now define. Let
{P}_{\pm}:={\psi}_{\pm}{\psi}_{\pm}^{\top}
, let
{Q}_{\pm}:=I-{P}_{\pm}
, and consider the auxiliary operators

\displaystyle{H}_{\pm}

\displaystyle={Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}+{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}{P}_{\pm}.

\lambda_{-}
{\mathbf{E}}_{-}
{\mathbf{E}}_{+}
\lambda_{+}
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\lor}
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\land}

Figure 5
:
A cartoon illustration of the interlacing argument in Lemma
3.8
; the dashed lines represent the current scale Rellich functions
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
, the full lines are the next scale eigenvalues
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
, and the dotted lines are the monotone interlacing curves
\lambda_{\pm}
well-approximating
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
. The differences between
\lambda_{\pm}
and
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
are exaggerated for emphasis.

Lemma 3.8
.

The compressions
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
have unique eigenvalues
\lambda_{\mp}:B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\to B_{\widecheck{\rho}/4}(E_{**})
near
E_{**}
. These eigenvalues interlace
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
:

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\leq\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)\leq\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)

and are uniformly
C^{1}
-close to previous-scale eigenvalues; that is, there exists a constant
C=C(v)
depending only on
v
so that

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(\lambda_{\pm}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm})|

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}^{2k}},\quad k=0,1.

In particular,
\lambda_{\pm}
are monotone having different signed derivatives, and

\displaystyle\pm\partial_{\theta}\lambda_{\pm}\geq\nu/2

uniformly in
B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
.

Proof.

Since
{Q}_{\pm}
and
{P}_{\pm}
are orthogonal projections and
(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){P}_{\pm}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}
, we have

\displaystyle H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{H}_{\pm}

\displaystyle={Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){P}_{\pm}

\displaystyle={Q}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){P}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){Q}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){P}_{\pm}

\displaystyle={Q}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}

\displaystyle=\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}

By (
3.4
), we have
\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{P}_{\pm}\|\leq 2\delta
, and so

\displaystyle\|H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{H}_{\pm}\|\leq 4\delta.

It follows from the Min-Max principle that any eigenvalue of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
must be within
4\delta
of an eigenvalue of
{H}_{\pm}
, and conversely. In particular,
{H}_{\pm}
each have at least two eigenvalues in
B_{\frac{1}{4}\widecheck{\rho}+4\delta}(E_{**})
and at most two eigenvalues in
B_{\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}-4\delta}(E_{**})
. One of those eigenvalues must be
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
by definition; the other is an eigenvalue of
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
.

Denote by
\lambda_{\mp}
the unique eigenvalue of
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
in
B_{\frac{1}{4}\widecheck{\rho}+4\delta}(E_{**})
. By Cauchy Interlacing, the eigenvalues of
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
interlace those of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
. Since
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
has precisely one eigenvalue in
B_{\frac{1}{4}\widecheck{\rho}+4\delta}(E_{**})
, it must lie between
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
; that is,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\leq\lambda_{\mp}(\theta)\leq\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta).

We will show now that
\lambda_{\mp}
is
C^{1}
-close to
{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}
. By (
3.4
), we have

\displaystyle\|({Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}){\psi}_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle=\|{Q}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}){\psi}_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle=\|{Q}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{\psi}_{\mp}\|\leq 2\delta.

Let
\varphi_{\mp}
denote the unit eigenvector of
{H}_{\pm}
corresponding to
\lambda_{\mp}
.
Since
\lambda_{\mp}
is the unique eigenvalue of
{Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}
in
B_{\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}-4\delta}(E_{**})\supset B_{\frac{5}{4}\widecheck{\rho}-4\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\mp})
, it follows from Lemma
1.2
that we have

\displaystyle|\lambda_{\mp}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}|\leq 2\delta

and (up to a choice of sign for
\varphi_{\pm}
)

\displaystyle\|\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp}\|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{2}\delta}{\frac{5}{4}\widecheck{\rho}-4\delta}\leq\frac{4\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}}.

By the Feynman formula (
1.7
), we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\theta}\lambda_{\mp}

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{\mp},\partial_{\theta}({Q}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm})\varphi_{\mp}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\varphi_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle-2\langle\varphi_{\mp},{P}_{\pm}^{\prime}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle.

Since
{Q}_{\pm}{\psi}_{\mp}={\psi}_{\mp}
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle-\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}|

\displaystyle=|\langle\varphi_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle-\langle{\psi}_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}{\psi}_{\mp}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle+\langle{\psi}_{\mp},{Q}_{\pm}V^{\prime}{Q}_{\pm}(\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp})\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 2\|V^{\prime}\|\|\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle\leq 8D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}}.

It remains to show that
|\langle\varphi_{\mp},{P}_{\pm}^{\prime}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle|
is small. By (
1.8
) we have

\displaystyle-{P}_{\pm}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}({\mathbf{E}}_{\pm})V^{\prime}{P}_{\pm}+{P}_{\pm}V^{\prime}R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}({\mathbf{E}}_{\pm})

On the one hand, we have

\displaystyle\|{P}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle=\|{P}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}){Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle=\|{P}_{\pm}(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}){Q}_{\pm}(\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp})+{P}_{\pm}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{\psi}_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle\leq 24\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}},

and so

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi_{\mp},R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}V^{\prime}{P}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle V^{\prime}R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}\varphi_{\mp},{P}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|V^{\prime}\|\|R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}\|\|{P}_{\pm}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle\leq 96D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}^{2}}.

On the other hand, since
{P}_{\pm}{\psi}_{\mp}=0
, we have

\displaystyle|\langle\varphi_{\mp},{P}_{\pm}V^{\prime}R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle|

\displaystyle=|\langle\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp},{P}_{\pm}V^{\prime}R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\|\varphi_{\mp}-{\psi}_{\mp}\|\|V^{\prime}\|\|R_{\perp}^{{\Lambda}_{\pm}}\|\|H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}{Q}_{\pm}\varphi_{\mp}\|

\displaystyle\leq 48D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}^{2}}

Bringing it all together, it follows that

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}\lambda_{\mp}-\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\mp}|

\displaystyle\leq 400D_{0}\frac{\delta}{\widecheck{\rho}^{2}};

the other claims follow.
∎

Proof of Proposition
3.6
.

By the previous lemma, we have separated the Rellich functions
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
by two transverse curves. We use the quantitative transversality to now derive the size of the gap.

Let
\theta_{\bullet}
denote the minimizing/maximizing values of
\theta
for
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
, and let
h=|\theta_{\vee}-\theta_{\wedge}|
.
By (
3.17
), we have

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{\vee})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\wedge})\geq\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{\vee})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\vee})-|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\vee})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\wedge})|\geq\widehat{\sigma}-D_{0}h,

which is an effective bound for small
h
.
On the other hand, we have by transversality of the bounding curves
\lambda_{\pm}

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{\vee})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\wedge})\geq\frac{\nu}{2}|\theta_{\vee}-\theta_{\wedge}|=\frac{\nu h}{2},

which is an effective bound for large
h
.
Taking a convex combination of the two bounds yields

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{\vee})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{\wedge})

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu}{2D_{0}+\nu}(\widehat{\sigma}-D_{0}h)+\frac{2D_{0}}{2D_{0}+\nu}\frac{\nu h}{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{\nu\widehat{\sigma}}{2D_{0}+\nu},

which was the claim.
∎

3.2.3
Simple resonance of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
away from
\theta_{**}

In this section, we will show the following:

Lemma 3.9
.

Let
\theta_{*}\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\setminus B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
, and suppose
E_{*}={\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}(\theta_{*})
. Then
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
