satisfies Assumption
1
for
(\theta_{*},E_{*})
with
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{\pm}
,
\rho,\delta
as above, and Green’s function decay parameters
\widetilde{\ell}\geq 16|\log\varepsilon||\log\rho|
and
\widetilde{\gamma}=\gamma-6|\log\varepsilon||\log\rho|/\widetilde{\ell}
as in Lemma
A.2
.

In particular,
for
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
,
there exists a constant
C=C(v)
depending only on
v
such that we have

\displaystyle\left|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet})\right|\leq C\frac{\delta}{\rho^{k}},\quad 0\leq k\leq 2,

and

\displaystyle\|R_{\perp}(E;H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta),\{\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}(\theta)\})\|\leq 4\rho^{-1}

uniformly for
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\setminus B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})

and
E\in B_{\rho}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}(\theta))
.

Proof.

Items 1, 2, and 5 are immediate by the definition of
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{\pm}
and
E_{*}={\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}(\theta_{*})
.

Suppose that
{\Lambda}={\Lambda}_{-}<{\Lambda}_{+}
(the other cases are completely analogous), and consider now the interval
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}:={\Lambda}_{c}\cup{\Lambda}_{+}\cup{\Lambda}_{r}
. To verify items 3 and 4, we need to verify that
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}
has the Green’s function decay property, and that
H^{\overline{\Lambda}_{r}}
has no eigenvalues in
B_{7\rho/4}(E_{*})=B_{7\rho/4}({\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{*}))
.

To see the eigenvalue separation, notice that, by Assumption
2
,
H^{\overline{\Lambda}_{r}}
satisfies Assumption
1
with
\widecheck{\rho}
replacing
\rho
.
Thus,
H^{\overline{\Lambda}_{r}}
has a unique eigenvalue
\overline{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta):B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\to B_{3\widecheck{\rho}/2}(E_{**})
such that

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)-{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)|\leq 2\delta.

The necessary separation follows from the transversality of
{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
and the definition of
\rho
; indeed, since
\eta>9\rho/8\nu
, for
|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho/8D_{0}
we have

\displaystyle|E_{*}-\overline{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\geq|{\mathbf{E}}_{-}(\theta_{*})-{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta_{*})|-D_{0}|\theta_{*}-\theta|-|{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)-\overline{\mathbf{E}}_{+}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\geq 2\nu\eta-\rho/8-2\delta>2\rho.

It remains to verify Item 3 for
\widetilde{\gamma}
and
\widetilde{\ell}
. The Green’s function
R^{\Lambda_{l}}
has
(\ell,\gamma)
decay (and thus
(\widetilde{\ell},\widetilde{\gamma})
decay) by assumption. On the other hand,
for
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
, the bound we just established implies
\|R^{\overline{\Lambda}_{r}}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 2\rho^{-1}
; thus,
the interval
\overline{\Lambda}_{r}
satisfies Assumption
5
, and so
R^{\overline{\Lambda}_{r}}
also has
(\widetilde{\ell},\widetilde{\gamma})
decay by Lemma
A.2
.
∎

3.2.4
Proof of Theorem
3.2

Proof of Theorem
3.2
.

First, by (
1.7
) and (
1.9
), we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}|\leq D_{0}+D_{0}+2D_{0}^{2}\|R^{\Lambda}_{\perp}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet})\|.

By Proposition
3.1
and Lemma
1.5
, for all
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
, we have

\displaystyle\|R^{\Lambda}_{\perp}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet})\|\leq\widehat{\sigma}^{-1}.

The Morse upper bound
D
for
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
follows immediately.

The Morse lower bound
d
for
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
for
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})\setminus B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
follows from Lemma
3.9
and the transversality assumption on
\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}
; specifically,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}|\geq|\partial_{\theta}{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}|-|\partial_{\theta}(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet})|\geq\nu-C\delta\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}\geq\nu/12

For
\theta\in B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
, the Morse lower bound follows from Proposition
3.3
and the fact that

\displaystyle C\frac{\widecheck{\rho}\nu^{5/2}}{\delta}\geq\nu/12.

That
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
can each have at most one critical point in
B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
follows again from Proposition
3.3
, Lemma
3.9
, and continuity of the second derivative. In particular, by Lemma
3.9
,
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
can only have critical points in
B_{\eta}(\theta_{**})
, and by Proposition
3.3
, the concavity of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}
at any such critical point is uniquely determined.

Items 2 and 3 were shown in Proposition
3.6
and Lemma
3.9
, respectively.
∎

3.2.5
Approximation of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
by previous scale

To conclude this section, we note that the Rellich functions
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee},\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
are uniformly close (on order
\delta
) to the previous-scale functions
{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee},{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
; this implies upper bounds on the size of the vertical gap between the two Rellich functions and on the horizontal deviation of the Rellich functions’ critical points from the center of resonsance
\theta_{**}
.
Since
\delta\gg\widehat{\sigma}
, these estimates are not fine enough to contribute meaningfully to the lower bounds on the second derivative and the size of the gap. Moreover, these results are not necessary to prove our Main Theorem; indeed, larger gaps only help localization and Cantor spectrum, as we outlined in the introduction. Nevertheless, we include these results to provide a more complete picture of the local Rellich function structure.

Proposition 3.10
.

For all
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
, we have

|\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}(\theta)-{\mathbf{E}}_{\bullet}(\theta)|\leq 4\delta,\quad\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}.

(3.18)

Proof.

Fix
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})
.
Letting
{P}_{\pm}={\psi}_{\pm}{\psi}_{\pm}^{\top}
, we have
\|(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\pm}){P}_{\pm}\|=\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}{P}_{\pm}\|\leq 2\delta
by (
3.4
). Thus, by Lemma
1.2
,

H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
must have an eigenvalue in
\overline{B}_{2\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\vee})
and in
\overline{B}_{2\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge})
. If these two intervals are disjoint, (
3.18
) most hold.

Otherwise, let
{P}={P}_{+}+{P}_{-}
, and consider that

\displaystyle\left\|\left(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}-\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}+{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}}{2}\right){P}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}{P}\|+\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\delta+\frac{{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}-{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}}{2}.

Thus, by Lemmas
1.1
and
1.2
,
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
must have two eigenvalues in the interval
[{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}-4\delta,{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}+4\delta]
. Since
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
must have an eigenvalue in
\overline{B}_{2\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\vee})
, the greater of the two eigenvalues must be in
\overline{B}_{4\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\vee})
; similarly, the lesser of the two eigenvalues must be in
\overline{B}_{4\delta}({\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge})
. Thus (
3.18
) holds.
∎

Corollary 3.11
.

We have the following upper bound on the size of the gap between
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
and
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
:

\displaystyle\inf_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-\sup_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)\leq 8\delta.

Proof.

We observe that

\displaystyle\inf_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta)-\sup_{B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{**})}\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta)

\displaystyle\leq\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{**})-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{**})

\displaystyle\leq(\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{**})-E_{**})+(E_{**}-\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}(\theta_{**}))

\displaystyle\leq 4\delta+4\delta=8\delta,

where the last inequality follows from Proposition
3.10
.
∎

Corollary 3.12
.

Let
\theta_{c}
be a critical point of
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
or
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
. Then
|\theta_{c}-\theta_{**}|<8\delta/\nu
.

Proof.

Suppose
\theta_{c}
is the critical point where
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
attains its minimum. If
|\theta_{c}-\theta_{**}|\geq 8\delta/\nu
, then we would have, by Proposition
3.10
,

\displaystyle\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{c})

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{c})-4\delta

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{**})+\nu|\theta_{c}-\theta_{**}|-4\delta

\displaystyle\geq{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{**})+4\delta

\displaystyle\geq\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}(\theta_{**}),

contradicting the fact that
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\vee}
attains its minimum at
\theta_{c}
.

The proof procedes analogously if
\theta_{c}
is the critical point where
\widehat{\mathbf{E}}_{\wedge}
attains its maximum.
∎

4
Resonance via inverse functions

The present section describes carefully the double-resonant situation diagrammed in Figure
1
and contains the final pieces of machinery required to handle our multiscale inductive procedure. The ultimate goal of this discussion is the construction of a covering of the codomain of our cosine-like function on whose components we can control recurrence; cf. Propositions
4.9
and
4.10
below.

It is here that the “cosine-like” properties of our potential and its descendants are explicitly utilized. Specifically, for a cosine-like function
f:I\to J
, the two-monotonicity interval structure allows us to describe double resonances as zeroes of a uniquely defined difference of inverse functions, and the Morse condition will yield upper bounds on the size of images of these inverse functions. Combined with the Diophantine assumption on the frequency
\alpha
, we can then quantifiably separate the double resonances in terms of the Diophantine and Morse parameters. The procedure requires somewhat careful assumptions; we demonstrate the robustness of these assumptions under
C^{2}
perturbations at the end of the section.

Let
I_{\pm}\subset\mathbb{T}
be two closed intervals with disjoint interiors, let
I:=I_{-}\cup I_{+}
,
and consider a
C^{2}
function

\displaystyle f:I\to J,\quad f_{\pm}:=f|_{I_{\pm}},\quad\pm\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}\geq 0.

Assumption 3
.

Suppose the following hold:

1.

The function
f
is Morse on
I
:

d\leq|\partial_{\theta}f|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f|\leq D

2.

Each function
f_{\pm}
maps onto
J
:

f_{\pm}(I_{\pm})=J

3.

There is a constant
0<\nu<d/2
such that
|\partial_{\theta}f|\geq\nu
on the boundary points of
I
.

With these assumptions, we can define a function
T_{f}:J\to(I_{+}-I_{-})
by

\displaystyle T_{f}(E):=f_{+}^{-1}(E)-f_{-}^{-1}(E).

We also fix a constant
D_{0}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\theta\in I}|\partial_{\theta}f(\theta)|\leq D_{0}\leq D.

4.1
Preimages and crossing points

We begin with a few lemmas about
f_{\pm}
and
T_{f}
:

Lemma 4.1
.

The derivative
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
is monotone on each connected component of

\displaystyle I_{\pm,<d/2}:=\left\{\theta\in I_{\pm}:|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta)|<d/2\right\}.

In particular, any critical point of
f_{\pm}
must lie at the boundary of
I_{\pm}
; and each connected component of
I_{\pm,<\nu}
must contain a critical point.

Proof.

That
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
is monotone on each such connected component follows immediately from the Morse condition, since
|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f|\geq d/2
and
\partial_{\theta}^{2}f
is continuous. Since
\pm\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
is strictly monotone and nonnegative, zeros must lie on the boundary of
I_{\pm}
.
By monotonicity of
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
, any connected component of
I_{\pm,<\nu}
must contain a boundary point of
I_{\pm}
; since that point cannot be a boundary point of
I
, it must belong to
I_{+}\cap I_{-}
, i.e., it must be a critical point.
∎

Lemma 4.2
.

For any
\theta
and
\theta_{*}
in
I_{\pm}
,

\displaystyle|f_{\pm}(\theta)-f_{\pm}(\theta_{*})|\geq\frac{d}{12}|\theta-\theta_{*}|^{2}.

In particular, for any subinterval
J_{0}\subset J
,

\displaystyle\frac{|J_{0}|}{D_{0}}\leq|f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{0})|\leq\sqrt{\frac{12}{d}|J_{0}|}

Proof.

Suppose without loss of generality that
\theta_{*}\leq\theta
. Then

\displaystyle|f_{\pm}(\theta)-f_{\pm}(\theta_{*})|

\displaystyle=\int_{[\theta_{*},\theta]}|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(t)||{\mathrm{d}}t|.

Consider the set

\displaystyle I_{\pm,<d/2}

\displaystyle:=\{\theta\in I_{\pm}:|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta)|<d/2\}.

As in the previous lemma,
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
is monotone on each connected component of this set; furthermore, there are at most two connected components of
[\theta_{*},\theta]\cap I_{\pm,<d/2}
, and, by monotonicity of
\partial_{\theta}f
on
I_{\pm,<d/2}
, these components must lie at the edges of
[\theta_{*},\theta]
. Thus, we may write

\displaystyle[\theta_{*},\theta]=[\theta_{*},\theta_{1})\cup[\theta_{1},\theta_{2}]\cup(\theta_{2},\theta],

where
[\theta_{*},\theta]\cap I_{\pm,<d/2}=[\theta_{*},\theta_{1})\cup(\theta_{2},\theta]
, and
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}|
is increasing on
[\theta_{*},\theta_{1})
and decreasing on
(\theta_{2},\theta]
.

Since
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}|
is increasing on
[\theta_{*},\theta_{1})
, we have

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(t)|\geq\left|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(t)-\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta_{*})\right|

\displaystyle=\int_{[\theta_{*},t)}|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f_{\pm}(s)||{\mathrm{d}}s|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{2}\left|t-\theta_{*}\right|,\quad t\in[\theta_{*},\theta_{1}).

Similarly, since
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}|
is decreasing on
(\theta_{2},\theta]
,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(t)|\geq\frac{d}{2}\left|t-\theta\right|,\quad t\in(\theta_{2},\theta].

Combining these observations and using that
|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|<1
, we get

\displaystyle|f_{\pm}(\theta)-f_{\pm}(\theta_{*})|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{4}\left(|\theta_{*}-\theta_{1}|^{2}+|\theta_{2}-\theta|^{2}\right)+\frac{d}{2}|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{4}\left(|\theta_{*}-\theta_{1}|^{2}+|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}|^{2}+|\theta_{2}-\theta|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{12}|\theta_{*}-\theta|^{2},

where the last step is a standard inequality following from Cauchy-Schwarz.

Since
f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{0})
are intervals, the upper bound on
|f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{0})|
is immediate. The lower bound follows from the observation that

\displaystyle|J_{0}|

\displaystyle=\int_{f^{-1}_{\pm}(J_{0})}|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(t)||{\mathrm{d}}t|\leq D_{0}|f^{-1}_{\pm}(J_{0})|.\qed

We now turn our attention to the difference of inverse functions
T_{f}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

We have uniform bounds on
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}|
when
\|T_{f}\|_{\mathbb{T}}
is large; specifically, if
E_{*}=f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})\in J
, then

\displaystyle\|T_{f}(E_{*})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{7\nu}{d}\implies|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})|\geq\nu

Proof.

Suppose
|\partial_{\theta}f_{-}(\theta_{-})|<\nu
(the case
|\partial_{\theta}f_{+}(\theta_{+})|<\nu
is analogous). By Lemma
4.1
, there is a critical point
\theta_{c}
of
f
such that for all
\theta\in[\theta_{-},\theta_{c}]
(we assume
\theta_{c}>\theta_{-}
; the reverse case is exactly analogous),
|\partial_{\theta}f_{-}(\theta)|<\nu
. Since
|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f_{-}|\geq d/2
on this interval, by the Mean Value Theorem,

\nu>|\partial_{\theta}f_{-}(\theta)-0|\geq\frac{d}{2}|\theta_{-}-\theta_{c}|,

and so
|\theta_{-}-\theta_{c}|<2\nu/d
; similarly,

|E_{*}-f(\theta_{c})|<\nu|\theta_{-}-\theta_{c}|<\frac{2\nu^{2}}{d}.

Let
J_{0}:=[f(\theta_{c}),E_{*}]
; by Lemma
4.2
,

|f_{+}^{-1}(J_{0})|\leq\sqrt{\frac{12}{d}|J_{0}|}<\frac{5\nu}{d},

and so

\displaystyle\|T_{f}(E_{*})\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\leq|f_{-}^{-1}(J_{0})|+|f_{+}^{-1}(J_{0})|<\frac{2\nu}{d}+\frac{5\nu}{d}=\frac{7\nu}{d}.\qed

Lemma 4.4
.

For all
n
and irrational
\alpha
, there exists at most one value
E_{n}=E(n,\alpha,f)
such that

\displaystyle T_{f}(E_{n})-n\alpha\mod 1=0.

Proof.

First, note the function
T_{f}
is strictly increasing on the interior of
J
, since, for any
E_{*}=f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})
such that
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})\neq 0
, we have

\displaystyle\partial_{E}T_{f}(E_{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\partial_{\theta}f_{+}(\theta_{+})}-\frac{1}{\partial_{\theta}f_{-}(\theta_{-})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\partial_{\theta}f_{+}(\theta_{+})|}+\frac{1}{|\partial_{\theta}f_{+}(\theta_{-})|}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{2}{D_{0}}

by the inverse function theorem. By Lemma
4.1
,
\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}
is nonzero on the interior of
I_{\pm}
, and monotonicity follows on the interior of the interval
J
.

Since
\alpha
is irrational,
n\alpha\mod 1
is distinct for all
n
. Uniqueness of
E_{n}
follows immediately from strict monotonicity of
T_{f}
.
∎

For
\alpha
irrational and
l\in\mathbb{N}
, define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\alpha,f):=\{n\in\mathbb{Z}:n\alpha\mod 1\in T_{f}(J)\}.

Lemma 4.5
.

There exists
\theta\in I_{-}
such that
\theta+n\alpha\in I_{+}
if and only if
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,f)
.

Proof.

One direction is immediate: indeed, if
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,f)
, then there exists
E=E_{n}\in J
such that
T_{f}(E_{n})-n\alpha\mod 1=0
; the relevant
\theta
value is
f_{-}^{-1}(E_{n})
.

Suppose that
\theta\in I_{-}
and
\theta+n\alpha\in I_{+}
. By monotonicity of
T_{f}
,
T_{f}(J)=[\inf I_{+}-\sup I_{-},\sup I_{+}-\inf I_{-}]
. Since

\displaystyle\inf I_{+}-\sup I_{-}<(\theta+n\alpha\mod 1)-\theta<\sup I_{+}-\inf I_{-},

we get
n\alpha\mod 1\in T_{f}(J)
.
∎

4.2
Double resonance

Let
\alpha\in DC_{C,\tau}
and
L^{(1)}\in\mathbb{N}
be fixed satisfying

\frac{C}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}\geq\frac{7\nu}{d},

and denote by

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f):=\{n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,f):\;0<|n|\leq L^{(1)}\}

We can separate the distinct points
E_{n}
,
n\in\mathcal{N}
developed in Lemma
4.4
via the Diophantine condition:

Lemma 4.6
.

For
n\neq m\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
such that
E_{n}
and
E_{m}
are in the interior of
J
, we have

\displaystyle|E_{n}-E_{m}|\geq\frac{C\nu}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}

Proof.

Letting
\|T_{f}(E_{*})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq C/(L^{(1)})^{\tau}
and
\theta_{\pm}
denote the unique points in
I_{\pm}
such that
f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})=E_{*}
, we have by Lemma
4.3
that

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})|\geq\nu.

Provided
E_{*}
is in the interior of
J
, it follows that

\displaystyle\partial_{E}T_{f}(E_{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\partial_{\theta}f_{+}(\theta_{+})|}+\frac{1}{|\partial_{\theta}f_{-}(\theta_{-})|}\leq\frac{2}{\nu}.

By monotonicity of
T_{f}
and the definition of
\|\cdot\|_{\mathbb{T}}
, the set

\displaystyle J_{0}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f):=\left\{E:\|T_{f}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}>0\right\}

is a connected subinterval of
J
. By definition and the Diophantine condition, for
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
we have

\displaystyle\|T_{f}(E_{n})\|_{\mathbb{T}}=\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C}{|n|^{\tau}}\geq\frac{C}{(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}.

Thus, for any
n,m\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
, the interval
(E_{n},E_{m})
must lie inside of
J_{0}
(and, by openness of
(E_{n},E_{m})
, the interior of
J
). The result follows from the mean value theorem applied to
T_{f}
; specifically,

\displaystyle\frac{C}{(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}\leq\|(n-m)\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}

\displaystyle\leq|T_{f}(E_{n})-T_{f}(E_{m})|\leq\frac{2}{\nu}|E_{n}-E_{m}|.\qed

We can also separate these points from any critical values of
f
, if one exists:

Lemma 4.7
.

For
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
and
E_{c}
a critical value of
f
, we have

|E_{n}-E_{c}|\geq\frac{C^{2}d}{48(L^{(1)})^{2\tau}}.

Proof.

Let
\theta_{\pm}
again denote the unique points in
I_{\pm}
such that
f_{\pm}(\theta_{\pm})=E_{n}
. Assume
E_{c}<E_{n}
(the reverse case is analogous), and let
J_{c}=[E_{c},E_{n}]
. By Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle|E_{n}-E_{c}|=|J_{c}|\geq\frac{d}{12}|f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{c})|^{2}.

Since
f_{-}^{-1}(J_{c})\cup f_{+}^{-1}(J_{c})=[\theta_{-},\theta_{+}]
, we have
|f_{-}^{-1}(J_{c})|+|f_{+}^{-1}(J_{c})|=\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}
; thus
|f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{c})|\geq\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}/2
for some choice of sign. Then

\displaystyle|E_{n}-E_{c}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{d}{48}\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{C^{2}d}{24(L^{(1)})^{2\tau}}.\qed

We fix now a length scale
L^{(2)}\gg L^{(1)}
and notions of resonance

\displaystyle\rho\ll\bar{\rho}\ll\widecheck{\rho}<\min\left\{\frac{d}{D^{2}}\left(\frac{C}{24(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}\right)^{2},\frac{C\nu}{12(L^{(1)})^{\tau}},\frac{C\nu}{3(L^{(2)})^{\tau}}\right\}

In order to handle technicalities that arise near the boundaries of the functions we consider, we fix notation for “modified codomains”
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)
of functions
f
satisfying Assumption
3
. Specifically, we denote

\displaystyle\mu_{l}(f)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\rho&\text{ if $f$ attains its minimum at a critical point}\\
-\frac{9}{8}\widecheck{\rho}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mu_{r}(f)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\rho&\text{ if $f$ attains its maximum at a critical point}\\
-\frac{9}{8}\widecheck{\rho}&\text{ otherwise}\end{cases}

and define

\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f):=[\inf J-\mu_{l}(f),\sup J+\mu_{r}(f)].

(4.1)

Define
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(f)
similarly, with
\frac{5}{4}
replacing
\frac{9}{8}
,
and denote

\widetilde{J}:=\{E\in\mathbb{R}:\overline{B}_{\rho}(E)\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)\}\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)\cap J

and
\widetilde{\widetilde{J}}=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(f)\cap J
.

I_{-}
I_{+}
f
J
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)
\sim\widecheck{\rho}
\sim\rho

I_{-}
I_{+}
J
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)
\sim\widecheck{\rho}
\sim\widecheck{\rho}
f_{-}
f_{+}

Figure 6
:
An illustration of two different functions
f
satisfying Assumption
3
alongside their modified codomains
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)
; note how the definition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}
depends on the presence of critical extrema. The intervals
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(f)
are visually analogous.

For
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
, define the double-resonant interval

\displaystyle J_{n}^{\textnormal{DR}}:=J_{n}^{\textnormal{DR}}(\bar{\rho},L^{(1)},\alpha,f):=\overline{B}_{\bar{\rho}}(E_{n}),

and recall the special interval

\displaystyle J_{0}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)=\left\{E:\|T_{f}(E)\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}>0\right\}.

Lemma 4.8
.

For all
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
,
J_{n}^{\textnormal{DR}}\subset J_{0}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
.

Proof.

This is a corollary of monotonicity of
f_{\pm}
and Lemma
4.2
; specifically, if
E_{*}\in J_{n}^{\textnormal{DR}}
, then
|E_{*}-E_{n}|\leq 3\widecheck{\rho}
, and

\displaystyle\|T_{f}(E_{*})-T_{f}(E_{n})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\leq|f_{+}^{-1}((E_{*},E_{n}))|+|f_{-}^{-1}((E_{*},E_{n}))|\leq 12\sqrt{\frac{\widecheck{\rho}}{d}}\leq\frac{C}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}.

In particular,

\displaystyle\|T_{f}(E_{*})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\|T_{f}(E_{n})\|_{\mathbb{T}}-\|T_{f}(E_{*})-T_{f}(E_{n})\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C}{2(L^{(1)})^{\tau}},

which was the claim.
∎

We define the complementary region

\displaystyle J^{SR}:=\widetilde{J}\setminus\bigcup_{n\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}}B_{\bar{\rho}-3\rho}(E_{n}).

We now divide it into simple-resonant intervals of comparable size to the double-resonant intervals:

Proposition 4.9
.

\widetilde{\widetilde{J}}
can be covered by closed intervals
J_{i}^{(j)}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, such that

1.

J_{n}^{(2)}=\overline{B}_{\bar{\rho}}(E_{n})
, where
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
.

2.

\bar{\rho}\leq|J_{i}^{(j)}|\leq 2\bar{\rho}
.

3.

J_{i}\cap J_{i^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\implies|J_{i}\cap J_{i^{\prime}}|=3\rho
.

4.

For any
E\in J_{i}^{(j)}
,
\overline{B}_{\rho}(E)\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(f)
.

5.

The total number of such intervals
J_{i}^{(j)}
does not exceed
5\lceil|J|\bar{\rho}^{-1}\rceil
.

Denoting the corresponding preimages

\displaystyle I_{i,\pm}^{(j)}:=f_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{i}^{(j)}),\quad I_{i}^{(j)}:=I_{i,-}^{(j)}\cup I_{i,+}^{(j)},

each function
f|_{I_{i}^{(j)}}:I_{i}^{(j)}\to J_{i}^{(j)}
satisfies Assumption
3
with Morse constants
d,D
and boundary derivative constant

\widetilde{\nu}:=\min\left\{\nu,\frac{3d}{8D_{0}}\bar{\rho}\right\}.

Proof.

If
[E_{m}+\bar{\rho}-3\rho,E_{n}-\bar{\rho}+3\rho]
is a connected component of
J^{SR}
, then, by Lemma
4.6
, it has length at least

\displaystyle|E_{n}-E_{m}|-2\bar{\rho}+6\rho

\displaystyle\geq C\nu/2((L^{(1)})^{\tau})-2\bar{\rho}+6\rho

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\rho}.

Similarly, if a connected component of
J^{SR}
contains a critical point of
f
, it has length at least

\displaystyle C^{2}d/48((L^{(1)})^{2\tau})-\bar{\rho}+3\rho

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\rho}

by Lemma
4.7
.
We can cover each connected component of at least this size (by the above remarks, this includes all connected components except possibly those at the boundaries of
J^{SR}
where
f
does not attain a critical point) by at most
4\lceil|E_{n}-E_{m}|/\bar{\rho}\rceil
closed intervals of size between
\bar{\rho}
and
2\bar{\rho}
overlapping with only their nearest neighbors by exactly
3\rho
.
We thus construct
a collection of at most
4\lceil|J|/\bar{\rho}\rceil
closed intervals
J_{i}^{(1)}
.
Together with the collection of intervals

\{J_{n}^{(2)}:=J_{n}^{DR}\;:\;n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)\text{ s.t. }J_{n}^{DR}\subset\widetilde{J}\},

we have a total of at most
5\lceil|J|/\bar{\rho}\rceil
intervals
J_{i}^{(j)}
(
j\in\{1,2\}
) satisfying the conditions laid out in the proposition.
Moreover, these intervals cover
\widetilde{J}
, with the possible exception of intervals of length at most
2\bar{\rho}
at the boundaries of
\widetilde{J}
where
f
does not attain a critical point;
since
2\bar{\rho}<\frac{1}{8}\widecheck{\rho}-\rho
, they thus cover
\widetilde{\widetilde{J}}
.

It is clear that restrictions of
f
to
I_{i}^{(j)}
satisfy Assumption
3
; all that remains is to compute the new boundary derivative constant. By Lemma
4.1
, if
|\partial_{\theta}f(\theta_{*})|<\nu
for some
\theta_{*}\in I
,
\theta_{*}
belongs to a connected component
I_{\pm,<\nu}\subset I
containing a critical point
\theta_{c}
and on which
|\partial_{\theta}f|<\nu
. If
\theta_{*}
is a boundary point of some
I_{i}^{(j)}
, then, by construction,

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{c})|

\displaystyle\geq\bar{\rho}-3\rho

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4}\bar{\rho};

and by Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle|\theta_{*}-\theta_{c}|

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3}{4D_{0}}\bar{\rho}.

Since
|\partial_{\theta}f|<\nu<d/2
on
I_{\pm,\nu}
,
|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f|>d_{s}/2
on this component; thus,

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}f(\theta_{*})|

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\theta_{c}}^{\theta_{*}}|\partial_{\theta}^{2}f(\theta)|d\theta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{3d}{8D_{0}}\bar{\rho}.\qed

We denote the two preimages of each crossing point
E_{n}
by

\displaystyle\theta_{n,\pm}

\displaystyle:=f_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{n}),\quad n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f).

Proposition 4.10
.

We have the following:

1.

(Simple resonance): If
\theta_{*}\in I_{i}^{(1)}
, then, for any
0\neq|n|\leq L^{(1)}
such that
\theta_{*}\in I-n\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|>3\rho,

and for all
\theta\in B_{\rho/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\cap I\cap(I-n\alpha)
,

\displaystyle|f(\theta)-f(\theta+n\alpha)|>2\rho.

2.

(Double resonance): If
\theta_{*}=\theta_{n_{0},-}\in I_{n_{0}}^{(2)}
for
n_{0}\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
, then for any
|n|\leq L^{(2)}
,
n\notin\{0,n_{0}\}
such that
\theta_{*}\in I-n\alpha
, we have

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|

\displaystyle>3\widecheck{\rho};

and for all
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\cap I\cap(I-n\alpha)
,

\displaystyle|f(\theta)-f(\theta+n\alpha)|

\displaystyle>2\widecheck{\rho}

and

\displaystyle\min\{-\partial_{\theta}f(\theta),\,\partial_{\theta}f(\theta+n_{0}\alpha)\}\geq\nu.

Proof.

We begin with the simple-resonant case, i.e.
\theta_{*}\in I_{i}^{(1)}
. Suppose
\theta_{*}\in I_{-}
(the case
\theta_{*}\in I_{+}
is completely analogous).
If
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in I_{-}
,
0\neq|n|\leq L^{(1)}
, we can apply Lemma
4.2
to get that

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|\geq\frac{d}{12}\|n\alpha\|^{2}_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{d}{12}\left(\frac{C}{(L^{(1)})^{\tau}}\right)^{2}>3\widecheck{\rho}>3\rho.

We thus suppose that
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in I_{+}
; by Lemma
4.5
,
n\in\mathcal{N}(L^{(1)},\alpha,f)
.

Assume
\theta_{*}\leq\theta_{n,-}
. Then
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\leq\theta_{n,+}=\theta_{n,-}+n\alpha
, and, by the monotonicity of
f
on
I_{\pm}
,
f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)\leq E_{n}
. Thus,

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|

\displaystyle=f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)

\displaystyle\geq f(\theta_{*})-E_{n}>\bar{\rho}-3\rho>3\rho.

If
\theta_{*}\geq\theta_{n,-}
, then similarly
f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)\geq E_{n}
, and

\displaystyle|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|

\displaystyle=f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)-f(\theta_{*})

\displaystyle\geq E_{n}-f(\theta_{*})>3\rho.

The claim for
\theta\in B_{\rho/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\cap I\cap(I-n\alpha)
follows from the Mean Value Theorem and the uniform bound
|\partial_{\theta}f|\leq D_{0}
. This concludes the simple resonant case.

We now consider the case
\theta_{*}=\theta_{n_{0},-}
.
Suppose
n
with
0\neq|n|\leq L^{(2)}
satisfies
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in I_{-}
and
|f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)-f(\theta_{*})|\leq 3\widecheck{\rho}
. Then by Lemma
4.8
,

\displaystyle 3\widecheck{\rho}\geq|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|\geq\nu\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C\nu}{(L^{(2)})^{\tau}},

a contradiction. Thus, for any
0\neq|n|\leq L^{(2)}
such that
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in I_{-}
,
|f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)-f(\theta_{*})|>3\widecheck{\rho}
.

If instead
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in I_{+}
,
|n|\leq L^{(2)}
and
n\neq n_{0}
, and suppose
|f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)-f(\theta_{*})|\leq 3\widecheck{\rho}
. Then
\theta_{*}+n_{0}\alpha\in I_{+}
and

\displaystyle 3\widecheck{\rho}\geq|f(\theta_{*})-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|

\displaystyle=|f(\theta_{*}+n_{0}\alpha)-f(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|\geq\nu\|n\alpha\|_{\mathbb{T}}\geq\frac{C\nu}{(L^{(2)})^{\tau}},

a contradiction as above. Again, the claim for
\theta\in B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\cap I\cap(I-n\alpha)
follows from the Mean Value Theorem and the uniform bound
|\partial_{\theta}f|\leq D_{0}
. The lower bound for the derivatives follows from Lemmas
4.3
and
4.8
, and that
B_{\widecheck{\rho}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})\cap I\subset I^{\textnormal{DR}}_{n_{0},-}
.
∎

4.3
Domain adjustment

We now show that a function
g
well-approximating a function
f
satisfying Assumption
3
can have its domain slightly modified to satisfy Assumption
3
.

Let
I_{\pm}\subset\mathbb{T}
be two closed intervals with disjoint interiors, let
I:=I^{f}_{-}\cup I^{f}_{+}
, and consider a piecewise
C^{1}
function

\displaystyle f:I\to J,\quad f_{\pm}:=f|_{I^{f}_{\pm}}

such that each function
f_{\pm}
maps onto
J
.

Let
\delta>0
satisfy
16\delta<|J|
and
\frac{16\delta}{\nu}<|I_{\pm}|
. Our first lemma handles the interval adjustment in the absence of critical points for
f
:

Lemma 4.11
.

Suppose
\pm\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}\geq\nu>0
uniformly on
I_{\pm}
, and suppose
g\in C^{2}(I,\mathbb{R})
satisfies the following stability conditions:

1.

The function
g
is Morse on
I

\displaystyle d\leq|\partial_{\theta}g|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}g|\leq D

with
I_{\pm}^{g}:=\{\theta\in I:\pm\partial_{\theta}g\geq 0\}
two closed intervals with disjoint interiors.

2.

There is a constant
0<\tilde{\nu}<d/2
such that
|\partial_{\theta}g|\geq\tilde{\nu}
on the boundary points of
I
.

3.

If
\theta\in I
is such that
g(\theta)\notin g(I^{g}_{-})\cap g(I^{g}_{+})
, or if
\theta
is a boundary point of
I
, then

\displaystyle|f(\theta)-g(\theta)|\leq 2\delta.

Then there exists a subset
\tilde{I}^{g}=\tilde{I}_{-}^{g}\cup\tilde{I}_{+}^{g}\subset I
,
\tilde{I}_{\pm}^{g}
being closed intervals with disjoint interiors, such that

\displaystyle|I\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}|\leq\frac{32\delta}{\nu}

on which
g
satisfies Assumption
3
.

Proof.

Let
J^{g}_{r}:=\min\{\sup g(I_{g}^{+}),\sup g(I_{g}^{-})\}

and
J^{g}_{l}:=\max\{\inf g(I_{g}^{+}),\inf g(I_{g}^{-})\}
.

We first show that
J^{g}_{r}>J^{g}_{l}
.
Note that
\max\{g(\sup I^{g}_{+}),g(\inf I^{g}_{-})\}=\sup g
; likewise,
\min\{g(\inf I^{g}_{+}),g(\sup I^{g}_{-})\}=\inf g
.
Define
g_{\pm}:=g|_{I^{g}_{\pm}}
; note that we have either
g_{+}^{-1}(J^{g}_{r})=\sup I^{g}_{+}
or
g_{-}^{-1}(J_{g}^{r})=\inf I^{g}_{-}
.
Either
\inf I^{g}_{-}=\sup I^{g}_{+}
is a critical point of
g
, in which case
J^{g}_{r}=\sup g
, or both
\inf I^{g}_{-}
and
\sup I^{g}_{+}
are (distinct) boundary points of
I
, in which case
f(\inf I^{g}_{-})=f(\sup I^{g}_{+})\in\{\inf J,\sup J\}
, and thus
|f(\inf I^{g}_{-})-J^{g}_{r}|,|f(\inf I^{g}_{-})-\sup g|\leq 2\delta
. Thus in either case we have
|J^{g}_{r}-\sup g|\leq 4\delta
, and it follows that

|J^{g}_{r}-\sup J|\leq 6\delta.

(4.2)

By analogous reasoning,

|J^{g}_{l}-\inf J|\leq 6\delta.

(4.3)

Since, by assumption,
|J|>12\delta
, we must have
J^{g}_{r}>J^{g}_{l}
.

We define
J^{g}:=[J^{g}_{l},J^{g}_{r}]
and
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}:=g_{\pm}^{-1}(J^{g})
(so
\tilde{I}^{g}=g^{-1}(J^{g})
). Since we have shown
J^{g}_{r}>J^{g}_{l}
,
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
are nonempty intervals, and
g_{\pm}
maps
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
onto
J^{g}
.
We now measure the set

I\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}

by considering the sets
I_{\pm}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
.
We first note that by applying the Mean Value Theorem to
f
on
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
, we conclude that
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}\cap I_{\pm}\neq\emptyset
. Thus each of the sets
I_{\pm}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
consists of at most two intervals
[\inf I_{\pm},\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm})
and
(\sup\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm},\sup I_{\pm}]
.
Consider first
[\inf I_{-},\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})
.Such an interval belongs to
I_{-}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}
if and only if
\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}\in I_{-}
.
If
J^{g}_{r}
is not a critical point of
g
, we have
|f(\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})-J^{g}_{r}|=|(f-g)\circ g_{-}^{-1}(J^{g}_{r})|\leq 2\delta
; combining this with (
4.2
) gives
|f(\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})-\sup J|\leq 8\delta
. Thus

\displaystyle|[\inf I_{-},\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})|

\displaystyle=|\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}-\inf I_{-}|

\displaystyle=|f_{-}^{-1}(f(\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}))-f_{-}^{-1}(\sup J)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\nu}|f(\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})-\sup J|\leq\frac{8\delta}{\nu}.

Analogously,
|(\sup\tilde{I}^{g}_{+},\sup I_{+}]|\leq\frac{8\delta}{\nu}
if
J^{g}_{r}
is not a critical point of
g
, and
|(\sup\tilde{I}^{g}_{-},\sup I_{-}]|\leq\frac{8\delta}{\nu}
,
|[\inf I_{+},\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{+})|\leq\frac{8\delta}{\nu}
if
J^{g}_{l}
is not a critical point of
g
.

If
g
contains no critical points, we conclude that
|I_{\pm}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}|\leq\frac{16\delta}{\nu}
, and
|I\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}|\leq|I_{-}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}|+|I_{+}\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}_{+}|\leq\frac{32\delta}{\nu}
.

If
g
attains its minimum, but not its maximum, at a critical point, then
\tilde{I}^{g}=[\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-},\sup\tilde{I}^{g}_{+}]
, and likewise
I=[\inf I_{-},\sup I_{+}]
; thus,

\displaystyle|I\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}|

\displaystyle\leq|[\inf I_{-},\inf\tilde{I}^{g}_{-})|+|(\sup\tilde{I}^{g}_{+},\sup I_{+}]|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\delta}{\nu}

by the above computation. The argument is analogous if
g
attains its maximum, but not its minimum, at a critical point. If
g
attains both its maximum and its minimum at critical points, then trivially
\tilde{I}^{g}=I
.

It remains to verify a lower bound for
|\partial_{\theta}g|
on the boundary points of
\tilde{I}^{g}
.
We first note that we can apply Lemma
4.1
to the unrestricted function
g
on
I
, since the condition that
g_{\pm}
map onto the same image is not used in Lemma
4.1
.
Thus, if
|\partial_{\theta}g|<\tilde{\nu}
on a boundary point of
\tilde{I}^{g}
(which is also a boundary point of
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm})
, it must belong to a connected component of
I^{g}_{\pm}
containing a critical point of
g
(which is a boundary point of
I^{g}_{\pm}
), and
|\partial_{\theta}^{2}g|\geq d/2
throughout that component. By construction, the critical point belongs to
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
, and is thus the other boundary point of that interval; thus
|\partial_{\theta}^{2}g|\geq d/2
on the entirety of
\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}
. Then, on the boundary point of
\tilde{I}^{g}
,

|\partial_{\theta}g|\geq\frac{d}{2}|\tilde{I}^{g}_{\pm}|\geq\frac{d}{2}\left(|I_{\pm}|-\frac{16\delta}{\nu}\right),

so we conclude
|\partial_{\theta}g|\geq\min\left\{\tilde{\nu},\frac{d}{2}\left(|I_{\pm}|-\frac{16\delta}{\nu}\right)\right\}
on the boundary of
\tilde{I}^{g}
.
∎

If
f:I\to J
satisfies Assumption
3
and
g:I\to\mathbb{R}
is
C^{2}
close to
f
, we likewise can find these adjusted intervals:

Lemma 4.12
.

Suppose
g\in C^{2}(I,\mathbb{R})
is
C^{2}
close to
f
, i.e.

\displaystyle\|\partial_{\theta}^{k}(f-g)\|\leq C\frac{\delta}{\rho^{k}},\quad 0\leq k\leq 2.

Then there exists a subset
\tilde{I}^{g}=\tilde{I}^{g}_{-}\cup\tilde{I}^{g}_{+}\subset I
,
\tilde{I}_{\pm}^{g}
being closed intervals with disjoint interiors, such that

\displaystyle|I\setminus\tilde{I}^{g}|\leq\frac{32\delta}{\nu}

and on which
g
satisfies Assumption 3.

Proof.

By the well-approximation of
g
to
f
, we immediately get that
g
is Morse on
I
, e.g.

\displaystyle\tilde{d}=d-C\left(\frac{\delta}{\rho}+\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}\right)<|\partial_{\theta}g|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}g|\leq D+C\left(\frac{\delta}{\rho}+\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}\right)=\tilde{D}.

Furthermore, on the boundary points of
I
, one has

\displaystyle|\partial_{\theta}g|\geq\nu-C\frac{\delta}{\rho}>\nu-2C\left(\frac{\delta}{\rho}+\frac{\delta}{\rho^{2}}\right)=:\tilde{\nu}

and
\tilde{\nu}<\tilde{d}/2
. By the Morse condition on
g
and the assumption on
f
, we can decompose
I=I^{g}_{-}\cup I^{g}_{+}
into intervals such that

\displaystyle\pm\partial_{\theta}g|_{I^{g}_{\pm}}\geq 0.

We denote by
g_{\pm}:=g|_{I^{g}_{\pm}}
. By Lemma
4.1
, critical points of
g_{\pm}
must lie on the boundary of
I^{g}_{\pm}
and each connected component of
I^{g}_{\pm,<\tilde{\nu}}
must contain a critical point, and, conversely, any critical point of
g
must lie in a connected component of
I^{g}_{\pm,<\tilde{\nu}}
.

If
g
has no critical point in
I
, then neither can
f
, and we are in the situation outlined in Lemma
4.11
(with
\nu
replaced by
\nu/4
, e.g.). If
g
has a critical point
\theta_{c}
, then by the Morse condition it is an extremum of
g
. Suppose the critical point is a minimum; then, defining
J^{g}
as in the proof of Lemma
4.11
, we have

\displaystyle g_{+}^{-1}(J^{g})

\displaystyle=:\tilde{I}_{+}^{g}=[\theta_{c},\tilde{\theta}_{+}],

\displaystyle g_{-}^{-1}(J^{g})

\displaystyle=:\tilde{I}_{-}^{g}=[\tilde{\theta}_{-},\theta_{c}].

By monotonicity of
g_{\pm}
, it must be that
\tilde{I}_{\pm}^{g}=I_{\pm}^{g}
for at least one of
+
or
-
; suppose
\tilde{I}_{+}^{g}=I_{+}^{g}
. Since
\sup f_{+}=\sup f_{-}
and
\|f-g\|\leq C\delta
, we get that
|\sup g_{+}-\sup g_{-}|=|g(\tilde{\theta}_{+})-J_{r}^{g}|\leq C\delta
.

To measure
|I_{-}\setminus\tilde{I}_{-}^{g}|
, it suffices to compute
|\tilde{\theta}_{-}-\theta_{-}|
, where
\theta_{\pm}=\inf I_{\pm}^{g}
. We have

\displaystyle|g(\theta_{-})-g(\tilde{\theta}_{-})|=|g(\theta_{-})-f(\theta_{+})|\leq C\delta+|f(\theta_{-})-f(\theta_{+})|=C\delta;

furthermore,
\theta_{-}
and
\tilde{\theta}_{-}
live on the same monotonicity interval of
g
, and so

\displaystyle|\theta_{-}-\tilde{\theta}_{-}|\leq\sqrt{\frac{12C\delta}{\tilde{d}}}.

Since our only critical point is a minimum, it must be that
\theta_{-}=\inf I_{-}^{f}
, and thus for any
\theta\in[\theta_{-},\tilde{\theta}_{-}]
, we have

\displaystyle|g^{\prime}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\geq|f^{\prime}(\theta)|-\frac{C\delta}{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq\nu-\left(\frac{C\delta}{\rho}+D\sqrt{\frac{12C\delta}{\tilde{d}}}\right)\geq\tilde{\nu}.\qed

We conclude by estimating the difference of inverses of close functions satisfying Assumption
3
.

Lemma 4.13
.

Let
f
be a function satisfying Assumption
3
with boundary derivative constant
\nu
and containing no critical point.
Let
g
be another function satisfing Assumption
3
with
\dom g_{\pm}\subset\dom f_{\pm}
and
\|f-g\|_{\infty}\leq 2\delta
.
Then for
E_{*}\in\Ima f\cap\Ima g
,
|T_{f}(E_{*})-T_{g}(E_{*})|\leq\frac{4\delta}{\nu}
.

Proof.

Since
f
contains no critical point, by Lemma
4.1
,
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}(\theta)|\geq\nu
for all
\theta\in I
, and thus
|\partial_{\theta}f_{\pm}^{-1}(E)|\geq\nu^{-1}
for all
E\in J
. Then

\displaystyle|f_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})-g_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})|

\displaystyle=|f_{\pm}^{-1}\circ g\circ g_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})-f_{\pm}^{-1}\circ f\circ g_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})|

\displaystyle\leq\nu^{-1}|g\circ g_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})-f\circ g_{\pm}^{-1}(E_{*})|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta\nu^{-1}.

Thus,

\displaystyle|T_{f}(E_{*})-T_{g}(E_{*})|\leq|f_{+}^{-1}(E_{*})-g_{+}^{-1}(E_{*})|+|f_{-}^{-1}(E_{*})-g_{-}^{-1}(E_{*})|

\displaystyle\leq 4\delta\nu^{-1}.\qed

5
Multiscale spectral analysis

We wish to understand how the Rellich functions of
H^{\Lambda}
inherit the cosine-like properties from the sampling function
v
. Given
(\theta_{*},E_{*})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, we claim that, by properly choosing our notions of resonance, we can inductively construct increasing intervals
\Lambda_{s}=\Lambda_{s}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
in the integer lattice, centered at 0, such that any
E_{*}
-resonant Rellich functions of
H^{\Lambda}(\theta_{*})
are locally cosine-like with eigenfunctions localized near 0. Furthermore, these intervals will be stable in the parameters
\theta_{*}
and
E_{*}
.

In broad strokes, the induction will proceed as follows: we suppose that we inductively have been given a cosine-like Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{s}:\mathbf{I}_{s}\to\mathbf{J}_{s}
and that certain “nonresonant” intervals for
\mathbf{E}_{s}
exhibit off-diagonal Green’s function decay for energies
E_{*}
near the codomain of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
. Under these assumptions, we apply Section 4 to
\mathbf{E}_{s}
to classify energy regions
J_{s}^{(j_{s+1})}\subset\mathbf{J}_{s}
as being simple- or double-resonant (indicated by
j_{s+1}\in\{1,2\}
) and prevent recurrence to those energy regions for long times. The resonant sites for
\mathbf{E}_{s}
will be so well-separated that we can find an even integer
L_{s+1}
for each energy region well-separated from resonances of all the ancestors of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
. Thus, the intervals
\Lambda_{s+1}
of length
L_{s+1}
will have long shoulder intervals with Green’s function decay by our inductive assumption.

Having found the integers
L_{s+1}
, we can then apply Sections 2 and 3 to construct Rellich children
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
which are likewise cosine-like. Finally, for energies which are not resonant with any child
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
for long intervals
\Lambda
, we can use these constructed intervals to build coverings of
\Lambda
like those described in Appendix A, proving the inductive nonresonance hypothesis.
Here we crucially use the uniform local separation between double-resonant Rellich children established in Proposition
3.6
; since the separation is much larger than the scale-
s+1
resonance parameter, energies near the codomain of one of a pair of double-resonant Rellich functions do not allow resonance with the other.

The perturbative upper bound
\varepsilon_{0}(v,\alpha)
on the interaction
\varepsilon
will be polynomially small, depending on
v
and
\alpha
, in the initial length scale
L
; the size of
L
effectively will be dictated by avoiding substantial cumulative loss in the Green’s function decay parameters
\gamma_{k}
, cf. Lemma
5.2
below.

\mathbf{E}_{s}
\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}
n_{s}\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
\cdots
L_{s+1}\sim M_{s+1}^{(1)}
L_{s+1}\sim M_{s+1}^{(2)}

Figure 7
:
The inductive expansion of our Rellich tree at a particular node
\mathbf{E}_{s}
.

5.1
Preparing the induction

5.1.1
The initial scale

Suppose
\mathbf{E}_{0}:=v
,
v:\mathbb{T}\to[-1,1]
, is cosine-like in the sense of the main theorem, with Morse constants

d_{0}\leq|\partial_{\theta}v(\theta)|+|\partial_{\theta}^{2}v(\theta)|\leq D_{0},\quad\theta\in\mathbb{T},

and that
\alpha\in DC_{C,\tau}
is Diophantine. Letting
L
be a large even integer, we consider the initial length scales

L_{1}^{(1)}:=L,\quad L_{1}^{(2)}:=L^{2},

and initialize the following parameters:

\nu_{0}=\frac{C_{\alpha}d_{0}}{14(8L)^{\tau}},\quad\rho_{-1}=\frac{d_{0}}{24D_{0}^{2}}\frac{1}{(8L)^{2\tau}},\quad\bar{\rho}_{0}=\frac{d_{0}}{15000D_{0}^{2}}\rho_{-1}^{2},

\rho_{0}=\bar{\rho}_{0}^{3},\quad\delta_{0}:=\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}:=\rho_{0}^{4},\quad\gamma_{-1}=\gamma_{0}=\frac{1}{4}|\log\varepsilon|,\quad\ell_{-1}=\ell_{0}=1.

With these parameters, we recall a classical Neumann series argument ensuring off-diagonal Green’s function decay on nonresonant intervals:

Lemma 5.1
.

Let
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
and suppose
|v(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{0}
for
m\in\Lambda
. Then for
m,n\in\Lambda
,
|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho_{0}/8D_{0}
, and
|E-E_{*}|<\rho_{0}/2
,

\displaystyle|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq\frac{8}{\rho_{0}}\left(\frac{8\varepsilon}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{|m-n|}.

In particular, for
\gamma_{0}=\frac{1}{4}|\log\varepsilon|
and
|m-n|\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\log|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{0}|m-n|,

(5.1)

i.e. the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{0},\gamma_{0})
holds.

Proof.

For
m\in\Lambda
,
|\theta-\theta_{*}|<\rho_{0}/8D_{0}
, and
|E-E_{*}|<\rho_{0}/2
, one has

\displaystyle|v(\theta+m\alpha)-E|

\displaystyle\geq|v(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|-|E-E_{*}|-D_{0}|\theta-\theta_{*}|>\rho_{0}/4.

Thus
|v(\theta+m\alpha)-E|\geq\rho_{0}/4>4\varepsilon
for all
m\in\Lambda
, and
\|(V(\theta)-E)^{-1}\varepsilon\Delta\|_{\mathbb{C}^{\Lambda}}<1/2
; hence, by the Neumann series, one has for
m,n\in\Lambda

\displaystyle|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|

\displaystyle=|\langle\delta_{m},(H^{\Lambda}(\theta)-E)^{-1}\delta_{n}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{\rho_{0}}\sum_{k\geq|m-n|}\left(\frac{8\varepsilon}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{k}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\rho_{0}}\left(\frac{8\varepsilon}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{|m-n|}.

Provided
|m-n|\geq 1
, we have

\displaystyle|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq\frac{8}{\rho_{0}}\left(\frac{8\varepsilon}{\rho_{0}}\right)^{|m-n|}

\displaystyle\leq\rho_{0}^{2|m-n|-1}\leq\rho_{0}^{|m-n|}.\qed

This lemma, combined with our assumptions on
v
, will be the foundation of our induction.

5.1.2
Inductive definitions

Fix a scale
s\geq 0
. If
s\geq 1
, we suppose that, for
0\leq k\leq s-1
, we have collections

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}^{(j)}

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k-1}}\mathcal{E}^{(j)}(\mathbf{E}),\quad j\in\{1,2\},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{E}_{k}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{E}_{k}^{(2)}

of Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{k}:\mathbf{I}_{k}\to\mathbf{J}_{k}
of certain Dirichlet restrictions
H^{\Lambda_{k}}
,
\Lambda_{k}=[-L_{k}/2,L_{k}/2]
, where
L_{k}=L_{k}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
are even integers on scale
k

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M_{k}^{(j)}\leq L_{k}\leq M_{k}^{(j)},\quad\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}^{(j)},

where we define the length scales

M_{k}^{(j)}:=(L^{4^{k-1}})^{j},\quad k\geq 1,\;j\in\{1,2\}.

We take as convention that
M_{0}^{(1)}=M_{0}^{(2)}=1
.
For each
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
,
k\geq 1
, we define the corresponding parameters:

d_{k}=\frac{\nu_{k-1}}{12}
,
D_{k}=2D_{0}(1+\frac{D_{0}}{\sigma_{k}})
, (Morse)

\nu_{k}=\bar{\nu}_{k-1}/2
,
\bar{\nu}_{k}:=\frac{3d_{k}}{8D_{0}}\bar{\rho}_{k}
(Derivative control)

\rho_{k}=\varepsilon^{L_{k}^{2/3}}
,
\bar{\rho}_{k}=\frac{d_{k}}{15000D_{0}^{2}}\rho_{k-1}^{2}
,
\sigma_{k}=\varepsilon^{72M_{k}^{(1)}}
(Resonance)

\delta_{k}=\varepsilon^{L_{k}/8}
(Eigenfunction interaction)

\ell_{k}=L_{k}^{5/6}
,
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{0}(1-64\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{|\log(\rho_{i}/8)|}{\ell_{i}})
(Green’s function decay)

We likewise define
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
and
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
as in equation (
4.1
) with
\rho=\rho_{k}
and
\widecheck{\rho}=\rho_{k-1}
, cf. Figure
6
. As matters of convention, we define
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{-1})=\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{-1})=\mathbb{R}
and
\mathcal{E}_{0}=\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{-1}):=\{v\}
.

We have the following relations among these parameters:

Lemma 5.2
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
with
L_{k}
and constants defined as above. For
L
sufficiently large (depending only on
C_{\alpha}
,
\tau
,
d_{0}
, and
D_{0}
), we have the following for all
k\geq 0
:

1.

\gamma_{k}\geq\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}\geq\log 7
.

2.

10M_{k}^{(2)}<M_{k+1}^{(1)}
,
24L_{k+1}^{2/3}<8L_{k+1}^{5/6}<L_{k+1}
,
\ell_{k+1}\gg 16|\log\varepsilon||\log\rho_{k+1}|
.

3.

\frac{C_{\alpha}}{2(8M_{k+1}^{(1)})}\geq\frac{7\nu_{k}}{d_{k}}
,
\frac{3d_{k}}{8D_{0}}\bar{\rho}_{k}<\nu_{k}
, and
\frac{9\rho_{k}}{8\nu_{k}}\ll\bar{\rho}_{k}\ll\frac{\nu_{k}^{2}\rho_{k-1}}{100D_{0}^{3}}
.

4.

\delta_{k}<\rho_{k}^{3}/2\ll\rho_{k-1}^{2}\nu_{k}^{5/2}
.

5.

If
j_{k}=2
, then
2\rho_{k}<\frac{\nu_{k-1}\sigma_{k}}{2D_{0}+\nu_{k-1}}
.

Proof.

We proceed with each item in turn:

1.

For
1\leq i\leq k
, we have by definition that

\displaystyle\frac{|\log(\rho_{i}/8)|}{\ell_{i}}

\displaystyle\leq L_{i}^{-1/6}|\log\varepsilon|\lesssim\tau L^{-\frac{1}{6}4^{i-1}}\log L\lesssim\tau L^{-\frac{1}{7}(4^{i-1})}

Since this sequence is subgeometric, we may find
L
sufficiently large such that

\displaystyle 64\sum_{i=1}^{k}\frac{|\log(\rho_{i}/8)|}{\ell_{i}}\leq\frac{1}{2}

independently of
k
. By possibly making
L
larger, one can insist that

\frac{1}{2}\gamma_{0}=\frac{1}{8}\log|\varepsilon|\sim\tau\log|L|>\log 7.

2.

This is immediate from the fact that, for
k\geq 1
,

M_{k+1}^{(1)}\geq LM_{k}^{(2)},

alongside the observation that
|\log\varepsilon|
is comparable to
\log L
.

3.

One can check by definitions that, for
L
sufficiently large (depending on
\tau
,
d_{0}
, and
D_{0}
), one has for
k\geq 0
that:

\displaystyle\nu_{k}^{5/4}<d_{k+1}<\nu_{k}<\frac{d_{k}}{2}

\displaystyle\rho_{k-1}^{9/4}<\bar{\rho}_{k}<\rho_{k-1}^{2}

\displaystyle\rho_{k-1}^{9/4}<\nu_{k+1},\bar{\nu}_{k}<\rho_{k-1}^{2}

\displaystyle\rho_{k}\ll\rho_{k-1}^{6}.

The inequalities follow for sufficiently large
L
.

4.

Given the above inequalities, one has

\rho_{k-1}^{2}\nu_{k}^{5/2}\gg\rho_{k-1}^{2}\rho_{k-2}^{45/8}\gg\rho_{k-1}^{2}\rho_{k-2}^{6}\gg\rho_{k-1}^{3}\gg\rho_{k}^{3}/2,

and
L_{k}>24L_{k}^{2/3}
.

5.

Since
j_{k}=2
, we have
L_{k}^{2/3}\geq(M_{k}^{(2)}/2)^{2/3}\geq\frac{1}{2}(M_{k}^{(1)})^{4/3}\gg 144M_{k}^{(1)}
provided
L
is sufficiently large,
and thus
\log\rho_{k}\ll 2\log\sigma_{k}
. The inequality follows because
\log\nu_{k-1}\gg\log\rho_{k-1}\gg\log\sigma_{k}
.
∎

It will be convenient to fix language describing “nonresonant” regions at each scale. Fix
(\theta_{*},E_{*})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
and a Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. We say that a set
S\subset\mathbb{Z}
is
k
-nonresonant
(relative to
(\theta_{*},E_{*})
and
\mathbf{E}_{k}
) if

\theta_{*}+m\alpha\notin\mathbf{I}_{k}\,\text{ or }\,|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{k}\quad\forall m\in S.

Note that, with this language, Lemma
5.1
shows that
0
-nonresonant intervals have
(\ell_{0},\gamma_{0})
Green’s function decay.

To show Green’s function decay at future scales
k\geq 1
, we must avoid resonant sites for all ancestors of
\mathbf{E}_{k}
; we codify this condition as
k
-regularity. Namely, we say that a point
m\in\mathbb{Z}
is
k
-left-regular
if, for each ancestor
\mathbf{E}_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq k-1
) of
\mathbf{E}_{k}
,

[m,m+\frac{3}{4}L_{i+1}]
is
i
-nonresonant. Analogously, we say that
m
is
k
-right-regular
if, for each ancestor
\mathbf{E}_{i}
(
0\leq i\leq k-1
) of
\mathbf{E}_{k}
,

[m-\frac{3}{4}L_{i+1},m]
is
i
-nonresonant, and we say that
m
is
k
-regular
if it is both
k
-left-regular and
k
-right-regular.

Finally, we define the slight enlargement of the set of
k
-resonant points by

\mathcal{S}_{k}(\theta_{*},E_{*}):=\left\{m\in\mathbb{Z}:\theta_{*}+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k},\;|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|\leq\frac{25}{24}\rho_{k}\right\}.

5.2
The inductive proposition

Subject to these definitions, we suppose that the following inductive proposition holds for
0\leq k\leq s-1
:

Proposition 5.3

(Induction, scale
k
)
.

Suppose the hypotheses below hold:

H 5.3.1

(Cosine-like Rellich functions, scale
k
)
.

Each
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
satisfies Assumption
3
with parameters
\nu=\nu_{k},d=d_{k},
and
D=D_{k}
.

H 5.3.2

(Resonant orbits, scale
k
)
.

If
k\geq 1
, let
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}^{(j_{k})}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})
,
\mathbf{E}_{k}:\mathbf{I}_{k}\to\mathbf{J}_{k}
, denote

\mathcal{N}_{k}:=\mathcal{N}(8M_{k+1}^{(1)},\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k}),

and let
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
. We can characterize aspects of the resonant orbits depending on the resonance type
j_{k}
:

1.

j_{k}=1
:

We have

\min\{|n|:n\in\mathcal{N}_{k}\}>8M_{k}^{(1)}>L_{k}.

Furthermore, suppose either
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
or the closest point in
\mathbf{J}_{k}
to
E_{*}
is a critical value of
\mathbf{E}_{k}
. Then, for any
m\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
,

B_{\rho_{k-1}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{k}.

2.

j_{k}=2

(and thus
\mathbf{E}_{k}=\mathbf{E}_{k,n_{k-1},\bullet}^{(2)}
)
:

We have

\mathcal{N}_{k}=\emptyset.

Furthermore, suppose
E_{*}\in[\inf\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k,n_{k-1},\wedge}^{(2)}),\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k,n_{k-1},\vee}^{(2)})].
Then, for any
m\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
, for some
p\in\{m,m-n_{k-1}\}
,

B_{\rho_{k-1}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\vee}\cup\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\wedge}.

If
\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\wedge}^{(2)}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\vee}^{(2)}
, then
|\mathbf{E}_{k,n_{k-1},\wedge}^{(2)}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|<\frac{9}{8}\rho_{k-1}
; similarly, if
\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\vee}^{(2)}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{k,n_{k-1},\wedge}^{(2)}
, then
|\mathbf{E}_{k,n_{k-1},\vee}^{(2)}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|<\frac{9}{8}\rho_{k-1}
.

H 5.3.3

(Nonresonance, scale
k
)
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{k-1}\in\mathcal{E}_{k-1}
, let
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
, and suppose
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})
is such that

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{k-1}
does not attain its maximum at a critical point, then
E_{*}\leq\sup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
.

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{k-1}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point, then
E_{*}\geq\inf\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})
be a Rellich curve minimizing
\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}))
among
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k-1})
.
Suppose
\Lambda=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}
is
k
-nonresonant and that
a
and
b
are
k
-left- and
k
-right-regular, respectively. Then for
|E-E_{*}|<\rho_{k}/2
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{k}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\displaystyle\log|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{k}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{k},

and

\displaystyle\|R^{\Lambda}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho^{-1}_{k}.

Furthermore, if
\widetilde{\Lambda}
is
k
-nonresonant with

|\widetilde{\Lambda}|\geq 2L_{k}+3M_{k}^{(1)}
, then
there exists a
k
-nonresonant subinterval
\Lambda=[a,b]\subset\widetilde{\Lambda}
with
|\Lambda|\geq|\widetilde{\Lambda}|-(2L_{k}+3M_{k}^{(1)})
such that
a
and
b
are
k
-left- and
k
-right-regular, respectively.

Subject to the above hypotheses, we have the following: Let
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
with
\mathbf{E}_{k}:\mathbf{I}_{k}\to\mathbf{J}_{k}
. There exists a collection
\mathcal{I}_{k+1}(\mathbf{E}_{k})=\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{(1)}\cup\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{(2)}
of intervals
I_{k+1}
and corresponding even integers
L_{k+1}
so that, denoting
\Lambda_{k+1}=[-L_{k+1}/2,L_{k+1}/2]
, the following hold:

(Simple resonance): If
I_{k+1}\in\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{(1)}
,
\theta_{*}\in I_{k+1}
, and
E_{*}=\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*})
, then
H^{\Lambda_{k+1}}(\theta_{*})
satisfies Assumption
1
with

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{k+1},\;{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{k},

\displaystyle\rho=\rho_{k},\;\delta=\delta_{k},\;\gamma=\gamma_{k},\;\ell=\ell_{k}.

(Double resonance): If
I_{k+1}\in\mathcal{I}_{k+1}^{(2)}
, there exists a unique
n
with
L_{k}<|n|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(1)}
and a unique point
\theta_{**}=\theta_{k,n,-}\in I_{k+1}
such that
E_{**}=\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{**})=\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{**}+n\alpha)
. Furthermore,
H^{\Lambda_{k+1}}(\theta_{**})
satisfies Assumption
2
with the following assignments:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{k+1},\;{\Lambda}_{-}=\Lambda_{k},\;{\Lambda}_{+}=\Lambda_{k}+n,

\displaystyle\widecheck{\rho}=\frac{2}{3}\rho_{k-1},\;\delta=\delta_{k},\;\gamma=\gamma_{k},\;\ell=\ell_{k},\;\nu=\nu_{k}.

Moreover, we can choose the additional parameters introduced in Section
3
as follows:

\displaystyle\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{k+1},\;\eta=\frac{5\rho_{k}}{4\nu_{k}},\;\rho=\rho_{k}.

In particular, there exists a family
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})=\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\cup\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
of Rellich children
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}:\mathbf{I}_{k+1}\to\mathbf{J}_{k+1}
such that each
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}
is a Rellich function of
H^{\Lambda_{k+1}}
satisfying Assumption
3
, such that Hypotheses
5.3.1
,
5.3.2
, and
5.3.3
hold at scale
k+1
.

Additionally, we have the following:

1.

The even integers
L_{k+1}
can be chosen such that
\pm L_{k+1}/2
and
\pm(L_{k+1}/2+1)
are
k
-regular for any
\theta_{*}\in\mathbf{I}_{k+1}
for any
E_{*}\in B_{\rho_{k}}(\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*}))
, and such that
L_{k+1}/2+1
(respectively,
-(L_{k+1}/2+1)
) is
k+1
-left-regular (respectively,
k+1
-right-regular) for any
E_{*}\in B_{\rho_{k}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1}(\theta_{*}))

2.

The intervals
\mathbf{I}_{k+1}
and their relevant translates cover
\mathbf{I}_{k}
, in the sense that if
\theta\in\mathbf{I}_{k}
and
\theta\notin\mathbf{I}_{k+1}
for all
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, then there exists a unique
n
with
8M_{k}^{(1)}<|n|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(1)}
and a Rellich curve
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}=\mathbf{E}_{k+1,n,\bullet}\in\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
such that
\theta\in\mathbf{I}_{k+1}+n\alpha
.

3.

If
E_{*}=E_{n_{k+1}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
for
n_{k+1}\in\mathcal{N}_{k+1}
, then
\overline{B}_{\rho_{k}}(E_{*})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\bar{\rho}_{k}/2\leq|\mathbf{J}_{k+1}|\leq 2\bar{\rho}_{k}.

Subject to these hypotheses at scale
s-1
, we verify that the statement holds at scale
s
with appropriately defined constants:

Theorem 5.4
.

There exists
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon_{0}(\alpha,v)
such that, for
\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, Proposition
5.3
holds for all
k\geq 0
.

For the remainder of this section, we suppose that the proposition holds for
0\leq k\leq s-1
, that Hypotheses
5.3.1
,
5.3.2
, and
5.3.3
hold for
0\leq k\leq s
, and prove the proposition for
k=s
. We note that, in light of Lemma
5.1
, the proof below also verifies the initial case
s=0
.

5.3
The family of descendants of
\mathbf{E}_{s}

If
s=0
, let
\mathbf{E}_{s}=v
; otherwise, let
\mathbf{E}_{s}\in\mathcal{E}^{(j_{s})}(\mathbf{E}_{s-1})
,
\mathbf{E}_{s}:\mathbf{I}_{s}\to\mathbf{J}_{s}
,
j_{s}\in\{1,2\}
, be a Rellich function of
H^{\Lambda_{s}}
,
\Lambda_{s}=[-L_{s}/2,L_{s}/2]
constructed as in Proposition
5.3
at scale
s-1
. In the case
j_{s}=2
(and thus
\mathbf{E}_{s}=\mathbf{E}_{s,n_{s-1},\bullet}^{(2)}
), we denote

\mathbf{I}_{s,n_{s-1},\cup}:=\mathbf{I}_{s,n_{s-1},\wedge}^{(2)}\cup\mathbf{I}_{s,n_{s-1},\vee}^{(2)}.

5.3.1
Construction of the next length scale

By our inductive hypotheses,
\mathbf{E}_{s}
satisfies Assumption
3
, with Morse constants
d_{s},D_{s}
and boundary derivative lower bound
\nu_{s}
. By Lemma
5.2
, Section
4
applies to
\mathbf{E}_{s}
with these values, and, recalling that

\bar{\rho}_{s}=\frac{d_{s}}{15000D_{0}^{2}}\rho_{s-1}^{2},

we have the following:

Lemma 5.5
.

\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\cap\mathbf{J}_{s}
can be covered by closed intervals
J_{s,i}^{(j)}
,
j\in\{1,2\}
, such that

1.

J_{s,n_{s}}^{(2)}=\overline{B}_{\bar{\rho}_{s}}(E_{n_{s}})
, where
\theta_{*}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
is the unique point in
\mathbf{I}_{s}
such that

E_{n_{s}}:=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+n_{s}\alpha)

for any
n_{s}\in\mathcal{N}_{s}:=\mathcal{N}(8M_{s+1}^{(1)},\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{s})
(cf. Section 4).

2.

\bar{\rho}_{s}\leq|J_{s,i}^{(j)}|\leq 2\bar{\rho}_{s}

3.

J_{s,i}\cap J_{s,i^{\prime}}\neq\emptyset\implies|J_{s,i}\cap J_{s,i^{\prime}}|=3\rho_{s}

4.

For any
E\in J_{s,i}^{(j)}
,
\overline{B}_{\rho_{s}}(E)\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
. Furthermore, if
j=2
and
E_{*}=E_{n_{s}}
, then
\overline{B}_{\rho_{s-1}}(E_{*})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s-1})
.

5.

The total number of such intervals
J_{s,i}^{(j)}
does not exceed
5\lceil|\mathbf{J}_{s}|\bar{\rho}_{s}^{-1}\rceil
.

These intervals are defined such that, on the corresponding preimages

\displaystyle I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}:=(\mathbf{E}_{s})_{\pm}^{-1}(J_{s,i}^{(j)}),\quad I_{s,i}^{(j)}:=I_{s,i,-}^{(j)}\cup I_{s,i,+}^{(j)},

one has either

(Simple resonance): If
\theta_{*}\in I_{s,i}^{(1)}
, then for any
n
with
0\neq|n|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(1)}
such that
\theta_{*}+n\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+n\alpha)|>3\rho_{s}.

(Double resonance): If
\theta_{*}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}\in I_{s,n_{s},-}^{(2)}
, then for
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s-1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
and any
n
with
n\notin\{0,n_{s}\},|n|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(2)}
such that
\theta+n\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta+n\alpha)|>2\rho_{s-1}

and

\displaystyle\min\{-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta),\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta+n_{s}\alpha)\}\geq\nu_{s}.

Finally, recalling that

\bar{\nu}_{s}=\frac{3d_{s}}{8D_{0}}\bar{\rho}_{s}<\nu_{s},

each function
\mathbf{E}_{s}|_{I_{s,i}^{(j)}}:I_{s,i}^{(j)}\to J_{s,i}^{(j)}
satisfies Assumption
3
with Morse constants
d_{s},D_{s}
and boundary derivative constant
\bar{\nu}_{s}
.

Proof.

This follows immediately from Propositions
4.9
and
4.10
, except the second half of item 4,
which follows from the inductive proposition.
∎

\mathbf{J}_{s}
E_{n_{s}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
\mathbf{E}_{s}
J_{s,n_{s}}^{(2)}
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\cap\mathbf{J}_{s}
\overline{\rho}_{s}
n_{s}\alpha
\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
\theta_{s,n_{s},+}
I_{s,n_{s},+}
I_{s,n_{s},-}
J_{s,i}^{(1)}
3\rho_{s}

Figure 8
:
A cartoon illustration of the covering constructed in Lemma
5.5
.

Fix an interval
J_{s,i}^{(j)}
(
j\in\{1,2\}
),
and note that
|I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}|\leq\rho_{s-1}/24D_{0}
by Lemma
4.2
, the definition of
\bar{\rho}_{s}
, and item 2 of Lemma
5.5
.
Consider all ‘ancestors’
\mathbf{E}_{k}:\mathbf{I}_{k}\to\mathbf{J}_{k}
(
0\leq k\leq s-1
) of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
.
For each such
k
, we define two sets
\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
as follows:

\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}:=\bigcup_{\theta\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}}\mathcal{S}_{k}(\theta,\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)).

The integers in
\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
are well-separated uniformly in
\theta
at each scale
0\leq k\leq s-1
in the following sense:

Lemma 5.6
.

Let
0\leq k\leq s-1
, and suppose
m\in\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
.

1.

If
j_{k+1}=1
, then for any
n\neq m
with
|n-m|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(1)}
,
n\notin\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
.

2.

If
j_{k+1}=2
(and thus
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}=\mathbf{E}_{k+1,n_{k},\bullet}
for some
M_{k}^{(2)}<|n_{k}|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(1)}
), then for some
p\in\{m,m-n_{k}\}
and for any
n\notin\{p,p+n_{k}\}
with
|n-p|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(2)}
,
n\notin\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
.

Proof.

Since
m\in\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
, there is some
\theta_{*}\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}
such that
m\in\mathcal{S}_{k}(\theta_{*},\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}))
; note that
J_{s,i}^{(j)}\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, so
E_{*}=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})
satisfies the assumptions of the inductive hypothesis
5.3.2
.
As noted above,
we also have that
|I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}|\leq\rho_{s-1}/24D_{0}\leq\rho_{k}/24D_{0}
.

We have two cases:

1.

j_{k+1}=1
:

Take
p=m
. By the length bound on
I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}
, we have

I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}+p\alpha\subset B_{\rho_{k}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{s}.

2.

j_{k+1}=2
:
By Hypothesis
5.3.2
and the length bound on
I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}
, for some
p\in\{m,m-n_{k+1}\}
, we have

I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}+p\alpha\subset B_{\rho_{k}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{k+1,\,\cup}\subset B_{\rho_{k}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{k+1,n_{k+1},-}).

In both cases, by Lemma
5.5
, for any
\theta\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}
and
n\neq p
with
|n-p|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(j_{k+1})}
such that
\theta+n\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k}
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+n\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)|\geq

\displaystyle|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+n\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+p\alpha)|-|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+p\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)|

\displaystyle-|\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|-|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)|

\displaystyle\geq 3\rho_{k}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s-1}-\frac{25}{24}\rho_{k}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s-1}\geq\frac{25}{24}\rho_{k},

and so
n\notin\mathcal{S}_{k,\pm}
, as claimed. ∎

This separation will, in turn, allow us to construct even integers
L_{s+1}
so that
\pm L_{s+1}/2
are
s
-regular:

Lemma 5.7
.

There is an even integer
L_{s+1}
with
M_{s+1}^{(j)}/2\leq L_{s+1}\leq M_{s+1}^{(j)}
so that, for all
\theta_{*}\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(j)}
for all
E_{*}\in B_{\rho_{s}}(\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}))
,
\pm L_{s+1}/2
and
\pm(L_{s+1}/2+1)
are
s
-regular relative to
(\theta_{*},E_{*})
.

Proof.

Let
a_{s}:=M_{s+1}^{(j)}/2
. We define a nonincreasing sequence of integers
a_{s},a_{s-1},\dots,a_{0}
as follows. For each
1\leq k\leq s
, we have by the previous lemma that

\#\left\{a\in[a_{k}-8L_{k},a_{k}]:\dist(a,\mathcal{S}_{k-1,\pm})<\frac{4}{5}L_{k}\right\}<\frac{9}{5}L_{k},

where we have crucially used here that
5M_{k-1}^{(2)}<M_{k}^{(1)}/2
. The same counting argument holds replacing
[a_{k}-8L_{k},a_{k}]
by
[-a_{k},-a_{k}+8L_{k}]
; since
4(9/5)<8
, we can find some
a_{k-1}\in[a_{k}-8L_{k},a_{k}]
such that

\dist(\pm a_{k-1},\mathcal{S}_{k-1,\pm})\geq\frac{4}{5}L_{k}

for all four choices of signs.

We define
L_{s+1}=2a_{0}
. By construction, we have

M^{(j)}_{s+1}\geq L_{s+1}\geq M^{(j)}_{s+1}-2\sum_{k=1}^{s}8L_{k}\geq M^{(j)}_{s+1}/2,

and

\dist(\pm L_{s+1}/2,\mathcal{S}_{k-1,\pm})\geq\frac{4}{5}L_{k}-8\sum_{i=1}^{k-1}L_{i}>\frac{3}{4}L_{k}+1

for all
1\leq k\leq s
and all four choices of signs.
∎

5.3.2
Decay of resonant eigenpairs

For the fixed interval
J_{s,i}^{(j)}
we denote by

\Lambda_{s+1}:=[-L_{s+1}/2,L_{s+1}/2]

and recall that

\ell_{s}=L_{s}^{5/6}\geq 16L_{s}^{2/3}|\log\varepsilon|^{2}=16|\log\varepsilon||\log\rho_{s}|.

From our inductive assumptions, we immediately find Green’s function decay away from
\{0\}
(and the double-resonant site
\{n_{s}\}
, if it exists):

Lemma 5.8
.

Fix
\theta_{*}\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
and find
\Lambda_{s+1}
as above. Then we have the following:

1.

If
\theta_{*}\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(1)}
, then for
|E-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|<\frac{3}{2}\rho_{s}
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\log|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus\Lambda_{s}}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{s}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{s}

and

\|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus\Lambda_{s}}\|\leq 4\rho_{s}^{-1}.

Furthermore, there are intervals
\Lambda_{l}\supset[-\frac{7}{8}L_{s},-\frac{1}{8}L_{s}]
and
\Lambda_{r}\supset[\frac{1}{8}L_{s},\frac{7}{8}L_{s}]
such that for
|E-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|<\frac{3}{2}\rho_{s}
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s-1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\displaystyle\log|R^{\Lambda_{l/r}}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{s-1}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{s-1}.

2.

If
\theta_{*}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
, then for
|E-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|<\frac{3}{2}\rho_{s-1}
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s-1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\log|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus(\Lambda_{s}\cup\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{s-1}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{s-1}

and

\|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus(\Lambda_{s}\cup\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})}\|\leq 4\rho_{s-1}^{-1}.

Furthermore, letting
c_{l}=\min\{0,n_{s}\}
and
c_{r}=\max\{0,n_{s}\}
, there are intervals
\Lambda_{l}\supset[c_{l}-\frac{7}{8}L_{s},c_{l}-\frac{1}{8}L_{s}]
,
\Lambda_{c}\supset[c_{l}+\frac{1}{8}L_{s},c_{r}-\frac{1}{8}L_{s}]
, and
\Lambda_{r}\supset[c_{r}+\frac{1}{8}L_{s},c_{r}+\frac{7}{8}L_{s}]
such that, for
|E-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|<\frac{3}{2}\rho_{s-1}
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s-1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\displaystyle\log|R^{\Lambda_{l/c/r}}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{s-1}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{s-1}.

In either case, the intervals
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
also satisfy the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{s-1},\gamma_{s-1})
,
as do the intervals
(\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
.

Proof.

Noting the
s
-regularity relative to
(\theta_{*},E_{*})
for any
E_{*}\in B_{\rho_{s}}(\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}))
, these statements are precisely the nonresonance hypothesis
5.3.3
applied to various intervals. In particular, the maximal connected components of
\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus\Lambda_{s}
are inductively
s
-nonresonant with
s
-directionally regular endpoints. Similarly, the maximal connected components of
\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus(\Lambda_{s}\cup\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})
are
s-1
-nonresonant, as are the maximal components of
[-L_{s},L_{s}]\setminus\Lambda_{s-1}
(in case
\mathbf{E}_{s}\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(\mathbf{E}_{s-1})
) or
[-L_{s},L_{s}]\setminus(\Lambda_{s-1}\cup\Lambda_{s-1}+n_{s-1})
(in case
\mathbf{E}_{s}\in\mathcal{E}^{(2)}(\mathbf{E}_{s-1})
).

The existence of the subintervals
\Lambda_{l/c/r}
follows from the nonresonance hypothesis after noting that

\frac{1}{8}L_{s}-M_{s-1}^{(2)}-1\geq\frac{1}{16}(M_{s-1}^{(2)})^{2}-M_{s-1}^{(2)}-1\gg 5L_{s-1}

for the left and right intervals, and

c_{r}-c_{l}-2(1+M_{s-1}^{(2)})\geq L_{s}-2(1+M_{s-1}^{(2)})\gg 5L_{s-1}

for the center interval in the double-resonant case.

Finally, the decay estimates on the intervals
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
likewise follow from the inductive nonresonance hypothesis and the
s-1
-directional regularity of
L_{s}/2
. The decay estimates on
(\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
follow from the corresponding regularities of
n_{s}\pm L_{s}/2
, which follow from the observation that
H^{\Lambda_{s}}(\theta_{*}+n_{s}\alpha)=H^{\Lambda_{s}+n_{s}}(\theta_{*})
.

∎

Noting that

\frac{3}{8}L_{s}>L_{s}^{5/6}=\ell_{s}\gg\ell_{s-1},

we apply the above decay to conclude stable eigenvector decay for resonant eigenvectors of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
:

Lemma 5.9
.

Suppose
\theta_{*}\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
, and let
E_{*}=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})
.

1.

If
\theta_{*}\in I_{s,i,\pm}^{(1)}
, let
\rho=\rho_{s}
and let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of
\Lambda_{s+1}
into
\Lambda_{s}
and its complement.

2.

If
\theta_{*}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
, let
\rho=\rho_{s-1}
and let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of
\Lambda_{s+1}
into
\Lambda_{s}\cup\Lambda_{s}+n_{s}
and its complement.

Then, for
\theta\in B_{\rho/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
, for any eigenpair
(E,\psi)
of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}(\theta)
with
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{2}\rho
, the unit eigenvector
\psi
has
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values no larger than
\delta_{s}/\varepsilon
:

\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}\psi\|\leq 4\delta_{s}.

Furthermore, the resonant unit eigenvector
\psi_{s}
of
H^{\Lambda_{s}}
corresponding to
\mathbf{E}_{s}
(as well as the associated unit eigenvector of
H^{\Lambda_{s}+n_{s}}
in case 2) has stably-small
\mathcal{P}
-boundary values:

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}\psi_{s}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\delta_{s}

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}(\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s})\|

\displaystyle\leq 24D_{0}\frac{\delta_{s}}{\rho}

Proof.

The estimate on
\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}\psi\|
follows in all cases from Lemma
5.8
and the Poisson formula (
1.11
); for example, in the case
\theta_{*}\in I_{s}^{(1)}
, denoting
\Lambda_{l}=[a_{l},b_{l}]
, one has

\displaystyle|\psi(-L_{s}/2)|

\displaystyle\leq\varepsilon\left(|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{l}}(-L_{s}/2,a_{l})|+|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{l}}(-L_{s}/2,b_{l})|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon e^{-3\gamma_{s-1}L_{s}/8}\leq\delta_{s}.

The other cases follow analogously.

The estimate on
\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}\psi_{s}\|
is made similarly, instead applying the decay on the intervals
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
.

Let
\chi_{s}
denote the linear projection onto the coordinates in the interval
\Lambda_{s}
, and
\widetilde{\chi}_{s}
denote the linear projection onto the interval

\{m:|m|\leq L_{s}/4\}\subset\Lambda_{s}.

Let
\Gamma_{s}:=\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}\chi_{s}
, and note that

\Gamma_{s}(\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s})=\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}}(\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s}).

Recall that

\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s}=-R_{\perp}(H^{\Lambda_{s}},\mathbf{E}_{s})V^{\prime}\psi_{s};

we break our estimate of
\|\Gamma_{s}(\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s})\|
into pieces corresponding to
\widetilde{\chi}_{s}
and its relative complement
(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})
.

By again applying the Poisson formula on the intervals
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
from Lemma
5.8
, we have

\displaystyle\|(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})V^{\prime}\psi_{s}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{L_{s}/4\leq|m|\leq L_{s}/2}D_{0}^{2}|\psi_{s}(m)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 8D_{0}^{2}\sum_{j>L_{s}/8}\varepsilon^{2}e^{-2\gamma_{s-1}j}

\displaystyle\leq(3D_{0}\delta_{s})^{2}.

Now let
\widecheck{\mathcal{P}}
be the partition of
\Lambda_{s}
into
\Lambda_{s}\cap(\Lambda_{l}\cup\Lambda_{c}\cup\Lambda_{r})
and its complement, and recall that
E_{*}=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})
. Letting
R_{\perp}=R_{\perp}(H^{\Lambda_{s}},\mathbf{E}_{s}),\widecheck{R}=R_{\widecheck{\mathcal{P}}}^{\Lambda_{s}}(E_{*}),
and
P_{s}=\psi_{s}\psi_{s}^{\top}
, we expand

\Gamma_{s}R_{\perp}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}=\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}(I-P_{s}-\Gamma_{\widecheck{\mathcal{P}}}^{\Lambda_{s}}R_{\perp})\widetilde{\chi}_{s}

by the resolvent identity. Since the intervals in
\Lambda_{s}\cap(\Lambda_{l}\cup\Lambda_{c}\cup\Lambda_{r})
are
s-1
-nonresonant, we get

\max\{\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|,\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\Gamma_{\widecheck{\mathcal{P}}}^{\Lambda_{s}}\|\}\leq 2\varepsilon e^{-\gamma_{s-1}L_{s}/4}.

By expanding
P_{s}=\widetilde{\chi}_{s}P_{s}+(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})P_{s}
, we likewise estimate

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}P_{s}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|\|P_{s}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|+\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\|\|(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})P_{s}\|\|\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon e^{-\gamma_{s-1}L_{s}/4}+\frac{8\varepsilon}{\rho_{s-1}}\cdot 3\varepsilon e^{-\gamma_{s-1}L_{s}/8}

\displaystyle\leq 6\frac{\delta_{s}}{\rho_{s-1}}.

It remains to estimate
\|R_{\perp}\|
, but this follows inductively. Specifically, by Hypothesis
5.3.2
, if
\theta_{*}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
, it must be that
j_{s}=1
and so
\|R_{\perp}\|\leq 4\rho_{s-1}^{-1}
by (
2.11
).
If
\theta_{*}\in I_{s}^{(1)}
, we have the estimate
\|R_{\perp}\|\leq 4\rho_{s}^{-1}
, either by (
2.11
) if
j_{s}=1
, or by (
3.13
) and Proposition
3.6
if
j_{s}=2
, since the separation guaranteed by Proposition
3.6
is bigger than
2\rho_{s}
. In either case,
\|R_{\perp}\|\leq 4\rho^{-1}
by our definition of
\rho
. Thus, we compute

\displaystyle\|\Gamma_{s}(\partial_{\theta}\psi_{s})\|

\displaystyle=\|\Gamma_{s}R_{\perp}V^{\prime}\psi_{s}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma_{s}R_{\perp}\|\|(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})V^{\prime}\psi_{s}\|+\|\Gamma_{s}R_{\perp}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|\|V^{\prime}\psi_{s}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|\Gamma_{s}R_{\perp}\|\|(\chi_{s}-\widetilde{\chi}_{s})V^{\prime}\psi_{s}\|+D_{0}\left(\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|+\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}P_{s}\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|+\|\Gamma_{s}\widecheck{R}\Gamma_{\widecheck{\mathcal{P}}}^{\Lambda_{s}}\|\|R_{\perp}\|\|\widetilde{\chi}_{s}\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 8\varepsilon\rho^{-1}\cdot 3D_{0}\delta_{s}+D_{0}\left(2\delta_{s}+6\delta_{s}\rho_{s-1}^{-1}+8\delta_{s}\rho^{-1}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 24D_{0}\frac{\delta_{s}}{\rho},

as claimed. The estimate for the unit eigenvector of
H^{\Lambda_{s}+n_{s}}
follows analogously using the intervals
(\Lambda_{s}+n_{s})\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
instead of the intervals
\Lambda_{s}\cap\Lambda_{l/c/r}
.
∎

5.3.3
Constructing the descendants
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}

With these estimates, we are ready to invoke the content of Sections 2 and 3:

Lemma 5.10
.

We have the following:

1.

(Simple resonance): If
\theta_{*}\in I_{s}^{(1)}
and
E_{*}=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})
, then
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}(\theta_{*})
satisfies Assumption
1
with the following assignments:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{s+1}

\displaystyle,\;\Lambda=\Lambda_{s},

\displaystyle\rho=\rho_{s},\;\delta=\delta_{s}

\displaystyle,\;\gamma=\gamma_{s},\;\ell=\ell_{s}.

2.

(Double resonance): If
\theta_{**}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}
and
E_{**}=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})
, then
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}(\theta_{**})
satisfies Assumption
2
with the following assignments:

\displaystyle\widehat{\Lambda}=\Lambda_{s+1},\;{\Lambda}_{-}

\displaystyle=\Lambda_{s},\;{\Lambda}_{+}=\Lambda_{s}+n_{s},

\displaystyle\widecheck{\rho}=\frac{2}{3}\rho_{s-1},\;\delta=\delta_{s}

\displaystyle,\;\gamma=\gamma_{s},\;\ell=\ell_{s},\;\nu={\nu}_{s}.

Proof.

We verify the relevant assumptions in each case:

1.

The values
\delta
,
\rho
, and
\gamma
satisfy (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) by definition. Taking
{\psi}=\psi_{s}
, item 1 of Assumption
1
is immediate by definition of
E_{*}
. Items 2 and 5 follow immediately from Lemma
5.9
, and item 3 follows immediately from Lemma
5.8
. Finally, item 4 follows from the inductive bound
\|R^{\Lambda_{s}}_{\perp,\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho_{s}^{-1}
(following from either Proposition
2.1
or
3.1
, depending on the value of
j_{s}
) and the bound
\|R^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus\Lambda_{s}}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho_{s}^{-1}
from Lemma
5.8
.

2.

Again,
\delta
,
\widecheck{\rho}
, and
\gamma
satisfy (
3.1
), (
3.2
), and (
3.3
) by definition. As in case 1, item 1 of Assumption
2
is immediate by definition of
E_{**}
, items 2 and 5 follow immediately from Lemma
5.9
, and item 3 follows immediately from Lemma
5.8
. Since
n_{s}\in\mathcal{N}_{s}\neq\emptyset
, we must have
j_{s}=1
, and so inductively
\|R_{\perp,\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s}}\|\leq 4\rho_{s-1}^{-1}<4\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}
for
|E-E_{**}|\leq\rho_{s-1}=\frac{3}{2}\widecheck{\rho}
(and similarly for
\|R_{\perp,\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s}+n_{s}}\|
) by Proposition
2.1
. Item 4 follows from this observation and the bound
\|R^{\Lambda_{s+1}\setminus(\Lambda_{s}\cup\Lambda_{s}+n)}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho_{s-1}^{-1}<4\widecheck{\rho}^{-1}
from Lemma
5.8
. Finally, item 6 follows from Lemma
5.5
. ∎

As a consequence of the above, for each phase interval
I_{s}\in\mathcal{I}
, we have found an interval
\Lambda_{s+1}=\Lambda_{s+1}(I_{s})
such that any resonant Rellich function is Morse with at most one critical point and whose eigenvector is localized. Specifically, if
I_{s}=I_{s,i}^{(1)}
we have found a unique Rellich function

\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}:I_{s,i}^{(1)}\to J_{s+1,i}^{(1)}

of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
,
where we define
J_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
to be the image of
I_{s,i}^{(1)}
under
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
.
Similarly, for each
I_{s,n,-}^{(2)}
,
n\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
, we have found two Rellich functions

\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n,\bullet}^{(2)}:I_{s,n,-}^{(2)}\to J_{s+1,n}^{(2)},\quad\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}

of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
,
where we define
J_{s+1,n}^{(2)}
to be the smallest interval containing the images of
I_{s,n,-}^{(2)}
under both Rellich functions.
We note that, since
\varepsilon<\frac{1}{3}
and
\ell_{s},L_{s}<M_{s+1}^{(2)}
, we may choose
