\widehat{\sigma}=\sigma_{s+1}
in Theorem
3.2
, as this choice satisfies (
3.14
).
To finish constructing the children of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
, we must modify the domains of these new Rellich functions so as to again satisfy Assumption
3
.

In the simple-resonant case, by Lemma
5.5
and Proposition
2.2
, the function
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
4.12
relative to the function
\mathbf{E}_{s,i}^{(1)}
with
\delta=\delta_{s}
and
\rho=\rho_{s}
; thus, we may find an interval
\mathbf{I}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}\subset I^{(1)}_{s,i}
and its image
\mathbf{J}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}:=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,i}^{(1)})
with

|I_{s,i}^{(1)}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}|\leq\frac{32\delta_{s}}{\bar{\nu}_{s}}\ll\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}.

We abuse notation and henceforth denote by
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
the restriction of the Rellich function of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
to the interval
\mathbf{I}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}
.

The double-resonant case requires a bit more care. If
I_{s}=I_{s,n_{s},-}^{(2)}
,
n_{s}\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
, we recall the regions

J^{(2)}_{s,n_{s}}:=\overline{B}_{\bar{\rho}_{s}}(E_{n_{s}}),\quad I_{s,n_{s},\pm}^{(2)}:=\mathbf{E}_{s,\pm}^{-1}(J^{(2)}_{s,n_{s}}).

Note that one has

B_{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{s}}{D_{0}}}(\theta_{s,n_{s},\pm})\subset I_{s,n_{s},\pm}^{(2)}\subset B_{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{s}}{\nu_{s}}}(\theta_{s,n_{s},\pm})

since
\nu_{s}\leq|\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E}_{s}|\leq D_{0}
on
I_{s,n_{s},\pm}^{(2)}
.

Denote by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\max\{\mathbf{E}_{s,-}(\theta),\mathbf{E}_{s,+}(\theta+n_{s}\alpha)\},\quad\theta\in\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}:=[\inf I_{s,n_{s},-}^{(2)},\sup I_{s,n_{s},+}^{(2)}-n_{s}\alpha],

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\min\{\mathbf{E}_{s,-}(\theta),\mathbf{E}_{s,+}(\theta+n_{s}\alpha)\},\quad\theta\in\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}:=[\inf I_{s,n_{s},+}^{(2)}-n_{s}\alpha,\sup I_{s,n_{s},-}^{(2)}].

We denote also

I_{s,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}:=I_{s,n_{s},-}^{(2)}\cap(I_{s,n_{s},+}^{(2)}-n_{s}\alpha)\subset\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)},\quad\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}.

Since
\theta_{s,n_{s},+}=\theta_{s,n_{s},-}+n_{s}\alpha
, we have that

\overline{B}_{\frac{\bar{\rho}_{s}}{D_{0}}}(\theta_{s,n_{s},-})\subset I_{s,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}.

By construction,

|\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}(\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)})|=\bar{\rho}_{s},\quad\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\},

and thus, by Lemma
4.2
,

\displaystyle|\{\theta\in\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}:\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}(\theta)=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta+p\alpha)\}|\geq\frac{\bar{\rho}_{s}}{D_{0}},\quad\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\},\;p\in\{0,n_{s}\}.

The functions
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
satisfy Assumption 3; by Lemma
5.5
and Theorem
3.2
, the Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
satisfy the assumptions of Lemma
4.11
relative to the functions
\tilde{\mathbf{E}}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
with
\delta=C\delta_{s}
; thus, we may find an interval
\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}\subset\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
and its image
\mathbf{J}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}:=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)})
with

|\tilde{I}_{s,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}|\leq\frac{32C\delta_{s}}{\bar{\nu}_{s}}\ll\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}.

In particular, we note that
|\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}|\geq\frac{\overline{\rho}_{s}}{D_{0}}-\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}\gg\frac{5\rho_{s}}{4\nu_{s}}
, where
\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}:=\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}\cap\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}
, so we may choose
\eta=\frac{5\rho_{s}}{4\nu_{s}}
and
\rho=\rho_{s}
in Theorem
3.2
, with
B_{\eta}(\theta_{s,n_{s},-})\subset\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}
.
We abuse notation and henceforth denote by
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
the restriction of the corresponding Rellich function of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
to the interval
\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
,
\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\}
.

Denote the collection of all descendants of
\mathbf{E}_{s}
constructed above by
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
.

Lemma 5.11
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
be constructed as above.

1.

\mathbf{E}_{s+1}:\mathbf{I}_{s+1}\to\mathbf{J}_{s+1}
satisfies Assumption
3
with Morse constants

\displaystyle d_{s+1}

\displaystyle=\nu_{s}/12,

\displaystyle D_{s+1}

\displaystyle=2D_{0}(1+D_{0}\sigma_{s+1}^{-1})

and boundary derivative constant

\displaystyle\nu_{s+1}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{d_{s+1}}{14(8M_{s+2}^{(1)})^{\tau}},\frac{\bar{\nu}_{s}}{2}\right\}=\frac{\bar{\nu}_{s}}{2}

2.

\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\cup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})

3.

If
\mathbf{E}_{s}
does not attain its maximum at a critical point, then

\sup\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\leq\sup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}).

Similarly, if
\mathbf{E}_{s}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point, then

\inf\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\geq\inf\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}).

4.

If
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
does not attain its supremum
\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}
at a critical point and
\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\neq\sup_{\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})}\sup\mathbf{J}^{\prime}
, then there is some
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
such that

B_{\frac{11\rho_{s}}{4}}(\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1})\subset\mathbf{J}_{s+1}^{\prime}.

The same statement holds with
\inf
in place of
\sup
.

Proof.

Item 1 follows immediately from Lemma
5.10
; from the same result, we likewise have

|\sup J_{s+1,i}^{(j)}-\sup J_{s,i}^{(j)}|,|\inf J_{s+1,i}^{(j)}-\inf J_{s,i}^{(j)}|\leq C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/2

for
j\in\{1,2\}
and all
i
for which these intervals are defined. The process of “trimming” the function by Lemma
4.11
or
4.12
does not change the supremum or infimum; thus, we still have

\displaystyle|\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}-\sup J_{s,i}^{(1)}|,|\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,i}^{(1)}-\inf J_{s,i}^{(1)}|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/2,

\displaystyle|\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}-\sup J_{s,n_{s}}^{(2)}|,|\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}-\inf J_{s,n_{s}}^{(2)}|

\displaystyle\leq C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/2.

In particular, since each
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n,\vee}^{(2)}
attains its infimum at a critical point, and each
\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n,\wedge}^{(2)}
attains it supremum at a critical point, this estimate applies at every non-critical value extremum of any
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
.

The next three items follow. Specifically, by construction,

\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})-\sup J_{s+1,i}^{(j)}\geq\rho_{s};

and by definition,

\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})-\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\leq\rho_{s+1}.

These estimates, combined with the analogous estimates with
\inf
and with the estimates established above, give item 2. For item 3, we use the fact that by construction,

\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})-\sup_{i,j}\sup J_{s,i}^{(j)}\leq 2\bar{\rho}_{s}+\rho_{s}\ll\rho_{s-1}/24,

and the fact that, by definition, for each
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
,
\mathbf{E}:\mathbf{I}\to\mathbf{J}
, we have

\sup\mathbf{J}-\sup\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\leq\frac{5}{4}\rho_{s}\ll\rho_{s-1}/24.

Since, by definition,
\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})-\sup\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})=\rho_{s-1}/8
, and the analogous estimates with
\inf
hold, item 3 follows from the above estimates. Item 4 follows from the above estimates and the fact that, by construction, each pair of adjacent intervals
J_{s,i}^{(j)}
overlap by
3\rho_{s}
.
∎

Finally, we verify the directional regularity of our new endpoints:

Lemma 5.12
.

For all
\theta_{*}\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
for all
E_{*}\in B_{\rho_{s}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{*}))
,
L_{s+1}/2+1
is
s+1
-left-regular and
-(L_{s+1}/2+1)
is
s+1
-right-regular relative to
(\theta_{*},E_{*})
.

Proof.

We will show the point
L_{s+1}/2+1
is
s+1
-left-regular; the other case is analogous. Since
L_{s+1}/2+1
is
s
-regular by Lemma
5.7
, it suffices to check that
[L_{s+1}/2+1,5L_{s+1}/4+1]
is
s
-nonresonant.

In the case
j_{s+1}=2
, we have
\theta_{*}\in\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}
, an interval of length at most
\rho_{s-1}/12D_{0}
; thus
\dist(\theta_{*},\mathbf{I}_{s,-})\leq\rho_{s-1}/12D_{0}
, and
\dist(\theta_{*}+n_{s}\alpha,\mathbf{I}_{s,+})\leq\rho_{s-1}/12D_{0}
. Then, by the Diophantine condition,
\dist(\theta_{*}+m\alpha,\mathbf{I}_{s})>\rho_{s-1}/12D_{0}
for all
m\in[L_{s+1}/2+1,5L_{s+1}/4+1]
, which implies that interval is
s
-nonresonant.

Consider instead the case
j_{s+1}=1
.
We have

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{*})-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|\leq C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/2,

and for any
m\in[L_{s+1}/2+1,5L_{s+1}/4+1]
,

0<|m|\leq(5L_{s+1}/4+1)<8M_{s+1}^{(1)};

by Lemma
5.5
, it follows that, for any such
m
, if
\theta_{*}+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s}
,

\displaystyle|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)|-|E_{*}-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})|

\displaystyle\geq|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*})-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)|-2\rho_{s}>\rho_{s}.\qed

5.4
Resonant orbits

We note that double resonance of any descendant
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
can happen only at lengths greater than
L_{s+1}
:

Lemma 5.13
.

If
j_{s+1}=1
, then

\displaystyle\min\{|n|:n\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}\}>8M_{s+1}^{(1)}\geq L_{s+1}.

If
j_{s+1}=2
, then
\mathcal{N}_{s+1}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

In the case
j_{s+1}=2
, the interval
\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
is a single interval of length at most
\rho_{s-1}/24D_{0}
, and
\mathcal{N}_{s+1}=\emptyset
by the Diophantine condition.

Consider instead the case
j_{s+1}=1
, and suppose for the sake of contradiction that there exists
n\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}
with
|n|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(1)}
. Then by definition there exists
\theta_{s+1,n,-}\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,-}
such that
\theta_{s+1,n,-}+n\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,+}
and
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{s+1,n,-})=\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{s+1,n,-}+n\alpha)
.

Define the function
g(\theta)=\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta+n\alpha)
. Since
j_{s+1}=1
, we have that
|g(\theta_{s+1,n,-})|\leq 2C\delta_{s}
; furthermore, we have

|\partial_{\theta}g(\theta)|\geq 2\left(\nu_{s}-C\frac{\delta_{s}}{\rho_{s}}\right)\geq\nu_{s}

for
\theta\in I_{s+1,n,-}^{(2)}
by Lemma
5.5
. Thus, there exists some point
\theta_{s,n,-}
with

|\theta_{s,n,-}-\theta_{s+1,n,-}|<2C\frac{\delta_{s}}{\nu_{s}}\ll\frac{3}{4D_{0}}\bar{\rho}_{s}

such that
|g(\theta_{s,n,-})|=0
; since
|n|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(1)}
,
n\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
. But
B_{3\bar{\rho}_{s-1}/4D_{0}}(\theta_{s-1,n,-})\cap I_{s-1,i,-}^{(1)}=\emptyset
for any
i
and any
n\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
; thus,
j_{s+1}=2
, a contradiction.
∎

This allows us to verify a remaining item of the inductive proposition:

Lemma 5.14
.

If
E_{*}=E_{n_{s+1}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
for
n_{s+1}\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}
, then
\overline{B}_{\rho_{s}}(E_{*})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
.

Proof.

Let
n_{s+1}\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}
and
E_{*}=E_{n_{s+1}}
.
We have two cases:

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point, since
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
,
E_{*}-\rho_{s}\in\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
, by Lemma
5.11
.

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
attains its minimum at a critical point, note that as
\mathcal{N}_{s+1}
is nonempty,
j_{s+1}=1
by Lemma
5.13
. Thus, by Lemma
5.10
,
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
is
C^{2}
-close to
\mathbf{E}_{s}
, so
\mathbf{E}_{s}
also attains its minimum at a critical point, with
|\inf\mathbf{J}_{s}-\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}|\leq 2\delta_{s}
. By Lemma
4.7
,
E_{*}-\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\gg 2\delta_{s}
; thus

\displaystyle E_{*}-\rho_{s}

\displaystyle\geq\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+2\delta_{s}-\rho_{s}

\displaystyle\geq\inf\mathbf{J}_{s}-\rho_{s}

\displaystyle=\inf\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s}).

In either case,
E_{*}-\rho_{s}\geq\inf\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
; analogously,
E_{*}+\rho_{s}\leq\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
.
∎

We likewise demonstrate that
s
-resonant points
m
correspond to points
\theta_{*}+m\alpha
in the domain of the relevant Rellich function (part of Hypothesis
5.3.2
):

Lemma 5.15
.

Let
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
and
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
, and suppose that
E_{*}
satisfies the following:

1.

If
j_{s+1}=1
, either
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
, or the closest point in
\mathbf{J}_{s+1}
to
E_{*}
is a critical value of
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
.

2.

If
j_{s+1}=2
(and so
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
), then
E_{*}\in[\inf\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}),\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)})]

Let
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
. Then:

1.

If
j_{s+1}=1
, then
B_{\rho_{s}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
.

2.

If
j_{s+1}=2
(and so
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
), then for some
p\in\{m,m-n_{s}\}
,
B_{\rho_{s}/24D_{0}}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)\subset\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}^{(2)}
. Furthermore, if
\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}
, then

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|<\frac{9}{8}\rho_{s}.

Proof.

The proof proceeds by case analysis:

1.

j_{s+1}=1
:

(a)

\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
is a union of two disjoint intervals:
In this case,
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
has no critical points, so
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})=[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+\frac{9}{8}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{9}{8}\rho_{s}]
. Thus,

\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\in[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+\frac{1}{12}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{12}\rho_{s}],

and so

\mathbf{E}_{s}\left(B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\right)\subset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}].

On the other hand, one has

\mathbf{E}_{s,\pm}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\pm})\supset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]

since
j_{s+1}=1
, and in particular

\mathbf{I}_{s+1}\supset\mathbf{E}_{s}^{-1}\left([\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]\right)\supset B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha).

(b)

\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
is a single interval:
In this case,
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}
has a unique critical point in
\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
. Suppose that this critical point is a minimum (the other case is analogous). Since
j_{s+1}=1
,
\mathbf{E}_{s}
likewise achieves its minimum at a critical point, and by definition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
and our assumption on
E_{*}
we have

E_{*}\leq\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{9}{8}\rho_{s}.

As above, we find

\mathbf{E}_{s}\left(B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\right)\subset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}].

On the other hand, one has that

\mathbf{E}_{s,\pm}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\pm})\supset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]

since
j_{s+1}=1
, and in particular

\mathbf{I}_{s+1}\supset\mathbf{E}_{s}^{-1}\left([\inf\mathbf{J}_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]\right)\supset B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha).

2.

j_{s+1}=2
and
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\bullet}^{(2)}
: By assumption, we have

E_{*}\in[\inf\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}),\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)})],

from which it follows that

\mathbf{E}_{s}\left(B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)\right)\supset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\wedge}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\vee}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]

in analog to the above. We have two subcases:

(a)

\theta_{*}+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s,-}
: Let
I=[\inf\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\vee},\sup\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\wedge}]\subset\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}
. Since
j_{s+1}=2
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{s,-}(I)\supset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\wedge}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\vee}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}],

from which it follows that

I\supset\mathbf{E}_{s,-}^{-1}\left([\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\wedge}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\vee}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]\right)\supset B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha).

Furthermore, for
\theta\in(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge})\cap I
, we have by Lemma
3.9
that

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}(\theta)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta)|<C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/12,

and similarly for
\vee
and
\wedge
swapped. The lemma thus holds in this case taking
p=m
.

(b)

\theta_{*}+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s,+}
: Let
I=[\inf\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\wedge},\sup\mathbf{I}_{s+1,\vee}]\subset\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}
. Since
j_{s+1}=2
, we have

\mathbf{E}_{s,+}(I+n_{s}\alpha)\supset[\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\wedge}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\vee}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}],

from which it follows that

I+n_{s}\alpha\supset\mathbf{E}_{s,+}^{-1}\left([\inf\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\wedge}+\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s},\sup\mathbf{J}_{s+1,\vee}-\frac{1}{24}\rho_{s}]\right)\supset B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha),

i.e. for
p=m-n_{s}
,

\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}\supset I\supset B_{\frac{\rho_{s}}{24D_{0}}}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha).

Furthermore, for
\theta\in(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge})\cap I
, we have by Lemma
3.9
that

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}(\theta)-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta+n_{s}\alpha)|<C\delta_{s}\ll\rho_{s}/12,

and similarly for
\vee
and
\wedge
swapped. The lemma thus holds in this case taking
p=m-n_{s}
. ∎

5.5
Nonresonance

We now prove that the nonresonance hypothesis
5.3.3
holds at scale
s+1
.
We begin by establishing Green’s function decay for
s+1
-nonresonant intervals provided their left and right endpoints are
(s+1)
-left- and
(s+1)
-right-regular, respectively.

Lemma 5.16
.

Let
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
and
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
be such that

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{s}
does not attain its maximum at a critical point, then
E_{*}\leq\sup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
.

•

If
\mathbf{E}_{s}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point, then
E_{*}\geq\inf\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
.

Let
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
be a Rellich curve minimizing
\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}))
among all
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
, and let
\widehat{\Lambda}=[a,b]\subset\mathbb{Z}
be an
(s+1)
-nonresonant interval with
a
being
(s+1)
-left-regular and
b
being
(s+1)
-right-regular. Then for
E\in B_{\rho_{s+1}/2}(E_{*})
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s+1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
,

\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\theta,E}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{s+1}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{s+1},

and

\|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho_{s+1}^{-1}.

Proof.

Recall the set
\mathcal{S}_{s}(\theta_{*},E_{*})
(defined relative to the parent curve
\mathbf{E}_{s}
), and define
\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}:=\mathcal{S}_{s}\cap\widehat{\Lambda}
. If
\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is empty, then
\widehat{\Lambda}
is
s
-nonresonant and the lemma follows from the inductive hypothesis. We thus assume
\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is nonempty.

In order to apply Lemmas
A.3
and
A.1
, we wish to find a finite family
\{\Lambda_{s+1}+p\}_{p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}}
of translates of
\Lambda_{s+1}
such that each
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is near the center of
\Lambda_{s+1}+p
for some
p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
and
\|R^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}_{\theta_{*},E_{*}}\|\leq\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
for all
p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
. We do so by case analysis:

1.

j_{s+1}=1
:

For any
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
,
\theta_{*}+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1}
by Lemma
5.15
, and since
|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|<25\rho_{s}/24
and
\widehat{\Lambda}
is
(s+1)
-nonresonant, we have

\frac{9}{8}\rho_{s}>|\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}.

By the eigenvalue separation estimates in Section
2
coming from Lemma
5.10
, it follows that
|\lambda-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}
for any eigenvalue
\lambda
of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}+m}(\theta_{*})
, and so
\|R_{\theta_{*},E_{*}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}+m}\|\leq\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
. In this case, we define
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
.

2.

j_{s+1}=2
:

Let
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}=\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}
(the
\wedge
case is analogous), and fix
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
. By Lemma
5.15
,
\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cup}^{(2)}
for some
p=p_{m}\in\{m,m-n_{s}\}
. By the regularity assumption on the endpoints of
\widehat{\Lambda}
, we must have that
p\in\widehat{\Lambda}
. We have subcases:

(a)

\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}
: We have
|\mathbf{E}_{s+1}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}
.
To separate
E_{*}
from
\mathbf{E}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)
, we crucially use the uniform separation between the images of
\mathbf{E}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}
and
\mathbf{E}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}
guaranteed by Proposition
3.6
. This separation is greater than
2\rho_{s+1}
, which implies that
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee})
and
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}^{(2)}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge})
are disjoint; since, by assumption,

\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1}))\leq\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)})),

we have

E_{*}\geq\sup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)})=\sup\mathbf{J}_{\wedge}+\rho_{s+1},

and so

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}.

It remains to separate
E_{*}
from other eigenvalues of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}
. Because

\dist\left(E_{*},\mathbf{E}_{s}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}\cup(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}+n_{s}\alpha))\right)<\frac{25}{24}\rho_{s}\ll\frac{1}{12}\rho_{s}

and by construction

|\mathbf{E}_{s}(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}\cup(\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}^{(2)}+n_{s}\alpha))|\ll\frac{1}{4}\rho_{s-1},

it follows that

|E_{*}-\mathbf{E}_{s}(\theta_{s,n_{s},-})|<\frac{1}{3}\rho_{s-1}=\frac{1}{2}\left(\frac{2}{3}\rho_{s-1}\right).

Thus we may apply the estimate on
\|R_{\perp}(E_{*};H^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p},\{\mathbf{E}_{s+1,\vee}\}\cup\{\mathbf{E}_{s+1,\wedge}\})\|
coming from Section
3
and Lemma
5.10
. It follows that
\|R_{\theta_{*},E_{*}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}\|\leq\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
.

(b)

\theta_{*}+p\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\vee}^{(2)}\setminus\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}

(The case with
\vee
and
\wedge
swapped is analogous.):
Lemma
5.15
and the eigenvalue separation estimate from Lemma
3.9
ensure
|\lambda-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}
for any eigenvalue
\lambda\neq\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}
of
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}(\theta_{*})
. As in the
\mathbf{I}_{s+1,n_{s},\cap}
case,

|\mathbf{E}_{s+1,n_{s},\wedge}^{(2)}(\theta_{*}+p\alpha)-E_{*}|\geq\rho_{s+1}.

It follows that
\|R_{\theta_{*},E_{*}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}\|\leq\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
.

We denote by
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
the set of all integers
p
coming from
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
as above.

For each interval
\Lambda_{s+1}+p
,
p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
, it follows immediately from
\|R_{\theta_{*},E_{*}}^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}\|\leq\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
that, for
E\in B_{3\rho_{s+1}/4}(E_{*})
and
\theta\in B_{\rho_{s+1}/8D_{0}}(\theta_{*})
, that

\|R_{\theta,E}^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p}\|\leq 8\rho_{s+1}^{-1}.

By the
s+1
-regularity of the endpoints of
\widehat{\Lambda}=[a,b]
, for any
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
, we have

\min\{|m-a|,|m-b|\}\geq 3L_{s+1}/4;

since
|p-m|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(1)}\ll L_{s+1}/4
, we have that
\Lambda_{s+1}+p\subset\Lambda
for all
p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
. Moreover, by Lemma
5.5
, the intervals
\{\Lambda_{s+1}+p\}_{p\in\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}}
are non-overlapping. Enumerating
\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=\{p_{i}\}_{i=1}^{|\overline{\mathcal{S}}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}|}
in increasing order, we denote by
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i}:=[p_{i},p_{i}+n_{s}]
(with the convention that
n_{s}=0
if
j_{s+1}=1
) and define the intervals
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,l/r}
to be the maximal intervals to the left/right of
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i}
, i.e.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,l}

\displaystyle=[\max\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i-1}+1,\min\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i}-1]=:[\widetilde{a}_{i,l},\widetilde{b}_{i,l}],

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,r}

\displaystyle=[\max\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i}+1,\min\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i+1}-1]=:[\widetilde{a}_{i,r},\widetilde{b}_{i,r}].

We note that
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,r}=\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i+1,l}
, but we fix this notation to emphasize the relationship of these intervals to
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i}
.

Again by Lemma
5.5
, each interval
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,l/r}
is
s
-nonresonant and has length at least
L_{s+1}\gg 2L_{s}+3M_{s}^{(1)}
, and thus each such interval satisfies the inductive hypothesis
5.3.3
. In particular, there exist intervals
{\Lambda}_{i,l/r}=[{a}_{i,l/r},{b}_{i,l/r}]\subset\widetilde{\Lambda}_{i,l/r}
satisfying the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{s},\gamma_{s})
with

\max\{|\widetilde{a}_{i,l/r}-{a}_{i,l/r}|,|\widetilde{b}_{i,l/r}-{b}_{i,l/r}|\}\leq 2L_{s}+3M_{s}^{(1)}\ll\frac{1}{2}M_{s+1}^{(1)}.

Moreover, by the regularity of the endpoints of
\widehat{\Lambda}
, we can choose

{a}_{1,l}=\widetilde{a}_{1,l}=a,\quad{b}_{|\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{s}|,r}=\widetilde{b}_{|\overline{\mathcal{S}}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{s}|,r}=b.

Define
{\Lambda}_{i}=[{b}_{i,l}+1,{a}_{i,r}-1]
. We have
{\Lambda}_{i}\subset\Lambda_{s+1}+p_{i}\subset\widehat{\Lambda}
and
\|R^{\Lambda_{s+1}+p_{i}}\|\leq 8\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
, so we may apply Lemma
A.3
to conclude that
\widehat{\Lambda}
, partitioned by the
\Lambda_{i}
and
\Lambda_{i,l/r}
subintervals, satisfies Assumption
4

with
({\ell},{\gamma})=(\ell_{s},\gamma_{s})
and
\widehat{\ell}=\ell_{s+1}
. By Lemma
A.1
,
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{s+1},\gamma_{s+1})
where
\gamma_{s+1}=\gamma_{s}-16|\log\varepsilon\log(\rho_{s+1}/8)|/\ell_{s+1}
.

Since we have shown the Green’s function decay property for
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{4}\rho_{s+1}
, it follows that the resolvent is well-defined for
|E-E_{*}|<\frac{3}{4}\rho_{s+1}
, i.e.,
E\notin\spec H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(\theta)
. Thus, for
|E-E_{*}|\leq\rho_{s+1}/2
,
\|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\theta,E}\|\leq 4\rho_{s+1}^{-1}
.
∎

Finally, the following lemma shows that if the endpoints of
\widehat{\Lambda}
are not regular, we can adjust them (provided
\widehat{\Lambda}
is sufficiently long) to find a subinterval which does satisfy the conditions of Lemma
5.16
.

Lemma 5.17
.

Let
\theta_{*},E_{*},\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}^{(j_{s+1})}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
satisfy the relations stated in Lemma
5.16
, and let

\widehat{\Lambda}=[a,b]
with

|\widehat{\Lambda}|\geq 2L_{s+1}+3M_{s+1}^{(1)}
.
Then there exists a subinterval
\tilde{\Lambda}=[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]\subset\Lambda
with
|\tilde{\Lambda}|\geq|\widehat{\Lambda}|-(2L_{s+1}+3M_{s+1}^{(1)})
such that
\tilde{a}
is
s+1
-left-regular and
\tilde{b}
is
s+1
-right-regular.

Proof.

In the following, if
j_{s+1}=1
, we adopt the convention that
n_{s}=0
.

Let
m_{a}
minimize
|m-a|
among
m\in\mathcal{S}_{s}^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
. If
\theta_{*}+m_{a}\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s}

and
|m_{a}-a|<L_{s}
, we replace
a
with
a_{s-1}=\max\{m_{a}+1,m_{a}+n_{0}+1\}
; similarly, if
\theta_{*}+m_{a}\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{s+1}+n_{s}\alpha
and
|m_{a}-a|<L_{s+1}
, replace
a
with
a_{s}=\max\{m_{a}+1,m_{a}-n_{s}+1\}
.
Otherwise
a_{s}=a
. We perform analogous adjustments to
b
.
We repeat this process for each
s-1\geq i\geq 1
, with
i
replacing
s
, and set
\tilde{a}=a_{0}
and
\tilde{b}=b_{0}
.
By the inductive proposition, these endpoints satisfy the necessary regularity conditions.
∎

5.6
Proof of Proposition
5.3
and Theorem
5.4

Proof.

The lemmas above complete the proof of inductive Proposition
5.3
at scale
s
. The integers
L_{s+1}
satisfy the Proposition by Lemmas
5.7
and
5.12
. Any function in
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
is a Rellich function of some
H^{\Lambda_{s+1}}
satisfying Assumption
3
by Lemma
5.11
; such a function satisfies inductive hypothesis
5.3.1
by Lemma
5.10
, hypothesis
5.3.2
by Lemmas
5.13
and
5.15
, and hypothesis
5.3.3
by Lemmas
5.16
and
5.17
. For any
\mathbf{E}_{s+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
,
\mathbf{J}_{s+1}\cup\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
by Lemma
5.11
; and if
E_{*}=E_{n_{s+1}}(\mathbf{E}_{s+1})
for
n_{s+1}\in\mathcal{N}_{s+1}
, then
\overline{B}_{\rho_{s}}(E_{*})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
by Lemma
5.14
. Item 4 follows from Lemma
5.5
.

Since our choice of
\varepsilon<\varepsilon_{0}=\rho_{0}^{4}
was arbitrary provided
L
was sufficiently long as in Lemma
5.2
, Theorem
5.4
holds.
∎

6
Localization and Cantor spectrum

With the induction argument of Section
5
complete, we have now constructed a tree of Rellich functions
\mathbf{E}_{s}
, cf. Figure
4
, with each child classified as being either simple- or double-resonant relative to its parent. In this section we relate this tree to spectral information about the limiting operator
H
. In particular, we will show the following:

1.

The spectral points of
H
are precisely the limit points of the modified codomains
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
along any infinite path
\{\mathbf{E}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
in our Rellich tree.

2.

Every Rellich function
\mathbf{E}
has some double-resonant descendant;
by the uniform local separation estimate from Proposition
3.6
,
the gap that this double resonance opens is large enough to remain open for all future scales, guaranteeing Cantor spectrum.

3.

The set
\mathrm{B}
of bad phases
\theta_{*}
which encounter a scale-
k
double resonance on an orbit of approximate size
M_{k+1}^{(2)}
for infinitely many scales
k
has zero measure. The full-measure complement
\Theta
of this bad set will have the property that, for
\theta\in\Theta
, any generalized eigenvalue
E(\theta)
of
H(\theta)
corresponds to a path
\{\mathbf{E}^{(j_{k})}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
which eventually consists only of simple resonances, i.e. there exists some
K
such that
j_{k}=1
for all
k\geq K
. In fact, we will show that this path can be chosen so that the corresponding center of localization
m_{k}
eventually remains fixed; this will yield exponential localization of the corresponding generalized eigenfunction, hence Anderson localization.

To this end, it will be useful to introduce some notation to refer to different parts of our Rellich tree. Recall that
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E})
denotes the collection of all immediate children of the Rellich curve
\mathbf{E}
, and that
\mathcal{E}_{s}
denotes the collection of all scale-
s
Rellich functions, i.e. the
s^{th}
“generation” of the tree. For a Rellich function
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
,
k\leq s
, we denote by
\mathcal{E}_{s}(\mathbf{E})
the collection of all the scale-
s
descendants of
\mathbf{E}
; i.e., for a scale-
k
Rellich function
\mathbf{E}_{k}
,
\mathcal{E}_{k}(\mathbf{E}_{k})=\{\mathbf{E}_{k}\}
, and for all
s\geq k
, if
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{s}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
and
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
, then
\mathbf{E}^{\prime\prime}\in\mathcal{E}_{s+1}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
.
With this notation we have
\mathcal{E}_{k}=\mathcal{E}_{k}(\mathbf{E}_{0})
; we denote by
\mathcal{E}:=\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{E}_{k}
the entire Rellich tree.

6.1
Characterization of the spectrum

In this first subsection, we use paths through the tree of Rellich curves to characterize the spectrum of
H
.

First, note that there can be at most one energy common to the modified codomains
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
of all Rellich functions in an infinite path:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
\{\mathbf{E}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
be a sequence of Rellich functions such that
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
for all
k\geq 0
. Then
\liminf_{k\to\infty}|{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})|=\liminf_{k\to\infty}|\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})|=0
. In particular,
\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
contains at most a single point.

Proof.

By construction,
|\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})|,|\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})|\leq 4\bar{\rho}_{k-1}\to 0
.
∎

Thus, each infinite path characterizes a single point; we will show these are spectral points. Define the sets

\Sigma:=\left\{E\in\mathbb{R}:E=\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k}),\;\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\,\forall k\geq 0\right\}

and

G:=(\mathbb{R}\setminus\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{0}))\cup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}}\left(\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\setminus\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})\right).

We will show that
\Sigma
characterizes the spectrum of
H(\theta)
, while
G
characterizes the spectral gaps. We first show that points in
\Sigma
are indeed spectral:

Lemma 6.2
.

\Sigma\subset\spec H(\theta).

Proof.

Let
E\in\Sigma
. Then for each
k\geq 0
, we can find a
(\theta_{k},E_{k})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
, with
|E-E_{k}|\leq\rho_{k}
, and
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}
such that
H^{\Lambda_{k}}\psi_{k}=\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta_{k})\psi_{k}=E_{k}\psi_{k}
. By irrationality of
\alpha
, we can find some
m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\|(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)-\theta_{k}\|_{\mathbb{T}}<\rho_{k}/D_{0}
. Let
\mathcal{P}
be the partition of
\mathbb{Z}
into
\Lambda_{k}
and its complement, and let
\psi_{k}^{m_{k}}
denote
\psi_{k}
shifted by
m_{k}
. Then

\displaystyle\|(H(\theta)-E)\psi_{k}^{m_{k}}\|

\displaystyle=\|(H(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)-E)\psi_{k}\|

\displaystyle\leq\|H(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)-H(\theta_{k})\|+\|(H(\theta_{k})-E_{k})\psi_{k}\|+|E_{k}-E|

\displaystyle\leq\|V(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)-V(\theta_{k})\|+\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\psi_{k}\|+|E_{k}-E|

\displaystyle<3\rho_{k}

Thus
\{\psi_{k}^{m_{k}}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
forms a Weyl sequence for
E
, and
E\in\spec H(\theta)
.
∎

We now establish an important technical lemma demonstrating that energies
outside of the modified codomains of all children of a Rellich curve are uniformly nonresonant on sufficiently long intervals
\Lambda
:

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\setminus\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})
. Then for any
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
, there are arbitrarily long intervals
\tilde{\Lambda}
with the
(\ell_{k+1},\gamma_{k+1})
Green’s function decay property.

Proof.

It suffices to show that the hypotheses of Lemma
5.17
are met by
E_{*}
, any
\theta_{*}\in\mathbb{T}
, and any sufficiently long interval
\Lambda\subset\mathbb{Z}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{E}_{k}
does not attain its maximum at a critical point. We have
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\subset{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, and, by item 2 of Lemma
5.11
,

E_{*}\leq\sup\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\leq\sup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\leq\sup\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})

Similarly
E_{*}\geq\inf\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
if
\mathbf{E}_{k}
does not attain its minimum at a critical point.

Now suppose that
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})\setminus\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})
for some
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
. By definition of
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}
and
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}
, this means that
E_{*}
is near a non-critical-point extremum of
\mathbf{E}
, and by item 3 of Lemma
5.11
, it follows that
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}^{\prime})
for some other
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, a contradiction. Thus we have
E_{*}\notin\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
for any
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{s})
.

Now let
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
minimize
\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}))
among all
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, let
\mathbf{I}
be its domain, and let
\mathbf{J}
be its image. By the above comments, this distance is nonzero; thus,
E_{c}:=\arg\min_{E\in\mathbf{J}}|E_{*}-E|
is an extremum of
\mathbf{E}
. But by item 3 of Lemma
5.11
, if this extremum were not a critical point, there would be some
\mathbf{E}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
with
\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}^{\prime}))<\dist(E_{*},\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}))
, which is not the case; thus,
E_{c}
is a critical point of
\mathbf{E}
. Furthermore, since
E_{*}\notin\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})
,
\dist(E_{*},\mathbf{J})>\rho_{k+1}
. It follows that for any
m\in\mathbb{Z}
, either
|\mathbf{E}(\theta_{*}+m\alpha)-E_{*}|>\rho_{k+1}
, or
\theta_{*}+m\alpha\notin\mathbf{I}
.
Thus, all the hypotheses of Lemma
5.17
are met.
∎

As a consequence of this result, we can separate the energies in
G
from generalized eigenvalues of
H
:

Lemma 6.4
.

If
E_{*}\in G
, then there is some
\rho>0
such that for
|E-E_{*}|<\rho
,
E
is not a generalized eigenvalue of
H(\theta)
corresponding to a generalized eigenvector
\psi
growing at most quadratically (
|\psi(j)|<C(j^{2}+1)
).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.3
, there is some scale
k\geq 0
for which we have
(\ell_{k},\gamma_{k})
Green’s function decay on arbitrary long intervals; i.e., for
|E-E_{*}|<\rho_{k}/2
,

\log|R^{\tilde{\Lambda}}_{E,\theta}(m,n)|\leq-\gamma_{k}|m-n|,\quad|m-n|\geq\ell_{k},

for arbitrarily long intervals
\tilde{\Lambda}
. Denote
\tilde{\Lambda}=[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]
.

Assume
E
is generalized eigenvalue of
H(\theta)
corresponding to a generalized eigenvector
\psi
growing at most quadratically (
|\psi(j)|<C(j^{2}+1)
). Fix
j\in\mathbb{Z}
, and choose
\tilde{\Lambda}\ni j
large enough so
\min\{|\tilde{a}-j|,|\tilde{b}-j|\}\geq|\tilde{\Lambda}|/3\geq\ell_{k}
. By the Poisson formula (
1.11
),

\displaystyle|\psi(j)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{k}|\tilde{\Lambda}|/3}\left(|\psi(\tilde{a}-1)|+|\psi(\tilde{b}+1)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2Ce^{-\gamma_{k}|\tilde{\Lambda}|/3}((|j|+|\tilde{\Lambda}|)^{2}+1)

As
|\tilde{\Lambda}|\to\infty
, the right-hand side approaches
0
, so we must have
|\psi(j)|=0
. Since this is true for arbitrary
j
,
\psi
must be identically zero, i.e.,
E
must not be a generalized eigenvalue.
∎

Combining the above results with the sets’ definitions, we arrive at the characterization of
\spec H(\theta)
:

Lemma 6.5
.

\spec H(\theta)=\mathbb{R}\setminus G=\Sigma.

Proof.

By Lemma
6.2
,
\Sigma\subset\spec H(\theta)
; and since generalized eigenvalues are dense in
\spec H(\theta)
by Schnol’s Lemma, Lemma
6.4
implies that
\spec H(\theta)\subset\mathbb{R}\setminus G
. It remains to show that
\mathbb{R}\setminus G\subset\Sigma
. Let
E\in\mathbb{R}\setminus G
. The definition of
G
ensures that
E\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{0})
, and if
E\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
, then
E\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
for some
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
. But this means precisely that
E\in\Sigma
; thus
\mathbb{R}\setminus G\subset\Sigma
.
∎

6.2
Cantor spectrum

Having characterized the spectrum, we now demonstrate that
\Sigma
is a Cantor set, i.e. it is a (closed) nowhere-dense set without isolated points. To do so, we demonstrate that the spectral gaps
G
always meet our modified codomains:

Lemma 6.6
.

For any
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}
,
{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\cap G
is nonempty.

Proof.

We proceed by contradiction: suppose there is some
\mathbf{E}_{k}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
such that
{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\cap G=\varnothing
.

We first note that
\mathbf{E}_{k}
cannot contain a critical point. Suppose it attained its minimum at a critical point; then we would have

\displaystyle\inf\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})

\displaystyle=\inf\mathbf{\Ima}\mathbf{E}_{k}-\rho_{k}

\displaystyle<\inf_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\inf\Ima\mathbf{E}-\rho_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\inf_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\inf\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}).

Then
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\setminus\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\subset{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\cap G
is nonempty, contradicting our initial assumption.
Analogously, if
\mathbf{E}_{k}
attains its maximum at a critical point, we reach the same contradiction. Thus,
\mathbf{E}_{k}
does not contain a critical point.

Let
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k})
such that
E_{n}\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
and
\dist(E_{n},\partial\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k}))\geq\rho_{k-1}/12
; by irrationality of
\alpha
, such an
n
exists. We will show that for some
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
,
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
and
E_{n}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
with
\dist(E_{n}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1}),\partial\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1}))\geq\rho_{k}/12
.
First note that if
E_{n}\in J_{k,n_{0}}^{(2)}
for some
n_{0}\in\mathcal{N}_{k+1}
, then, by construction, there are a pair of double-resonant Rellich functions
\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\ni\mathbf{E}_{k+1,\bullet}:\mathbf{I}^{(2)}_{k+1,n,\bullet}\to\mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{k+1,n,\bullet}\,(\bullet\in\{\vee,\wedge\})
with
\mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{k+1,n,\bullet}\subset\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
; since the uniform local separation between double-resonant Rellich pairs guaranteed by Proposition
3.6
is larger than
2\rho_{k+1}
, there is some
E\in(\sup\mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{k+1,n,\wedge}+\rho_{k+1},\inf\mathbf{J}^{(2)}_{k+1,n,\vee}-\rho_{k+1})
, which must then satisfy

E\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\setminus\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})\cap G,

contradicting our assumption.
Thus
E_{n}
is contained in the images of only simple-resonant functions
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
.
By Lemma
5.11
item 4,
we can find some such function with
B_{\rho_{k}/8}(E_{n})\subset\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
.
Let
E_{\pm}=E_{n}\pm\rho_{k}/24
. Then, since
\partial_{E}T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}\geq 2/D_{0}
by the inverse function theorem,

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{E}_{k+1}}(E_{+})

\displaystyle\geq T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}(E_{+})-\frac{4\delta_{s}}{\nu_{s}}

\displaystyle\geq T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}(E_{n})+\frac{\rho_{k}}{12D_{0}}-\frac{4\delta_{s}}{\nu_{s}}

\displaystyle\geq T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}(E_{n})

where the first line follows from Lemma
4.13
. Similarly,

\displaystyle T_{\mathbf{E}_{k+1}}(E_{-})

\displaystyle\leq T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}(E_{n}).

By continuity of
T_{\mathbf{E}_{k+1}}
, there is some
E_{*}\in[E_{-},E_{+}]
with
T_{\mathbf{E}_{k+1}}(E_{*})=T_{\mathbf{E}_{k}}(E_{n})
; by definition, this means
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
with
E_{n}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})=E_{*}
. By construction,
E_{*}\in\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
with
\dist(E_{*},\partial\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1}))\geq\rho_{k+1}/12
.

But
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})\subset\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
by Lemma
5.11
,
so we can repeat the above procedure to find
\mathbf{E}_{k+2}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k+1})
with
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k+2})
satisfying the above conditions.
As we continue to repeat this procedure, we will eventually reach some scale
s
with
|n|\leq 8M_{s+1}^{(1)}
; since
n\in\mathcal{N}(\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{s})
, we will have
n\in\mathcal{N}_{s}
, and we will construct a double-resonant interval
J_{s,n}^{(2)}
with
E_{n}(\mathbf{E}_{s})\in J_{s,n}^{(2)}
. As we have shown above, this leads to a contradiction with our original assumption.
∎

Proof of Cantor spectrum.

It suffices to show that for each
E\in\spec H(\theta)
, there are points arbitrarily close to
E
which are not in
\spec H(\theta)
. Fix
E\in\spec H(\theta)
. By Lemmas
6.1
and
6.5
, we can find some
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}
with
\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}(\mathbf{E})\supset\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\ni E
and
|{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})|
arbitrarily small. By Lemma
6.6
,
\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E})\ni E^{\prime}
for some
E^{\prime}\in G
; by Lemma
6.5
,
E^{\prime}\notin\spec H(\theta)
.
∎

6.3
Localization

We first construct the full-measure set on which localization occurs. Using the definitions preceding Lemma
4.8
, we define the double-resonant regions

J^{DR}_{n}(\overline{\rho}_{k},12M_{k+1}^{(2)},\alpha,\mathbf{E}):=\overline{B}_{\overline{\rho}_{k}}(E_{n}(\mathbf{E})),\quad n\in\mathcal{N}(12M_{k+1}^{(2)},\alpha,\mathbf{E})

of each function
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
, and we define

J^{DR}=\bigcup_{n\in\mathcal{N}(12M_{k+1}^{(2)},\alpha,\mathbf{E})}J^{DR}_{n}.

We now define and measure the sets

\mathrm{B}_{k}:=\bigcup_{\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}}\bigcup_{|n|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}}\left(\mathbf{E}^{-1}(J^{DR})+n\alpha\right)

on which two resonances at scale
k
occur at nearby shifts, and define

\mathrm{B}:=\bigcap_{n=0}^{\infty}\bigcup_{k=n}^{\infty}\mathrm{B}_{k}.

Lemma 6.7
.

|\mathrm{B}|=0
.

Proof.

By construction, for any
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
,
|\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E})|\leq 10\lceil\bar{\rho}_{k}^{-1}\rceil\ll\rho_{k-1}^{-3}
.
Thus,
for
k\geq 1
,

|\mathcal{E}_{k}|\leq|\mathcal{E}_{0}|\prod_{i=0}^{k-1}\rho_{i-1}^{-3}\leq\rho_{k-2}^{-4}.

(6.1)

Fix
\mathbf{E}\in\mathcal{E}_{k}
. By definition, each
|J_{n}^{DR}|=2\overline{\rho}_{k}
; by Lemma
4.2
,
|\mathbf{E}^{-1}(J_{n}^{DR})|\leq 2\sqrt{\frac{12}{d_{k}}\overline{\rho}_{k}}\ll\rho_{k-1}^{1/2}
.
Then
|\mathbf{E}^{-1}(J^{DR})|=\left|\sum_{n\in\mathcal{N}(12M_{k+1}^{(2)},\alpha,\mathbf{E})}\mathbf{E}^{-1}(J^{DR}_{n})\right|\leq 25M_{k+1}^{(2)}\rho_{k-1}^{1/2}
, and
\left|\bigcup_{|n|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}}\left(\mathbf{E}^{-1}(J^{DR})+n\alpha\right)\right|\leq 625(M_{k+1}^{(2)})^{2}\rho_{k-1}^{1/2}
.

Combining this with the above estimate (
6.1
), we have
|\mathrm{B}_{k}|\leq 625(M_{k+1}^{(2)})^{2}\rho_{k-2}^{-4}\rho_{k-1}^{1/2}
, and
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}|\mathrm{B}_{k}|\leq 625\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}(M_{k+1}^{(2)})^{2}\rho_{k-2}^{-4}\rho_{k-1}^{1/2}<\infty
.
Thus, by the Borel-Cantelli Lemma,
|\mathrm{B}|=0
.
∎

Thus
\Theta:=\mathbb{T}\setminus\mathrm{B}
is a set of full measure. For the remainder of this section, we fix
\theta\in\Theta
.

To show that
H(\theta)
is Anderson localized, it suffices by Schnol’s lemma
[
22
]
to show that every generalized eigenvector
\psi
of
H(\theta)
that grows at most quadratically (
|\psi(j)|<C(j^{2}+1)
) in fact decays exponentially.
For the remainder of this section,
we fix a generalized eigenvalue
E(\theta)
and corresponding generalized eigenvector
\psi
. By Lemma
6.5
, we can also fix a sequence
\{\mathbf{E}_{k}\}_{k=0}^{\infty}
, with
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
for all
k\geq 0
, such that

E\in\bigcap_{k=0}^{\infty}\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathbf{J}}}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
.

To locate the center of localization for
\psi
, we first employ an argument similar to Lemma
6.4
:

Lemma 6.8
.

There is a
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
k\geq K
, there is an
m_{k}\in\mathbb{Z}
satisfying
|m_{k}|\leq 3M_{k}^{(2)}
such that
\theta+m_{k}\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k}
and
|E-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)|<\rho_{k}
.

Proof.

If not, there is an increasing integer sequence
\{k_{i}\}_{i=0}^{\infty}
such that
[-3M_{k_{i}}^{(2)},3M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}]
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.17
at scale
k_{i}
. Thus, for each
i
, there is an interval
\Lambda_{i}:=[a_{i},b_{i}]\supset[-\frac{2}{3}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)},\frac{2}{3}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}]
which satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{k_{i}},\gamma_{k_{i}})
. For any
|n|\leq\frac{1}{3}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}
,
|n-a_{i}|,|n-b_{i}|\geq\frac{1}{3}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}\geq\ell_{k_{i}}
; thus the Poisson formula (
1.11
) gives

\displaystyle|\psi(n)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\gamma_{k_{i}}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}/3}\left(|\psi(a_{i}-1)|+|\psi(b_{i}+1)|\right)

\displaystyle\leq 2Ce^{-\gamma_{0}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}/24}((3M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}+1)^{2}+1)

Fixing
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, we have
|n|\leq\frac{1}{3}M_{k_{i}}^{(2)}
as
i\to\infty
, and the right-hand side of the above inequality approaches
0
. Thus we must have
|\psi(n)|=0
. But
\psi
cannot be identically zero.
∎

The next lemma confirms that
m_{k}
is unique at scale
12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
, and is in fact (eventually) independent of
k
, for
\theta\in\Theta
.

Lemma 6.9
.

There is a
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
m_{\infty}\in\mathbb{Z}
, with
|m_{\infty}|\leq 3M_{K}^{(2)}
, such that for all
k\geq K
,

\theta+m_{\infty}\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k},\text{ and }|E-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{\infty}\alpha)|<\rho_{k},

(6.2)

and
[m_{\infty}-12M_{k+1}^{(2)},m_{\infty}-1]\cup[m_{\infty}+1,m_{\infty}+12M_{k+1}^{(2)}]
is
k
-nonresonant (relative to
(\theta,E)
and
\mathbf{E}_{k}
).

Proof.

By Lemma
6.8
, for sufficiently large
k
, we can choose
|m_{k}|\leq 3M_{k}^{(2)}
satisfying (
6.2
) (with
m_{k}
replacing
m_{\infty}
). If
\theta\in\Theta
, then
\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m\alpha)\notin J^{DR}
for all
|m|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
, and thus
\theta+m_{k}\alpha
is in the simple resonance case of Proposition
4.10
(with
12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
in the role of
L^{(1)}
), for sufficiently large
k
; thus, for
0<|m-m_{k}|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
, if
\theta+m\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k}
, then

|E-\mathbf{E}(\theta+m\alpha)|\geq|\mathbf{E}(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)-\mathbf{E}(\theta+m\alpha)|-|E-\mathbf{E}(\theta+m_{k}\alpha)|>2\rho_{k}-\rho_{k}=\rho_{k};

i.e.,
[m_{k}-12M_{k+1}^{(2)},m_{k}-1]\cup[m_{k}+1,m_{k}+12M_{k+1}^{(2)}]
is
k
-nonresonant.

It remains only to show that
m_{k}
must be independent of
k
. Suppose
m_{k+1}\neq m_{k}
.
Note that
|m_{k+1}|\leq 3M_{k+1}^{(2)}
, so
|m_{k+1}-m_{k}|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
. Since

\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha\in\mathbf{I}_{k+1}\subset\mathbf{I}_{k}

and
[m_{k}-12M_{k+1}^{(2)},m_{k}-1]\cup[m_{k}+1,m_{k}+12M_{k+1}^{(2)}]
is
k
-nonresonant, we have
|E-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)|>\rho_{k}
.
Furthermore, since

\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha\notin J^{DR}(\overline{\rho}_{k},12M_{k+1}^{(2)},\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k})\supset J^{DR}(\overline{\rho}_{k},8M_{k+1}^{(1)},\alpha,\mathbf{E}_{k}),

we must have
\mathbf{E}_{k+1}\in\mathcal{E}^{(1)}(\mathbf{E}_{k})
; thus, by Proposition
2.2
,

|\mathbf{E}_{k+1}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)|\leq 2\delta_{k}\ll\rho_{k}/2

. Thus,

\displaystyle|E-\mathbf{E}_{k+1}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)|

\displaystyle\geq|E-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)|-|\mathbf{E}_{k+1}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)-\mathbf{E}_{k}(\theta+m_{k+1}\alpha)|

\displaystyle>\rho_{k}-\rho_{k}/2\gg\rho_{k+1}.

In particular, this means
\{m_{k+1}\}
is
k+1
-nonresonant, which contradicts the construction of
m_{k+1}
; we must therefore have
m_{k+1}=m_{k}
, and consequently
m_{k}
is in fact independent of
k
, and we may set
m_{\infty}=m_{k}
for all sufficiently large
k
.
∎

Proof of Anderson localization.

As noted above, we need only show that
\psi
decays exponentially.
Let
K
and
m_{\infty}
be the integers defined in Lemma
6.9
.
For
|n-m_{\infty}|>8M_{K+1}^{(2)}
, let
k\geq K+1
be such that
8M_{k}^{(2)}<|n-m_{\infty}|\leq 8M_{k+1}^{(2)}
. Consider the interval

\Lambda=\left[n-\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}\right\rceil,n+\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}\right\rceil\right].

All
m\in\Lambda
satisfy
0<|m-m_{\infty}|\leq 12M_{k+1}^{(2)}
.
Thus, by Lemma
6.9
,
\Lambda
satisfies the hypotheses of Lemma
5.17
at scale
k
;
therefore, there is some subinterval

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}:=[\tilde{a},\tilde{b}]

\displaystyle\supset\left[n-\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}\right\rceil+3M_{k}^{(2)},n+\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}\right\rceil-3M_{k}^{(2)}\right]

\displaystyle\supset\left[n-\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{9}\right\rceil,n+\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{9}\right\rceil\right]

which satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\ell_{k},\gamma_{k})
.
Note that
\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{9}\right\rceil>\frac{8}{9}M_{k}^{(2)}\geq\ell_{k}
; thus, by the Poisson formula (
1.11
),

\displaystyle|\psi(n)|

\displaystyle\leq e^{-|\log\varepsilon||n-m_{\infty}|/9}\left(|\psi(\tilde{a}-1)|+|\psi(\tilde{b}+1)|\right).

We have

\displaystyle\max\{|\tilde{a}-1|,|\tilde{b}+1|\}

\displaystyle\leq|n|+\left\lceil\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}\right\rceil+1

\displaystyle\leq|n-m_{\infty}|+\frac{|n-m_{\infty}|}{2}+2+3M_{K}^{(2)}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{2}|n-m_{\infty}|+M_{k}^{(2)}

\displaystyle<2|n-m_{\infty}|.

Therefore,

\displaystyle|\psi(n)|

\displaystyle\leq 4Ce^{-|\log\varepsilon||n-m_{\infty}|/9}(|n-m_{\infty}|^{2}+1),

which decays exponentially away from
m_{\infty}
.
∎

Appendix A
Bootstrapped Green’s function decay

In this appendix, we will show that, if one can partition an interval
\widehat{\Lambda}
into subintervals alternating between long intervals with Green’s function decay and short intervals with resolvent bounds, one can iterate the resolvent identity to bootstrap the decay to all of
\widehat{\Lambda}
. Results of this variety are well-established in the literature (cf. e.g.
[
13
]
); we include the details relative to our specific application for the reader’s convenience.

Let
v\in C^{2}(\mathbb{T},[-1,1])
with
\|\partial_{\theta}v\|_{\infty}+\|\partial_{\theta}^{2}v\|_{\infty}\leq D_{0}
,
(\theta_{*},E_{*})\in\mathbb{T}\times\mathbb{R}
,
0<\varepsilon<1/7
, and
0<{\widehat{\rho}}<1/2
. Additionally, let
\ell\in\mathbb{N}
,
1<{\gamma}\leq|\log\varepsilon|
, and

M:=\max\{{\ell},|\log{\widehat{\rho}}|\}.

Consider an integer
\widehat{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
with
\widehat{\ell}>16|\log\varepsilon|M
, and define

\displaystyle\widehat{\gamma}

\displaystyle={\gamma}-16|\log\varepsilon|M/\widehat{\ell}.

Assumption 4
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\Lambda}\subset\mathbb{Z}
admits a partition
\mathcal{P}
satisfying:

1.

Every other interval of
\mathcal{P}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
({\ell},{\gamma})
. We denote the intervening intervals by
\Lambda_{i}
and use the notation
\Lambda_{i,l/r}
to refer to the interval (on which the Green’s function decay property for
({\ell},{\gamma})
is satisfied) immediately to the left/right of
\Lambda_{i}
.

Note that, with this convention, one will have, e.g.,
{\Lambda}_{i,r}={\Lambda}_{i+1,l}
.

2.

For each
{\Lambda}_{i}=[a_{i},b_{i}]
,
the interval
\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}:=\Lambda_{i,l}\cup\Lambda_{i}\cup\Lambda_{i,r}
satisfies

\displaystyle\|{P}_{i}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|\leq 2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1},

(A.1)

where
{P}_{i}
denotes the projection onto coordinates
[a_{i}-{\ell},b_{i}+{\ell}]\cap\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}
.
Furthermore,
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}}
is well-defined.

3.

|\Lambda_{i,l/r}|>2\widehat{\ell}
.

4.

|\Lambda_{i}|\leq M
.

We illustrate one segment of Assumption
4
in Figure
9
. Under these assumptions, we get
(\widehat{\ell},\widehat{\gamma})
decay on
\widehat{\Lambda}
:

{\Lambda}_{i,l}
{\Lambda}_{i,r}
{\ell}
\left({\ell},{\gamma}\right)
decay
\left({\ell},{\gamma}\right)
decay
{\Lambda}_{i}
decoupled resolvent bound
{\ell}

Figure 9
:
An illustration of one segment
\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}
of the partition
\mathcal{P}
from Assumption
4
; note that the naming convention for the decay intervals is non-unique, i.e.
\Lambda_{i,r}=\Lambda_{i+1,l}
.

Lemma A.1
.

Suppose
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies Assumption
4
. Then
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\widehat{\ell},\widehat{\gamma})
.

Before proving Lemma
A.1
, we will first handle the special case where there is precisely one interval
\Lambda_{i}
:

Assumption 5
.

Suppose that
\widehat{\Lambda}
is partitioned by

\mathcal{P}
into
{\Lambda}_{l}\cup{\Lambda}\cup{\Lambda}_{r}
, such that

1.

{\Lambda}_{l/r}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
({\ell},{\gamma})
.

2.

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is well-defined, and,
denoting
\Lambda=[a,b]
,

\displaystyle\|{P}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|\leq 2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1},

(A.2)

where
{P}
denotes the projection onto coordinates
[a-{\ell},b+{\ell}]\cap\widehat{\Lambda}
.

3.

|\Lambda|\leq M
.

Letting

\widetilde{\gamma}:={\gamma}-6|\log\varepsilon|M/\widehat{\ell},

in this case we get slightly improved decay on
\widehat{\Lambda}
:

Lemma A.2
.

Suppose
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies Assumption
5
. Then
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\widehat{\ell},\widetilde{\gamma})
.

Proof.

Suppose
m,n\in\widehat{\Lambda}
and
|m-n|\geq\widehat{\ell}\geq 3M\geq 2{\ell}+|\Lambda|
.
Then at most one of
m,n
satisfies
\dist(\cdot,{\Lambda})\leq{\ell}
. In the case neither
m
nor
n
is within
{\ell}
of
{\Lambda}
, we use the resolvent expansion

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}.

Since
m,n
do not belong to adjacent intervals in
\mathcal{P}
,
the middle term of
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)
in this expansion vanishes. By Green’s function decay on
{\Lambda}_{l/r}
and (
A.2
),

\displaystyle\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)|

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(e^{-{\gamma}|m-n|}+2\varepsilon{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{-{\gamma}(|m-n|-|\Lambda|-1)}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\log\left(3{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{-{\gamma}(|m-n|-|\Lambda|-2{\ell}-1)}\right)

\displaystyle=-\left({\gamma}-\frac{\log 3+|\log{\widehat{\rho}}|+{\gamma}|\Lambda|+2{\gamma}{\ell}+{\gamma}}{|m-n|}\right)|m-n|

\displaystyle\leq-\left({\gamma}-\frac{6|\log\varepsilon|M}{\ell}\right)|m-n|.

On the other hand, if
\dist(n,\Lambda)\leq{\ell}
, we have
|m-n|\leq\dist(m,\Lambda)+|\Lambda|+2{\ell}
.
We use the resolvent expansion

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}.

By Green’s function decay on
{\Lambda}_{l/r}
and (
A.2
),

\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)|\leq\log\left(e^{-{\gamma}|m-n|}+2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{-{\gamma}(\dist(m,\Lambda)-1)}\right)\leq\log\left(3{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{-{\gamma}(|m-n|-|\Lambda|-2{\ell}-1)}\right),

and the proof concludes as in the first case.
∎

The following lemma is useful to ensure that the decoupled resolvent bounds (
A.1
) or (
A.2
) are satisfied:

Lemma A.3
.

Suppose
\widehat{\ell}\in\mathbb{N}
is an integer with

\widehat{\ell}\geq\max\left\{{\ell},\frac{|\log{\widehat{\rho}}|+1}{{\gamma}}\right\}.

Suppose that
\widehat{\Lambda}
is partitioned by

\mathcal{P}
into
{\Lambda}_{l}\cup{\Lambda}\cup{\Lambda}_{r}
, such that

1.

{\Lambda}_{l/r}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
({\ell},{\gamma})
.

2.

There exists an interval
\widehat{\Lambda}\supset\widetilde{\Lambda}=[\widetilde{a},\widetilde{b}]\supset{\Lambda}=[{a},{b}]
such that

\displaystyle\min\{|\widetilde{a}-{a}|,|\widetilde{b}-{b}|\}

\displaystyle\geq\ell,

\displaystyle\|R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\|\leq{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}.

Then

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is well-defined,
(
A.2
) is satisfied, and
\widehat{\Lambda}
satisfies Assumption
5
.

Proof.

Denote by
\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}
the partition of
\widehat{\Lambda}
into
\widetilde{\Lambda}
and its complement
\widehat{\Lambda}\setminus\widetilde{\Lambda}
, and denote as shorthand
\widetilde{R}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}
and
\widetilde{\Gamma}=\Gamma^{\Lambda}_{\widetilde{\mathcal{P}}}
.

We expand the resolvent
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
by alternating the resolvent formulas

\displaystyle R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{R}-\widetilde{R}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}},

\displaystyle R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}},

to get the expansion

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=\widetilde{R}-\widetilde{R}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+\widetilde{R}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{R}-\dots.

(A.3)

Note that
{P}\widetilde{R}={P}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
, where
{P}
is defined as in (
A.2
); similarly, because the boundary points of
\mathcal{P}
are in
\widetilde{\Lambda}
,
\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\widetilde{R}=\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
. Thus multiplying on the left
by
{P}
gives

\displaystyle{P}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle={P}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}-{P}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+{P}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}-\dots.

By the Green’s function decay on
{\Lambda}_{l/r}\supset\widetilde{\Lambda}\setminus{\Lambda}
,

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|\leq\|\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{{\Lambda}_{l}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|+\|\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{{\Lambda}_{r}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|\leq\exp(-{\gamma}\ell),

(A.4)

and so we get that

\displaystyle\|{P}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\|

\displaystyle=\left\|{P}\sum_{k\geq 0}(R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}})^{k}(R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}-R^{\widetilde{\Lambda}}\widetilde{\Gamma}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}})\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{k\geq 0}\left(\frac{e^{-{\gamma}\ell}}{{\widehat{\rho}}}\right)^{k}\frac{2\varepsilon+e^{-{\gamma}\ell}}{{\widehat{\rho}}}

\displaystyle\leq 2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}.

Using the same method to estimate the expansion (
A.3
) gives an upper bound on
\|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\|
, which ensures
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is well-defined.
∎

Proof of Lemma
A.1
.

Each interval
\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}=\Lambda_{i,l}\cup\Lambda_{i}\cup\Lambda_{i,r}
satisfies the Green’s function decay property for
(\widehat{\ell},\widetilde{\gamma})
by Lemma
A.2
. Define the partitions
\mathcal{P}_{1}:=\bigcup_{i\text{ odd}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{i\text{ even}}\Lambda_{i}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}:=\bigcup_{i\text{ even}}\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\cup\bigcup_{i\text{ odd}}\Lambda_{i}
.
For ease of notation, we define
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\mathcal{P}_{0}

whenever
i
is an even integer, and
\mathcal{P}_{i}=\mathcal{P}_{1}
whenever
i
is an odd integer.

We first show that
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
is well-defined. Partition
\widehat{\Lambda}=\check{\Lambda}_{0}\cup\check{\Lambda}_{1}
such that
\bigcup_{i\text{ even}}\Lambda_{i}\subset\check{\Lambda}_{0}
and
\bigcup_{i\text{ odd}}\Lambda_{i}\subset\check{\Lambda}_{1}
, and let
Q_{0},Q_{1}
be projections onto the coordinates
\check{\Lambda}_{0},\check{\Lambda}_{1}
respectively. Then, by Assumption
4
, we have upper bound estimates on
\|Q_{0}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\|
and
\|Q_{1}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\|
, and we have
\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\|,\|\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\|\ll 1
. Thus, we can write
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=Q_{0}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}+Q_{1}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
and expand each term using an infinite resolvent expansion alternating between
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and
\mathcal{P}_{1}
, the first term starting with
\mathcal{P}_{0}
and the second term starting with
\mathcal{P}_{1}
. This will give an upper bound on
\|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\|
, ensuring it is well-defined.

Suppose
m,n\in\widehat{\Lambda}
and
|m-n|\geq\widehat{\ell}
.
Because the
\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}
cover
\widehat{\Lambda}
with overlaps
of size at least
2\widehat{\ell}
, there must some
\widehat{\Lambda}_{i}\ni m
such that
m
is separated from its boundary by at
least
\widehat{\ell}
. Without loss of generality, let this be

\widehat{\Lambda}_{0}
.

Suppose first that
\dist(n,\Lambda_{i})>{\ell}
for all
\Lambda_{i}
. We expand the resolvent first from the left using
\mathcal{P}_{0}
, then from the right using the partition
\mathcal{P}
defined in Assumption
4
, and then from the left alternating between
\mathcal{P}_{1}
and
\mathcal{P}_{0}
:

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}+\dots.

(A.5)

We now expand
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)
using (
A.5
). Since
|m-n|\geq\widehat{\ell}
, the first term is bounded by
e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}
by Lemma
A.2
. The second term gives a sum of terms of the form
\varepsilon R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(m,p\pm 1)R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(p,n)
where
p\in\Lambda_{i}
for some
i
; then
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(p,n)=0
because
n
does not belong to any
\Lambda_{i}
. Subsequent terms are of the form

(-1)^{k+1}\varepsilon^{k+1}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(p_{0},q_{0})R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}(p_{1},q_{1})\cdots R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(p_{k+1},n)

(A.6)

for
k\geq 1
, where
p_{0}=m
, and for
0\leq i<k
,
q_{i}
is a boundary point of the interval in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
containing
p_{i}
, and
p_{i+1}=q_{i}\pm 1
;
p_{k+1}
is a boundary point of the interval in
\mathcal{P}
containing
n
, and
q_{k}=p_{k+1}\pm 1
.

The structure of the intervals, our assumption on
m
, and
Lemma
A.2
guarantee that
\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{i}}(p_{i},q_{i})|\leq-\widetilde{\gamma}|p_{i}-q_{i}|

for all
0\leq i<k
. Similarly, Assumption
4
guarantees
\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}}(p_{k+1},n)|\leq-{\gamma}|p_{k+1}-n|
. To estimate
|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})|
, we note that
q_{k}
must be near some
\Lambda_{j}
(i.e., either
q_{k}\in\Lambda_{j}
or
\dist(q_{k},\Lambda_{j})\leq{\ell}
), and we make the following considerations:

•

If
k
shares the same parity as
j
, then in order for
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})
to be nonzero, we must have
p_{k}\in\Lambda_{j}\in\mathcal{P}_{k}
.
Then (
A.2
) applies, and
|p_{k}-q_{k}|\leq 2{\ell}+|\log{\widehat{\rho}}|\leq 3M
, giving
|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})|=|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}_{j}}(p_{k},q_{k})|\leq 2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}\leq 2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|p_{k}-q_{k}|}
.

•

If
k
and
j
have opposite parities, then
|p_{k}-q_{k}|\geq 2\widehat{\ell}-{\ell}\geq 5M\geq\widehat{\ell}
. Thus either
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})=0
, or
Lemma
A.2
applies, giving
\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})|\leq-\widetilde{\gamma}|p_{k}-q_{k}|
.

Finally, since
|m-n|\leq|p_{k+1}-n|+\sum_{i=0}^{k}|p_{i}-q_{i}|+k+1
, and

\varepsilon^{k+1}\leq e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}(k+1)}
, each term of the form
(
A.6
) is bounded by
2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}
.
By the same considerations, but using the fact that
|p_{i}-q_{i}|\geq\widehat{\ell}
for all
0\leq i<k
, each term of the form
(
A.6
) is bounded by
2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-k\widetilde{\gamma}\widehat{\ell}}
. We consider the first term
R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(m,n)
from (
A.5
) to be the
sole term of the expansion with
k=0
, and note that the same bounds apply.

In the case that
n\in\Lambda_{j}
or
\dist(n,\Lambda_{j})\leq{\ell}
, we follow the same procedure but do not use
\mathcal{P}
, i.e., we use the expansion

R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}=R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}+R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}-R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}\Gamma_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}+\dots.

(A.7)

to obtain terms of the form

(-1)^{k}\varepsilon^{k}R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{0}}(p_{0},q_{0})R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{1}}(p_{1},q_{1})\cdots R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}_{\mathcal{P}_{k}}(p_{k},q_{k})

(A.8)

where
p_{0}=m
,
q_{k}=n
, and for
0\leq i<k
,
q_{i}
is a boundary point of the interval in
\mathcal{P}_{i}
containing
p_{i}
, and
p_{i+1}=q_{i}\pm 1
. By considerations analogous to those in the first case, we obtain the same bounds
2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}
and
2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-k\widetilde{\gamma}\widehat{\ell}}
for each term of the form (
A.8
).

Since each
p_{i}
gives two choices for
q_{i}
, there
are at most
2^{k+1}
nonzero terms of the form (
A.6
) or (
A.8
) for
each nonzero value of
k
. Let
k_{0}=\lceil\frac{|m-n|}{\widehat{\ell}}\rceil
. For the at most
2^{k_{0}+1}
terms with
k<k_{0}
, we use the bound
2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}
, while we use the bound
2{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-k\widetilde{\gamma}\widehat{\ell}}
for
k\geq k_{0}
, giving

\displaystyle|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)|

\displaystyle\leq 2\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}\left(2^{k_{0}+1}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}+\sum_{k=k_{0}}^{\infty}2^{k+1}e^{-k\widetilde{\gamma}\widehat{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}\left(2^{k_{0}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}+2^{k_{0}+1}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}k_{0}\widehat{\ell}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq 4\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}2^{k_{0}+2}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}

\displaystyle\leq 2^{|m-n|/\widehat{\ell}+5}\varepsilon^{-1}{\widehat{\rho}}^{-1}e^{3M\widetilde{\gamma}}e^{-\widetilde{\gamma}|m-n|}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\log|R^{\widehat{\Lambda}}(m,n)|

\displaystyle\leq-\left(\widetilde{\gamma}-\frac{\log 2}{\widehat{\ell}}-\frac{5\log 2+|\log\varepsilon|+|\log{\widehat{\rho}}|+3M\widetilde{\gamma}}{|m-n|}\right)|m-n|

\displaystyle\leq-\left(\widetilde{\gamma}-\frac{10|\log\varepsilon|M}{\widehat{\ell}}\right)|m-n|

\displaystyle\leq-\widehat{\gamma}|m-n|.\qed

Appendix B
Proofs of preliminaries

Below we collect proofs of the foundational results from the introduction.

B.1
Perturbation theory lemmas

Proof of Lemma
1.1
.

We proceed by contraposition. Suppose
\rank(\chi)<\rank(P)
. Then
\dim(\ker(\chi))+\dim(\Ima(P))>\dim(V)
by rank-nullity. Consequently,
\ker(\chi)\cap\Ima(P)
contains a unit vector
Pv
. But then

\displaystyle\|(I-\chi)Pv\|

\displaystyle=\|Pv\|=1,

so
\|(I-\chi)P\|\geq 1
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
1.2
.

There must be an eigenvalue in
\overline{B}_{\delta}(E_{*})
, since

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\geq\|(A-E_{*})\phi\|^{2}=\|(A-E_{*})\chi_{\mathbb{R}}(A)\phi\|^{2}\geq\inf_{\lambda_{j}\in\,\spec A}|\lambda_{j}-E_{*}|^{2}.

By assumption we have

\displaystyle\delta^{2}\geq\|(A-E_{*})\phi\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\|(I-\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A))(A-E_{*})\phi\|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\rho^{2}\|(I-\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A))\phi\|^{2}

which is (
1.4
). To see (
1.5
), notice that, since

\displaystyle\left\|(I-\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A))\phi\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=1-\|\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)\phi\|^{2}\leq(\delta/\rho)^{2},

it follows from
\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)^{2}=\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)
that

\displaystyle\|\phi-\psi\|^{2}

\displaystyle=2(1-\|\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)\phi\|)

\displaystyle\leq 2(1-\|\chi_{B_{\rho}(E_{*})}(A)\phi\|^{2})

\displaystyle\leq 2(\delta/\rho)^{2},

which was the claim.
∎

Proof of Lemma
1.3
.

The family of eigenpairs
(\mathbf{E},\psi)(\theta)
in question are the implicit function defined by the vanishing of

\displaystyle F(E,\phi,\theta):=\begin{bmatrix}(A(\theta)-E)\phi\\
\langle\phi,\phi\rangle-1\end{bmatrix}.

Indeed, since
E_{*}
is simple, one has that

\displaystyle(D_{(E,\phi)}F)(E_{*},\psi_{*},\theta_{*})=\begin{bmatrix}A(\theta_{*})-E_{*}&-\psi_{*}\\
2\psi_{*}^{\top}&0\end{bmatrix}

is non-singular: denoting
P=\psi_{*}\psi_{*}^{\top}\oplus 1
and
Q=I-P
, one computes via the Schur complement that

\displaystyle\det((D_{(E,\phi)}F)(E_{*},\psi_{*},\theta_{*}))

\displaystyle=\det\left(\begin{bmatrix}0&-1\\
2&0\end{bmatrix}\right)\det\left(Q(A(\theta_{*})-E_{*})Q\right)

\displaystyle=2\prod_{\lambda_{j}\neq E_{*}}(\lambda_{j}-E_{*})\neq 0.

Thus the implicit function
(\mathbf{E},\psi)(\theta)
is defined in a neighborhood of
\theta_{*}
and twice-differentiable at
\theta_{*}
.

We differentiate the eigenvalue relation
(A-\mathbf{E})\psi=0
at
\theta_{*}
to get that

\displaystyle(A^{\prime}-\partial_{\theta}\mathbf{E})\psi=-(A-\mathbf{E})\partial_{\theta}\psi.

(B.1)

Equation (
1.7
) follows by taking inner products of the relation (
B.1
) with
\psi
; (
1.8
) follows from the uniqueness of the implicit function since (
1.8
) satisfies (
B.1
); and (
1.9
) follows by differentiating (
1.7
). Finally, (
1.10
) is immediate from (
1.8
).
∎

Finally, we have the simple proof of the Poisson formula:

Proof of Lemma
1.4
.

This follows by applying the resolvent
R^{\Lambda}(E)
to the observation

\displaystyle(H^{\Lambda}-E)\psi

\displaystyle=\varepsilon\psi(a-1)\delta_{a}+\varepsilon\psi(b+1)\delta_{b}.\qed

B.2
Cauchy Interlacing Theorem

The Cauchy Interlacing Theorem
3.7
follows from the Min-Max Principle:

Proof of Theorem
3.7
.

If
w_{j}
is an eigenvector for
B
with eigenvalue
\beta_{j}
and
W_{j}^{m}:=\Span\{w_{j},\dots,w_{m}\}
, then one has that
P^{*}W_{1}^{k}
is a
k
-dimensional subspace of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
and

\displaystyle\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\max_{y\in W_{1}^{k}}\frac{\langle By,y\rangle}{\|y\|^{2}}=\max_{y\in W_{1}^{k}}\frac{\langle PAP^{*}y,y\rangle}{\|y\|^{2}}=\max_{y\in W_{1}^{k}}\frac{\langle A(P^{*}y),P^{*}y\rangle}{\|P^{*}y\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{U}\left\{\max_{x}\left\{\frac{\langle Ax,x\rangle}{\|x\|^{2}}\;:\;x\in U\setminus\{0\}\right\}:\dim(U)=k\right\}=\alpha_{k}.

On the other hand, one has that
P^{*}W_{k}^{m}
is an
m-k+1=n-(n-m+k)+1
-dimensional subspace, and one likewise has that

\displaystyle\beta_{k}

\displaystyle=\min_{y\in W_{k}^{m}}\frac{\langle A(P^{*}y),P^{*}y\rangle}{\|P^{*}y\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{U}\left\{\min_{x}\left\{\frac{\langle Ax,x\rangle}{\|x\|^{2}}\;:\;x\in U\setminus\{0\}\right\}:\dim(U)=m-k+1\right\}=\alpha_{n-m+k}.\qed

B.3
Eigenvalue separation lemma

This lemma is classical (cf.
[
14
,
20
]
), but we include a proof for the sake of completeness. Consider the transfer matrix

\displaystyle A(V,E)

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\frac{V_{0}-E}{-\varepsilon}&-1\\
1&0\end{bmatrix}

with associated Schrödinger cocycle

\displaystyle M_{[a,b]}(V,E):=\prod_{k=b}^{a}A(S^{k}V,E)

where
(S^{k}V)_{m}=V_{m+k}
, the product concatenates on the right, and
b\geq a
.

By definition, one has that
\psi=\psi(n)
solves the formal Schrödinger difference equation if and only if

\displaystyle M_{[a,b]}(V,E)\begin{bmatrix}\psi(a)\\
\psi(a-1)\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\psi(b)\\
\psi(b-1)\end{bmatrix}

for all
n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
. We also have the fundamental cocycle identity

\displaystyle M_{[a,b]}(V,E)

\displaystyle=M_{[c+1,b]}(V,E)M_{[a,c]}(V,E),\quad a\leq c<b.

(B.2)

The proof of Lemma
1.5
comes from the simplicity of the eigenvalues of
H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}
and the orthogonality of the corresponding eigenvectors; namely, forcing a sufficient proportion of the masses of two distinct eigenvectors into the same window pushes the corresponding eigenvalues apart. We make this quantitative below:

Proof of Lemma
1.5
.

If
|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|\geq\varepsilon\left(\left(\frac{m_{\Lambda}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)/|\Lambda|^{2}
, the claim follows immediately; suppose for the remainder of the proof that

\displaystyle|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|<\varepsilon\left(\left(\frac{m_{\Lambda}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)/|\Lambda|^{2}.

Denote
[a,b]:=\widehat{\Lambda}
,
[c,d]:={\Lambda}
, and

\vec{\psi}_{k}:=[\widehat{\psi}_{k}(c),\widehat{\psi}_{k}(c-1)]^{\top}
. Since the eigenvectors
\widehat{\psi}_{k}
are real, we may suppose they are normalized at the left endpoint
c
of
{\Lambda}
such that

\displaystyle\|\vec{\psi}_{k}\|^{2}=1,\quad k=1,2,

and

\displaystyle\langle\vec{\psi}_{1},\vec{\psi}_{2}\rangle\geq 0.

In particular, we note for later use that
\|\widehat{\psi}_{k}\|^{2}\geq\|\vec{\psi}_{k}\|^{2}\geq 1
. With this normalization, if
\theta\in(0,\pi/2]
denotes the acute angle between
\vec{\psi}_{1}
and
\vec{\psi}_{2}
, one has

\displaystyle 1-\langle\vec{\psi}_{1},\vec{\psi}_{2}\rangle

\displaystyle=1-\cos\theta\leq 1-\cos^{2}\theta

\displaystyle\leq\sin^{2}\theta\leq\sin\theta

\displaystyle=|\widehat{\psi}_{1}(c)\widehat{\psi}_{2}(c-1)-\widehat{\psi}_{1}(c-1)\widehat{\psi}_{2}(c)|=:|W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})(c)|

and consequently

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\vec{\psi}_{1}-\vec{\psi}_{2}\|^{2}\leq|W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})(c)|.

By Green’s identity, one has

\displaystyle\frac{\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}}{\varepsilon}\sum_{j=c}^{b}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(j)\widehat{\psi}_{2}(j)

\displaystyle={\frac{1}{\varepsilon}}\sum_{j=c}^{b}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(j)(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\widehat{\psi}_{2})(j)-(H^{\widehat{\Lambda}}\widehat{\psi}_{1})(j)\widehat{\psi}_{2}(j)

\displaystyle=W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})(c)-W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})({b}+1).

Since
\widehat{\psi}_{k}
are eigenfunctions for
H^{[a,b]}
, they satisfy the Dirichlet boundary condition at
b
; in particular,
W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})(b+1)=0
, and we see

\displaystyle|W(\widehat{\psi}_{1},\widehat{\psi}_{2})(c)|=\left|\frac{\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}}{\varepsilon}\sum_{j=c}^{b}\widehat{\psi}_{1}(j)\widehat{\psi}_{2}(j)\right|\leq|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|

by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Combining these observations, we see that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|\vec{\psi}_{1}-\vec{\psi}_{2}\|\leq\left(\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|\right)^{1/2}.

(B.3)

We will apply observation (
B.3
) with a telescoping estimate on transfer matrices to get an upper estimate on
\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2})\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}
in terms of
|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|
. Specifically, we have

\displaystyle\|M_{[c,d]}(V,\widehat{E}_{1})-M_{[c,d]}(V,\widehat{E}_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in{\Lambda}}\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}\|M_{[c,j-1]}(V,\widehat{E}_{1})\|\|M_{[j+1,d]}(V,\widehat{E}_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}|{\Lambda}|\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{(|{\Lambda}|-1)/2},

and consequently

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}

\displaystyle-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{{\Lambda}}\leq\sum_{j\in{\Lambda}}|\widehat{\psi}_{1}(j)-\widehat{\psi}_{2}(j)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in{\Lambda}}\|M_{[c,j]}(V,\widehat{E}_{1})\vec{\psi}_{1}-M_{[c,j]}(V,\widehat{E}_{2})\vec{\psi}_{2}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j\in{\Lambda}}\|M_{[c,j]}(V,\widehat{E}_{1})-M_{[c,j]}(V,\widehat{E}_{2})\|+\|M{[c,j]}(V,\widehat{E}_{2})(\vec{\psi}_{1}-\vec{\psi}_{2})\|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}|{\Lambda}|^{2}\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{(|{\Lambda}|-1)/2}+|{\Lambda}|\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{|{\Lambda}|/2}\left(\frac{2|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|\right)^{1/2}.

Since
|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|<\varepsilon\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)/|{\Lambda}|^{2}
by our very first assumption, it follows that

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{{\Lambda}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}\right)^{1/2}|{\Lambda}|\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{|{\Lambda}|/2}\left(1+\sqrt{2}(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|)^{1/2}\right)

and thus

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}|{\Lambda}|^{2}\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{|{\Lambda}|}\left(1+\sqrt{2}(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|)^{1/2}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle<6\frac{|\widehat{E}_{2}-\widehat{E}_{1}|}{\varepsilon}|{\Lambda}|^{2}\left(\left(\frac{m_{{\Lambda}}}{\varepsilon}\right)^{2}+2\right)^{|{\Lambda}|}\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|,

where in the last line we used that
1\leq(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|)^{1/2}
and
(1+\sqrt{2})^{2}<6
.

On the other hand, since
\widehat{\psi}_{1}
and
\widehat{\psi}_{2}
are eigenfunctions for different eigenvalues, they are orthogonal, and we have

\displaystyle\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}

\displaystyle=\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|^{2}-\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{\widehat{\Lambda}\setminus{\Lambda}}^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|^{2}+\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|^{2}\right)-\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|^{2}+\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\left(\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|^{2}+\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\geq\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}\|\|\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|

where the first line above uses orthogonality of vectors with disjoint supports, the second uses orthogonality of distinct eigenvectors and the localization assumption
\|\widehat{\psi}_{k}\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}\geq\|\widehat{\psi}_{k}\|^{2}/2
, and the fourth line uses the inequality of arithmetic and geometric means.

Combining the upper and lower bounds on
\|\widehat{\psi}_{1}-\widehat{\psi}_{2}\|_{{\Lambda}}^{2}
above yields the claim.
∎
